# Code Sandbox

> **v1 status.** Both backends run — `e2b` (a remote VM per run, on the vendor
> cloud or a self-hosted cluster) and `local` (the engine host, as a process
> tree or in a container) — and the engine-fronted OTLP receiver is wired.
> `providers.sandbox` is a **catalogue** of cells, required once a company has
> an agent seat and a `providers.llm` entry: every agent seat's executor runs in
> one of them, placed by the `run_in` of the `providers.llm` entry the launch
> picks, else `providers.sandbox.default_run_in`.

Every agent seat's **executor** is its harness's own agentic run — Claude Code or OpenCode — in a **box**: a remote VM, a container, or a directory on the engine host, each with a shell, a filesystem and a git checkout ([ADR-0066](../../adr/0066-every-executor-is-its-coding-clis-own-run.md)). This page is about where that run happens and what becomes of it. The [turn engine](turn-engine.md) launches it with the turn's ask as its brief and the seat's system prompt as its standing instructions, and the turn **parks**. The run does the seat's work — code and everything else — with its own shell and editor, calls the seat's tools over the [tool bridge](#the-tool-bridge--a-seats-own-tools-from-inside-a-box), and ends by calling `submit_work` there. Its collection, minutes or hours later and possibly on another node, re-enters the **same turn**, where the engine's delivery check and the reviewer judge what it did; a reviewer's `self_iterate` launches it again. The engine's own tool loop runs only the phases around it, on the engine host: the reviewer, delegated workers, the onboarding pass and the side passes (see [Where the harness runs](llm-backends.md#where-the-harness-runs)).

This is how a Crewlet agent answers a message, works a ticket, implements a feature, makes tests pass or reproduces a bug: one run, with a shell and a checkout. **The run is fully permissioned in every cell** — Claude Code under `--permission-mode bypassPermissions`; OpenCode with a `permissions` rule list that allows every action (`{"action": "*", "resource": "*", "effect": "allow"}`) and denies only `question`, since `ask_and_wait` asks the seat's people instead — because a headless run has nobody to answer a permission prompt, and a gate left to ask would stall it or refuse the step. The **cell** decides what that run can reach: `container` and `e2b` keep it off the engine host, and `direct` runs it on the engine host as the engine's own user (see [Where a run happens](#where-a-run-happens)).

The moving parts live in `internal/sandbox/`: the provider, the manager, the harness runners under `executor/`, the waiters, the coordinator, the pending store, `launch.go` (the plumbing between an executor's launch in `internal/engine/agentrun.go` and its detached run), `boxhost.go` (which keeps a `direct` or `container` box with the node that launched it, [ADR-0065](../../adr/0065-a-local-box-is-driven-by-the-node-that-launched-it.md)) and `answerseal.go` (which seals a person's answer onto the run's row).

---

## How a coding task runs

Every turn's executor is a run like this one. Its box is placed by the `run_in` of the `providers.llm` entry the launch picks, else `providers.sandbox.default_run_in`, and reads the seat's `role.sandbox` `setup`, `env` and `mcp` (see [Where a run happens](#where-a-run-happens)). Where the company hosts its own code, the run checks repositories on [Gitlet](gitlet.md) out into the box as it needs them, with a credential that ends with the run (see [Repositories on Gitlet](#repositories-on-gitlet)).

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant T as Turn (runner)
    participant E as Engine
    participant W as Waiter
    participant B as Box
    T->>T: open the Execute phase: the seat's surface and prompt, agent_phase_started
    T->>E: launch the executor, from the turn's pinned epoch
    E->>E: the turn's fence, the budget floor, the chain member and its cell
    E->>E: open the run's row as launching: the seat is held from here
    E->>E: open the bridge session under the job's id
    E->>B: continue the turn's box or provision one, apply setup, start the harness (detached)
    E->>E: publish executor_launched
    T->>E: the turn parks: its state is written, the row becomes running
    Note over E: the seat is busy: its inbox is held, so nothing is fetched
    B->>B: clone, code, test, push, open the PR or MR
    B->>E: the seat's tools over the bridge, ending with submit_work
    E->>W: the submission nudges an immediate poll
    loop every 15 s, the fallback
        W->>B: keepalive and completion check
    end
    W-->>E: the job finished: executor_finished
    E->>B: claim the row, collect the result, pause the box
    E->>E: charge the run's spend, publish its executor_collected record
    E->>T: resume the same turn with the run's calls and its submit_work
    T->>T: the engine's delivery check, then the reviewer
    alt the work is accepted
        T->>E: the turn settles
        E->>B: reclaim the box
    else self_iterate
        T->>E: launch again, continuing in the paused box
    end
```

The details, each load-bearing:

- **Launch → park.** Every launch takes the same steps, in order (`internal/engine/agentrun.go`): the turn's own fence, so a seat this node no longer holds, or one a person stopped, launches nothing; a sandbox runtime on the node, the turn's catalogue and a bridge its box can reach; the [budget floor](#budgets); and the member of the seat's executor chain the run goes to — the first whose harness has a runner and is not sitting out a spent plan ([The executor's run in a box](llm-backends.md#the-executors-run-in-a-box)) — with its cell. Every fact comes from the turn's pinned epoch, so a revision applied mid-turn is the next turn's. The coordinator then opens the run's row as `launching`, the job's bridge session is opened under the job's own id, the box is continued or provisioned and set up, and the harness starts as a background command. The row is a durable record in the [coordination store](coordination.md): the task the turn was working on, both conversation values — the conversation identity a later answer is matched on and the resume reports back to, and the inbox partition the triggering delivery arrived in — the trace context, the `providers.llm` entry the launch picked and its model, the node that launched it, whether the turn owes anybody an answer, and — where a colleague's [A2A ask](event-system.md#ephemeral-a2a-channels-internala2a) woke the turn — that ask's channel, question, chain and trace, which the frame that ends the turn replies to — or, for a run lost under its turn, the run's ending, which tells the asker so. The executor phase returns parked and the turn's segment ends. The seat is **busy from the write that opens the row** — before the box is provisioned, and so before the turn can end and the seat's consumer move on to its next message. The launch also publishes `executor_launched` to the seat's control topic, and processing it recounts the seat from the pending store's own list of the seat's active runs, which is what carries a busy seat across a restart or a handoff; but nothing waits for it. A launch that fails after opening the row (no box, a job that will not start, a parked state the turn cannot write) gives the seat back as it ends the row, and a launch refused before it closes its phase as failed — or as stopped, where a person stopped the seat — so no screen shows a phase that never ends. Nothing is parked in memory; everything the collection needs is in the row.

  **The record lands in two writes, and the state says which one it is at.** The turn's parked state (`internal/agent/execstate`: the round, the closed rounds, the surface and the skills it had loaded, the task, what it was asked and by whom, the items it wrote, the spend no segment has paid yet, the turn's deadline and the context it froze at its start — no conversation, since there is no engine loop to re-enter) is written by the turn as its frame unwinds, *after* the launch returns. So the launch opens the row as `launching` and the unwinding turn writes its state, flipping the row to `running` in the same compare-and-swap. In between, the job is already executing and the row has nothing to resume — so **nothing polls or claims a `launching` run**: a completion claimed in that window would find no turn to resume and would have to fail a run that finished. The window is milliseconds on an idle host and hundreds of milliseconds on a loaded one, and a trivial run is exactly the one that fits inside it.
- **A seat whose run is working takes no other work, and that is every turn.** While one of a seat's runs is running (or the seat owes an answer's resume, or a reply a run let go of), the seat **receives nothing at all**: not new work, not a colleague's message. And **no person's answer to a different run of the same seat is resumed meanwhile**, whether that answer is a chat reply or one given [by naming the run](#answering-a-parked-run). That is the rule, not a cost of the mechanism: an agent in the middle of its work is busy with it, and it takes up the next thing when this one is done, as a person would; resuming a second run beside the first would put two of the seat's turns in flight at once, each acting for the seat without seeing the other. So **each seat runs at most one executor run at a time**. A chat reply waits on the broker with the rest of the mail and is offered when the busy run **settles or parks on its own question**, which for a long job can be hours; an answer given by naming the run travels on the seat's control topic instead, is recorded on its run at once, and its resume waits for the busy run and then runs ahead of the held mail. Nothing is lost while either waits (the reply keeps its place and the question its anchor), and a parked run whose box outlives `pause_ttl_seconds` in the meantime re-seeds from its branch when the answer arrives.
- **Busy while it runs.** While a run HOLDS the seat, the seat's inbox is **held** (the `sandbox` hold): the coordinator takes it as the launch opens the run's row — and when a node that takes the seat over recovers a run still going, before the mailbox opens — and lifts it when the last such run settles or parks on a question. Held, nothing is fetched: the mail waits on the broker in order, nothing is held against a broker ack window during an hours-long run, no new turn starts and nothing runs at all until the run is done. A person's chat reply to *another* of the seat's runs waits with the rest of its mail and is offered when the run settles or parks; an answer by turn to one travels on the seat's control topic, is recorded at once, and its resume waits for the run and then runs ahead of the held mail. A colleague's [A2A ask](event-system.md#ephemeral-a2a-channels-internala2a) waits with the held mail as a chat reply does, and its channel is not closed under it: the idle sweep spares a channel whose seat's inbox is held, as it spares one whose ask a run's record carries. A run parked waiting on a human answer holds nothing — the seat works as usual, and its deliveries are offered to that question before they become turns (see [Mid-run clarification](#mid-run-clarification-ask_and_wait)).

  A delivery that reaches a held seat anyway (it raced the hold, or the queue refused to take it) asks for the hold again and is deferred, keeping its place at the head of the inbox — never requeued behind the seat's newer mail.
- **Completion → resume.** The resume starts a turn, so it asks the node's admission first, as every turn does ([ADR-0063](https://github.com/crewlet/crewlet/blob/main/adr/0063-every-turn-on-a-node-passes-the-nodes-admission.md)): the seat owned fresh, a slot under `node.max_concurrent`, and no drain. It asks **before the claim**, so a node that refuses leaves the run exactly as it was, and the completion is handed back by the condition the refusal names: a draining node defers it, which quiesces the seat's control topic for the rest of the drain and leaves the completion at its head for the seat's next holder, while a condition that clears by waiting (the seat's ownership not yet proven again after a store blip) acknowledges it, spending none of its deliveries, because the waiter that drives the run's box announces a finished run again on every poll until a node claims it. The node also keeps the refused completion and re-fires it **the moment the condition clears**, on the same signal that wakes an answer waiting on that condition, so the resume waits for the condition and not for the poll; a clearing nothing signals is caught by that waiter's next poll, at most **15 seconds** later. Whichever arrives first claims the run, once. Both waiters keep running through a drain, so that waiter's keepalive keeps the box meanwhile: the fleet's for an `e2b` box, and for a `direct` or `container` box its host's own, which announces the run to the seat's next holder, who reads the box back through that host ([two waiters](#durability-completion-and-observability), [ADR-0065](https://github.com/crewlet/crewlet/blob/main/adr/0065-a-local-box-is-driven-by-the-node-that-launched-it.md)). The admission is held until the resume and its settle return. The completion event is claimed **at-most-once** (an atomic status flip on the row) and **only for the job it reports**: every launch gets a new id on the row and the completion carries it, so a duplicate that arrives after its job parked on a question, or after a relaunch started the next job, claims nothing. The result is collected, tokens are post-accounted once per run (see [Budgets](#budgets)), each launch is published once as a record of its own (see [each launch is published as its own record](#what-ends-a-run-each-launch-is-published-as-its-own-record)), and the turn is rebuilt from its parked state: the surface and skill-guard state as the run left them (as the launch recorded them, every tool the run can reach, with every skill it loaded over the bridge replayed onto them, so the collected phase's record lists the tools the run held as `tools_available`, with nothing left to discover beside them), the call log seeded with every call the turn and the run made, each counted by the identity it was admitted under (the digest of its whole canonical arguments) — the run's bridged calls in the order the bridge admitted them — and the run's `submit_work`, replayed from the bridged calls in its log, taken as the round's work. A run that never submitted is rescued as `incomplete`, never read off the prose it ended with. The engine's delivery check and the reviewer then judge it in the same turn (`Runner.Resume`). The same `turn_id` continues, so the whole sequence renders as one turn on the dashboard, and so does the round count: the collection is the round the run was launched in, so a launch and its collection share one `turn_engine.max_iterations`. A failed resume dispatch hands the claim back, to the status it was taken from, so the redelivered completion can retry, and the parked turn is never lost. That includes a resume a drain interrupts: the hand-back does not inherit the drain's cancellation, so the seat's next owner resumes the turn rather than reaping it as abandoned. The one exception is a resumed turn that broke after it had already written outside the engine (pushed a branch, commented on an issue): a retry would repeat those writes, so the run is settled instead, its box reclaimed and its record deleted, and the seat is freed. Otherwise it hands back only the claim it took: when the resumed turn has already relaunched its executor and the relaunch started another job, or the seat's next owner has reaped the run as abandoned, the row is left as it stands, because the relaunch replaced the job a retry would collect and the reap has already ended the run. **The seat stays held through the resumed turn**: a run claimed for its resume is `resumed`, which holds the seat, and it is counted so until the resumed turn has returned and the run's record is gone, when the seat is recounted from the store. The record goes once the turn's end is on the executor log, which the run's ending records off the frame that ran the resume, by its own retry, once every capture of the turn has settled ([How a turn ends on the executor log](turn-engine.md#how-a-turn-ends-on-the-executor-log)): so the seat's next mail waits for the turn's captures to settle, at most a capture's own budget (about twenty-one minutes at the bounds, a session upload at the object store's slowest pace) — its taker's lease TTL and one read of the executor log for a capture whose taker stopped ([Each launch's session, kept](#each-launchs-session-kept)) — and for that write too. The resume's frame and that retry each recount the seat, and can do so at once; whichever is written second reads the store again first, so the seat is never counted held for a record the retry has deleted. The run used to be uncounted just before the resumer ran, which lifted the seat's inbox hold for the whole resumed turn and let the seat's next mail start a second turn, and a second job, beside it. And a seat runs one turn at a time on its node whichever road the turn comes by (`node.ErrSeatBusy`): a resume that finds the seat mid-turn waits for it, holding nothing, and one that is admitted holds the seat's inbox until it returns, so the mailbox fetches nothing meanwhile and spends none of its mail's deliveries.
- **What the resumed half writes under.** The resumed turn's tracker, knowledge-base and chat writes derive their operation ids from the same unit of work as the first half's, minted at the instant that work began, so a first-half write the second half repeats — the retry an `unknown` asks for — lands once. The exception is a resume that runs **more than twenty-nine days after its work began**: the [operation ledger](../guides/replication.md#what-a-retry-is-judged-by-the-instant-its-operation-was-minted) keeps each row thirty days, and an operation minted before what a node's ledger swept, whose row is gone, is answered `unknown` and never published — so minted at that start, every write the resumed half made would be lost on every data node. Such a half is **rebased**: it mints its ids at the attempt instead and records that instant in the fleet's coordination store under the unit of work, logged as `turn_rebased`; the price is that a first-half write it repeats is written a second time — except a work-item comment, a created item and a created view, whose ids are the keys of their rows and are derived as the work began, so the first half's is found and answered from its row rather than made again. **Every attempt is judged against its own clock.** A resume that fails is retried — by the completion coming round again, or by the person's next answer, which can be weeks later — and each retry is judged afresh, never held to a decision an attempt took while the start still looked recent. A later attempt **inherits** a recorded instant while it is within the same twenty-nine days of it, so a failed resume retried on this node, on the seat's next owner or after a restart derives the ids the attempt before it derived, and so does the resume of the turn's next launch — a reviewer's send-back, or a run carried on with a person's answer — whatever the earlier attempt or the earlier half wrote is not written twice. The line is drawn a day short of the thirty so that no sweep passes the instant while one attempt is still working. A turn with no unit of work seeds its ids from its run and mints them at the run's start, and is rebased by the same rule, under its run; a scheduled fire is not one, since its fire is a `task_assigned` the completion ledger records, and its unit of work is that fire, or every fire a merged run stands for. The same rule covers a trigger dispatched long after it arrived — see [Turn Engine](turn-engine.md#a-turns-two-identities).
- **Reuse across launches.** On collect the box is **paused** (snapshotted), not destroyed. A relaunch in the same turn — a reviewer's `self_iterate`, or a person's answer carrying a parked run on — **continues in it**, checkout and disk state kept and provisioning not applied again, when the job that used it is over, the member the launch picks runs the same harness in the same cell, the box is one this node can reach: any `e2b` box, or a local one on this node's own host, and the box is **younger than seven days**, counted from when it was first attached to the run (`PriorBox.ContinuesIn`). The age is the box's, not a job's: every send-back and every answer launches a job with a credential of its own, so without it one checkout could work for as long as the turn lasts, and Gitlet keeps a deleted seat branch's engine commits for fourteen days after the deletion, after which a box pushing one back is refused `forged_committer`. Seven days — the fourteen less the seven a job's credential is valid for — keeps every push from a box inside that window; an older box is reclaimed (`sandbox_prior_reclaimed`, its `box_since` the date) and the job starts in a fresh box from the pushed branch. Its harness's session is not the box's to keep: every relaunch of the turn — a send-back as much as a run carried on — continues the turn's session where it can, from the copy this box holds or from the turn's kept capture restored into whichever box the launch gets ([A relaunch continues the turn's session](#a-relaunch-continues-the-turns-session)). Otherwise the box is **reclaimed through its own cell** before the fresh one exists (`sandbox_prior_reclaimed`), so a fallback onto a member in another cell moves the run without leaving its last box behind; a box that cannot be reattached is reclaimed and replaced (`sandbox_reuse_failed`), the work re-seeding from the pushed branch; and a local box on another node's host — the seat moved since the last launch — is left to that host, whose waiter reclaims it (`sandbox_prior_left_to_host`, see [A local box is the node's that launched it](#durability-completion-and-observability)). Whichever ending the turn reaches reclaims the box its row names. The two jobs are told apart by the launch id, not the row's status: a new job that finishes, or parks on a question of its own, before the resumed turn has returned keeps its box and its tail.
- **Publishing a build from the box.** `create_artifact` and `publish_artifact` name a box as a source — `source: {sandbox: "dist"}` for a directory as an artifact's whole file set, `from_sandbox` for one file, each relative to the workspace — and the engine serves one only to **the run working in it**: the run's own call, made over its bridge while its record reads `running`, so the run publishes its build when it says the build is ready. A [worker](turn-engine.md#workers) the run delegates to never makes that call: both tools write to a surface people read, and a worker is granted no such tool. It attaches that box as a reader — never resumed, and a remote box's kill timer left where the waiter keeps it — lists the directory through the box itself with links never followed, holds every listed path to the workspace and the published-path grammar, and streams each file through the [artifact upload pipeline](../guides/tools-and-mcp.md#artifacts), which reserved its bytes first and reads them back verified before the version is decided. The box is never handed an upload URL or a credential. A seat's call that finds no such run — none launched yet, parked on a question (its job paused, which a read would wake), collected, another seat's, or torn down — is refused `not_running`, and nothing is published; so is one served on a node other than the one that launched a `direct` or `container` box, which only that node reaches ([ADR-0065](../../adr/0065-a-local-box-is-driven-by-the-node-that-launched-it.md)). A caller that is not an agent seat — a person, or their own assistant, calling through the operator catalogue — has no such run and is refused `forbidden`; a seat's call on a node that runs no sandbox is refused `unavailable`. A collected run's resumed segment calls no artifact tool — its reviewer holds one submission tool — so a running record is the only one a box source is read for. A `from_sandbox` path that names no file (one that's missing, a directory or a link), and a `sandbox` source that names no directory (one that's missing, a file or a link), are refused `invalid`, for the seat to name another. A workspace that's gone from the box is refused `not_running`, for the seat to build again, and a directory the box couldn't walk whole (a directory under it the box's user can't open, a read error) is refused `conflict` rather than published as the part it could read: the listing reads `find`'s own exit, and passes the directory to it as `./dir`, so a name that reads like one of its options is still a directory. A box its provider can't read right now is told by the [same classes](#when-a-run-does-not-start) a launch is, with a wait offered only where one helps, and so is a listing or a file check the box itself ended: one it timed out or killed, or whose container runtime failed on its own side, is the box not answering, and one whose box can't run its shell, `find` or `head` is its image, a `configuration`.
- **A settled run has no record.** When a run ends, whether its turn finished, a person stopped it, or it failed, its box is reclaimed first and then its record is **deleted** from the coordination store, once the ending's last step is done: where the engine records every turn's end, once that end is on the executor log. Until then the executors board shows the run's decided `ending` beside the status it was ended in, its box no longer its own, and nobody is asked to answer it. The record's bucket has no age (a parked run can wait days, and an age would reap the only thing that knows a billed box exists), and the completion poll and every seat recovery read all of it, so a finished run kept there would be read on every one of those passes for the life of the deployment. What a run ended as is recorded elsewhere: the resumed turn's own phase events, an `executor_failed` event naming why it was lost, or an `executor_stopped` event naming who stopped it. The box goes first on purpose: a record that outlives its box is reaped by the seat's next recovery, while a box that outlived its record would be named by nothing.
- **An ending is decided before it is done, and announced exactly once.** Ending a run is several effects in several systems — a person's reply handed back to the seat's inbox, the box reclaimed, the loss announced, the record deleted — and a node can stop between any two. So the ending is first **decided on the run's record** (`ending`), in one compare-and-set under the ending's terms: what it announces, under an identity of its own, and whether a person's reply goes back. From then on the record takes **no other write** — no claim, no take of an answer, no hand-back of a claim, no relaunch, no new answer, no park — so nothing can move the run off the ending, and whoever reads the record next (this node's retry, or the seat's next holder) finishes **that** ending from wherever it stopped, never one of its own. The loss is announced (`executor_failed`) **before** the record is deleted, under an id derived from the run and its ending and the instant it was decided, so a repeat after a crash is the same event to the event store — whose key is that (time, id) pair — and to the fleet's reads, which merge rows on it. The announcement used to be made only after the delete: a delete that landed and reported a failure, or a node that stopped between the two, left the loss announced by nobody, and the seat's next holder, finding the claim the delete had not taken, announced it as `abandoned_tail` whatever it had been ended for. A broker that refuses the announcement keeps the ending, and the seat's mail waits behind it until it is made. **The box goes after the decision, never before it.** An ending is made by a node that read the run and decided it is over, and that read can be stale: a node that lost the seat while settling a claim it had taken used to kill the box first, off its own read, and only then had its ending refused under the seat's next holder's lease — after that holder had given the claim back to its answer, so the run it then resumed had lost its checkout while its record still named the box. The reclaim now rides the decided ending, so the store checks the ending's lease against the run as it stands before anything is killed, and an ending refused there leaves the box to the run's holder; whoever finishes the ending — this node or the seat's next holder — reclaims the box the record names before deleting it. A node that loses the run that way says nothing about it either: what became of the run, and of a reply on it, is announced by whoever holds it now.
- **A person's stop is an ending too, and never a loss.** A [pause with `stop_running`](agent-runtime.md#pausing-a-seat) ends the seat's runs that are launching or running, at once, on the node that holds the seat — when the pause lands, when the node next reads every pause, or when it acquires the stopped seat. The stop is decided on the run's record like any ending, with **who stopped it** recorded in the same write, and only while the run is still that job in one of those two statuses: a run whose completion was claimed first is a resumed turn, which the turn's own fence stops at its next round, and whose call that was out as the stop landed, if it comes back refused for a limit or the seat's budget, is settled as stopped there and then rather than waiting for the limit's instant or the window's turn, and parks nothing; so is a run back in `running` whose job a collection already read — its resume held by the seat's budget, held by its node for a model's limit, or handed back for a retry — which the store refuses a stop of, because its job is over and its spend is the resume's to charge, once, and whose wait the stop ends instead (its hold lifted, a completion its node kept re-fired, and a stopped seat's completion admitted past its budget and its models' limit, which its stopped turn never reaches), so that turn is closed now rather than when its condition would have cleared; and a run parked on a person's question does no work and stays parked behind the pause. Finishing it closes the run's tool-bridge session first — nothing it does after that acts as the seat — then **ends the job**, off the frame that decided the stop and whatever `conversation_session.enabled` says, since ending a job is waiting for it: the job's script, asked, stops its harness and packs the session as its own end does ([Claude Code](#a-claude-code-runs-session), [OpenCode](#an-opencode-runs-session)), and a job not over within 2¼ minutes, or one whose script answers no request (one an older build started), is killed. Only then does it read what the run had spent off its box, charge it to the token counters and publish it as the launch's own `executor_collected` record (noted as stopped, not failed, and saying how the job was ended), all recorded on the row so a repeat after a crash neither charges nor publishes twice; then the box is reclaimed and the stop is announced as `executor_stopped`, under an id derived from the run and its ending exactly as a loss is. The turn that was parked on the run is closed as stopped — `agent_turn_stopped` and a completion carrying `stopped` — under identities derived from the same ending, so the seat's next holder finishing a stop this node could not finish closes the turn once. A run stopped while its turn was still launching it is closed by that turn as it unwinds: its park is refused, and it ends as stopped — from the stop's own decision, which the node keeps for that turn, so a seat resumed before the turn unwinds does not turn the stop into a park with nowhere to go. The completion poll leaves a run whose ending is decided alone, so it never keeps alive a box the ending is reclaiming.
- **Review sees the whole turn.** Every call the run made over the bridge is recorded in the log kept beside its row, in the order it was admitted, and joins the turn's record after the closed rounds' calls when the turn resumes, so the delivery check and the reviewer's evidence see what the run did as the seat. The harness's own editing, commands, tests and commits happen inside the box and are in **no** log, and every review is told so: the reviewer judges that work on the executor's report and on the branches and pull requests listed under `## What the agent delivered`, and a missing shell or test call is never fabrication. Nor is a person's reply in any log: one refused as too large for the run to take is shown to the reviewer under `## Replies the agent did not get`, so a question the run asked again after it reads as the follow-up it is ([below](#mid-run-clarification-ask_and_wait)).

There is deliberately no synchronous "block the turn until the run finishes" shape: a turn that held its concurrency slot and its broker message for a real run's length would trip the inbox ack window and the shutdown drain. A short run in the detached model simply completes fast.

---

## Configuring it

One block is required and one is optional. **`providers.sandbox`** is the catalogue every agent seat's executor runs in, and a company needs it as soon as it has an agent seat and a `providers.llm` entry: without it the company is refused at `providers.sandbox`, once, naming the first such seat and how many more, and suggesting `local: {}` with `default_run_in: direct` (the engine host) or `e2b:` (a remote box). A company with no agent seat, or an org chart written before its credentials (no `providers.llm` entry yet), needs none — it holds every seat's work until a revision adds a model, and the catalogue with it. A seat's own **`role.sandbox`** is optional, and only adds what that seat's box reads.

### Engine provider — `providers.sandbox`

A sibling of `providers.llm`, live-reloadable, with secrets kept as `${VAR}` references and resolved at construction time:

```yaml
providers:
  sandbox:
    # THE CATALOGUE: which boxes exist. Configure either, or both.
    e2b:
      api_key: "${E2B_API_KEY}"   # required whenever e2b: is present — the API
                                  #   authenticates every call
      domain: ""                  # set → self-hosted / local cluster; empty → the cloud
      template: ""                # empty → the prebuilt template for the harness;
                                  #   ALSO how a remote box is sized (below)
    local:
      state_dir: ""               # empty → $CREWLET_SANDBOX_LOCAL_HOME,
                                  #   else ~/.crewlet/sandboxes
      image: ""                   # required only where a run is placed in a container
      runtime: ""                 # auto | docker | podman; empty → auto
      network: ""                 # the container network; "none" cuts the box off
      run_args: []                # how a container box is SIZED
      runtime_env: {}             # extra environment for the docker/podman CLI
                                  #   itself, never the box (below)

    default_run_in: e2b           # direct | container | e2b — where a run on a
                                  #   providers.llm entry naming no run_in happens
    default_timeout_seconds: 900  # box TTL / keepalive window — NOT a run cap (below)
    default_pause_ttl_seconds: 1800     # omit for this default; 0 = never pause. how long a blocked run's paused box is
                                        #   held before the reaper takes it
    default_max_turns: 0          # agentic-round cap for one executor run; unset or 0 = uncapped
    commit_attribution: true      # credit Crewlet as co-author on a run's own commits;
                                  #   unset = true, false = credit nothing (below)
    setup: []                     # engine-wide provisioning steps (see Setup steps)
```

- **The block is a catalogue, not a mode.** It says which boxes *exist*; the `run_in` of each `providers.llm` entry, else `default_run_in`, says where a run launched on that entry goes. This is the shape's whole reason: a company has exactly one `providers.sandbox`, so a company-wide `type:` would choose the engine host for the run that needs the operator's own subscription login *and* for the run whose work must never touch that host. Those are different decisions about different work, and an operator who wants both writes two entries and points each seat at the one that is right for it. **The block may be left out** only by a company with no agent seat, or with no `providers.llm` entry yet: an absent block says "no boxes", and a company whose agent seats have a model and no box to run in is refused.
- **An empty block is refused.** `providers.sandbox: {}` configures nothing: every agent seat's executor runs in one of its cells, and it has none to run in. Give it `e2b:`, `local:`, or `fake: true`.
- **`default_run_in` is required unless the catalogue names exactly one cell — or nothing would read it.** `local:` alone is **two** cells (it serves both `direct` and `container`), and there is deliberately no implicit answer: `direct` runs the harness as the engine's own user, and `e2b` mints billable VMs, so neither may be chosen for an operator who did not say which they meant. Only an `e2b:`-only catalogue (and `fake: true`) resolve on their own. What makes the default *required* is something that would fall to it: an entry some agent seat's executor can launch on — a `claude-code` or `opencode` member of its executor chain — that names no `run_in`. A company whose every such entry names its own cell needs no default at all — that is the shape the catalogue exists for — and validation refuses the silent entry **where it is written**, offering `default_run_in` as the other remedy.

The one thing an ambiguous catalogue may not do is reach **nothing at all**: a default, an entry's `run_in` or a cell a [check](#check-boxes) runs in (`code.checks`) has to name some cell. A backend is built only for a cell something reaches, so a catalogue nothing names builds none — a block that reads as configuration and configures nothing, which is the reason an empty block is refused too. Every catalogue that resolves a default reaches it, precisely so a seat added later has somewhere to go.
- **`api_key`, `domain` and `template` live under `e2b:`**, where they can only mean what they say — a `domain:` at the top of the block used to read as a cluster address beside `type: local` and configure nothing. The key is **required** whenever `e2b:` is present, including against a self-hosted cluster: `domain` changes *which* API is talked to, never whether it authenticates.
- **`local.image`, `runtime`, `network`, `run_args` and `runtime_env` are container-only**, and are checked against the cells your executors and [checks](#check-boxes) actually reach: an image is **required** when some run or check is placed in a container and **refused** when none is, because there it looks like configuration and configures nothing. Required unconditionally it would refuse a perfectly good direct-only company; unchecked, the seat's first run fails at container create, minutes into a turn.

- **`default_timeout_seconds` is not a run-time limit, nor a provisioning one.** It is the box's TTL / keepalive window: the completion poll refreshes a running box's TTL to this value on every tick, so the clock never kills a live job — and a launch refreshes the box it is still [provisioning](#setup-steps--provisioning-the-box) the same way, every 15 s from the box's creation until its setup steps are done, so however long those take together, the clock never takes the box from under one. It is effectively the *orphan-reclaim grace* — how long a box outlives an engine that stopped heart-beating (a crash) before E2B reaps it — and the time after which a node that launched local boxes and stayed away is [taken for gone](#durability-completion-and-observability). A run is never stopped on its box's TTL. The one clock that ends a run is its **turn's** wall-clock cap, where the trigger carries one (a scheduled fire's `timeout_seconds`): it is written on the run's row as its deadline, the completion poll ends a run still working past it (`sandbox_run_past_deadline`), and the collection ends its job as a person's stop does — its harness told to stop and its session packed as its own end packs it — and reads what the run did up to then, marked as cut off.
- **`default_pause_ttl_seconds`** bounds how long a run blocked on a human answer keeps its *paused* box. E2B holds a paused sandbox indefinitely — there is no provider-side TTL — and bills for the snapshot, so expiring it is the engine's job: past this age the completion poll [reaps it](#mid-run-clarification-ask_and_wait), and when the answer arrives the run is carried on in a fresh box from the pushed branch. What a paused box keeps is the run's **working tree**, uncommitted work included, and its harness's own copy of the conversation. Where the turn kept the asking run's session ([Each launch's session, kept](#each-launchs-session-kept)), the relaunch continues that in whatever box it gets, so the conversation outlasts the reap; where it kept none — `turn_engine.conversation_session.enabled: false`, or the capture refused, the seat's session budget full say — the paused box is the conversation's one copy, and an answer that comes after the reap carries the run on in a session of its own, told the question and the answer ([A relaunch continues the turn's session](#a-relaunch-continues-the-turns-session)). 30 minutes trades a bounded snapshot bill against the working tree surviving for the replies that come back quickly; raise it if your teams answer in hours. `0` means never pause — the box is torn down the moment the run blocks, for zero snapshot cost — and *omitting* the key is how you take the 1800s default. The two are different settings and the field distinguishes them, which it did not before: read as a plain number, an explicit `0` was indistinguishable from an absent field and was quietly mapped onto the default, so a company that asked for zero snapshot cost was billed for every paused box for half an hour. Negative values are rejected here: an unbounded pause is the leak the knob exists to prevent. A *role* inherits this default by leaving `role.sandbox.pause_ttl_seconds` out entirely, and a negative role value is rejected for the same reason. Correctness never depends on the box: the durable state is the pushed branch plus the persisted row.
- **`default_max_turns` is off unless you set it.** An executor run is its harness's own loop, and how many rounds a task takes is the harness's to judge, as it is when a person runs it — so the engine leaves every run uncapped unless the company or the seat asks for a bound. A positive value caps the run's agentic *rounds*, a measure of work done rather than time spent, on both runners: Claude Code's `--max-turns`, and OpenCode's agent `steps`. **Unset or `0`, a run is uncapped**; a negative value is refused. An uncapped run is still never stopped on its box's clock (see `default_timeout_seconds` above — the completion poll refreshes a running box's TTL on every tick precisely so that it cannot be): it ends when it finishes, at a scheduled turn's own cap, or when a person [stops it](agent-runtime.md#pausing-a-seat), and what it spent is charged against the seat's [budget](#budgets) when it is collected. Set a cap where you want a wall that a run going in circles hits within one turn — a weaker model running unattended on your own plan window is the usual case. There is no companion budget cap — the fleet's own [token meter](agent-learning.md) already post-charges a collected run against the seat's budget, and a second ceiling denominated in the operator's dollars would just disagree with it. A role overrides this with `role.sandbox.max_turns`, where `0` lifts a company's cap for that one seat.
- **`commit_attribution` credits Crewlet on the commits a run makes as itself**, unless set to `false`: `Co-authored-by: Crewlet <noreply@crewlet.ai>`, and `Crewlet-Seat`, `Crewlet-Run: <turn id>` and `Crewlet-Work-Item` naming the seat, the run and the work item, added by git rather than by the model. A person's commit the run amends, cherry-picks or rebases is never credited. See [Commit attribution](#commit-attribution).
- **Sizing is `template`, not a limits knob.** There is deliberately no `default_limits`. A box's vCPU and RAM are properties of its **template**, fixed when the template is built; the sandbox-*create* API accepts no resource arguments at all, and disk is not exposed in either place. An engine-side limits field could only ever be parsed and dropped — an operator setting `memory_mb: 16384` would silently get whatever the template had. To give agents bigger boxes, build a template with the resources you want and name it in `template:`; a `container` box is sized with `local.image` and `local.run_args` instead. There is no per-run or per-seat template field: sizing is a property of the backend, configured once, and the `Spec` field that used to sit in front of `e2b.template` was set by nobody while looking exactly like a wired knob.
- **Hot-reload, in every direction, with no restart.** The runtime under the catalogue — the coordinator that holds which seats are mid-run, and the completion poll — is a fact about the *process*, so it is brought up **once**, by the first company that reaches a sandbox cell: at boot, or at whichever apply brings one (the apply's `sandbox_runtime` stage — see [Configuration](configuration.md#the-engine-half)). That covers a node started with no company, the quickstart's own route, and a company that adds `providers.sandbox` later. The same apply prepares the seats the node **already** holds, which were taken before there was anything to prepare: each attaches its completion topic and recovers the runs recorded on it, exactly as a seat taken afterwards does on its way in, and a seat whose preparation fails is handed back so its next acquisition runs the whole of it. From then on a revision only **swaps the manager** under the runtime (`Coordinator.SetManager`), and three rules keep the runs already in flight whole across the swap:
  - **The poll follows the swap.** It asks the coordinator for its current manager on every tick, so a job launched after a reload is polled through the backend that made it, rather than through the catalogue the node first ran.
  - **A dropped cell stays reachable for what is already on it.** A run's placement is on its row, so a revision that removes a cell (`local:` taken out, say, while a `container` job is still running) carries that cell's backend as *retired*: a running job is still polled, collected and reclaimed, and a parked one still reaped, through the backend that made it — while no new box is ever provisioned there. An entry still naming the removed cell, or falling to it as the default, is refused at validation.
  - **Removing the block keeps the last manager.** A company validates with no `providers.sandbox` only when it has no agent seat or no `providers.llm` entry, and every launch reads the catalogue of its turn's pinned epoch, so such a company launches nothing; the runtime and its last manager stay for the runs still in flight, which still have to be finished through it.

  A catalogue that cannot be **built** is refused before anything on the node changes — on a node that runs no sandbox yet as on one that does — so a company whose agent seats' executors would launch into a box nobody can mint is never published.

### A seat's own box settings — `role.sandbox`

Every agent seat's executor is its harness's own run in a box, so this block gates nothing and places nothing: the box is placed by the `run_in` of the `providers.llm` entry the launch picks, else `providers.sandbox.default_run_in`, and the runner is that entry's own harness. What the block holds is what the seat's box reads beyond the engine-wide settings — `setup`, `env`, `optional_keys`, `mcp`, `pause_ttl_seconds` and `max_turns` — and absent, the seat's box gets the engine-wide settings alone. Any other key is refused as unknown.

```yaml
roles:
  - name: Senior Engineer
    handle: senior-engineer
    mcp_env:
      github: { Authorization: "Bearer ${GITHUB_APP_TOKEN_SENIOR_ENGINEER}" }
    sandbox:
      # pause_ttl_seconds:              # leave out → provider default; 0 → never hold a paused box
      # max_turns:                      # leave out → provider default (uncapped unless set); 0 → uncapped
      env:                              # env injected into this seat's runs, ${VAR}-expanded
        NPM_TOKEN: "${NPM_TOKEN_SENIOR}"
        DATABASE_URL: "postgres://${TEST_DB_HOST}/app"
      optional_keys: [DATABASE_URL]     # expected to resolve to nothing at times: left out quietly,
                                        #   where any other unresolved key is left out and logged
                                        #   (sandbox_env_unresolved) as a gap in the run's environment
      mcp:
        servers: [files]                # the role's MCP servers that run in the box; the
                                        #   tool bridge serves every other one it holds
      setup:                            # per-role provisioning steps, applied after the
        - name: node                    #   engine-wide providers.sandbox.setup
          # No `corepack enable`: it writes pnpm's shim beside the Node binary,
          # and a setup command runs as the box's own account — `user` on E2B,
          # the engine's on the host for `direct` — which cannot write there
          # when Node was installed as root. `corepack pnpm …` needs no shim.
          commands: ["command -v corepack >/dev/null || { echo 'no corepack on PATH' >&2; exit 1; }"]
          brief: "Node and corepack are installed; run the repo's pinned package manager through corepack (`corepack pnpm install`), never a bare `pnpm`."
```

`role.sandbox.env` is where **external-service tokens are declared**: a registry token, a test database. The agent's own credential at an integration it is on at is not declared here: Crewlet puts it in the box itself, such as the agent's GitHub App token as `GITHUB_TOKEN` and its Expo robot's as `EXPO_TOKEN`, so PRs and MRs land under the agent's own identity (see [An agent's own integration credentials](#an-agents-own-integration-credentials)).

---

## Where a run happens

A `providers.llm` entry's `run_in` (else `providers.sandbox.default_run_in`) names one **cell** of the catalogue, and every executor run launched on that entry happens there. The three cells and the two backends behind them:

| `run_in` | Backend | Runs as | Isolates state | Isolates the **host** | Needs |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `direct` | `local:` | the engine user, on the host | yes | **no** | the harness installed on that host |
| `container` | `local:` | a PID in a Docker/Podman container | yes | yes | `local.image` with the harness ([bring your own](#bring-your-own-image)) |
| `e2b` | `e2b:` | a fresh remote VM per run | yes | yes | `e2b.api_key` |

Every **executor run** is placed by the `run_in` of the entry the launch picks rather than by the seat, because where that runtime runs is a property of the runtime: the harness's subscription login is on the engine host, so a local cell reaches it directly while a remote one needs the headless token instead. An entry that names none takes `providers.sandbox.default_run_in`, resolved at **launch** rather than at config time, so changing the catalogue reaches every entry that wrote nothing without rewriting it. The entry's cell is **reached**: the backend behind it is built for it, and `run_in: container` there needs `local.image`. A seat's own `role.sandbox` therefore places nothing: what the executor's box reads off it — its `setup`, `env` and `mcp`, and its `pause_ttl_seconds` and `max_turns` — are the seat's settings for the box its entry places. Validation refuses the impossible and nothing else: a `run_in` whose backend is not configured, a company default naming an unconfigured backend, an entry some seat's executor reaches that names no cell against a catalogue with no default, and agent seats with models in a company with no catalogue at all — each of which otherwise reads as working configuration and fails inside a turn. A `run_in` on an entry whose harness cannot run an executor (anything but `claude-code` and `opencode`) is refused too, because no launch would ever read it.

### Choosing a cell

The run is the same fully permissioned harness in every cell; what differs is what it can reach.

- **`direct`** runs it as a process tree on the engine host, as the engine's own user, in a directory of its own. It is the shortest path — no image, no remote account, the subscription login `crewlet llm login` already made on that host — and the right one for a dedicated host you are willing to hand to an autonomous agent, for the entry that needs the host itself, or for a first run on your own machine while its seats take work from you alone: the run can read what the engine's user can read and reach what that user's credentials reach. On any node that binds `api.port`, a `direct` box reaches its [tool bridge](#the-tool-bridge--a-seats-own-tools-from-inside-a-box) on that node's own loopback listener with nothing to set, which is why the [quickstart](../getting-started/quickstart.md), a first run, starts every seat this way; before a seat takes work from anyone else, move the runs into `container`.
- **`container`** runs it in a Docker or Podman container on the engine host, with only the box's own directory mounted, so the run reaches the host only through what the container is given. It needs `local.image` with the harness installed ([Bring your own image](#bring-your-own-image)) and `CREWLET_MCP_BRIDGE_URL`, which a single host sets to its own loopback: a loopback address is handed to the box under the runtime's name for the engine host, and on Linux `api.host` must not be loopback, since the container's calls arrive from its bridge network ([Local sandboxes](#local-sandboxes)). Pick it for any other local instance, a workstation included, a shared host, or wherever the work should not reach the engine host's files.
- **`e2b`** runs it on a fresh remote VM per run, off the engine host altogether, and needs `e2b.api_key` (a self-hosted cluster takes `e2b.domain` too) and `CREWLET_MCP_BRIDGE_URL`, set to an address the VM reaches, a reverse proxy or tunnel to the node included ([The tool bridge](#the-tool-bridge--a-seats-own-tools-from-inside-a-box)). A login never leaves the engine host for it, so the entry signs in with a headless token or a key in the run's environment ([Credentials and the run environment](#credentials-and-the-run-environment)).

A company can use all three: the cell is the entry's, so two entries on the same harness and model with different `run_in` let each seat take the one that is right for its work, and a fallback member of a seat's executor chain is placed by its own `run_in`.

**A run remembers its cell.** The placement is written onto the run's durable row *before the box exists*, and reconnect, teardown and the pause reaper all read it from there. It is not re-derived: the turn that collects a run may be a different process on a different node, days later, with the company configuration applied again in between — and reconnecting to a remote box through the local backend does not error usefully, it reports a box that has vanished, so a job that is still running would be abandoned as gone. An entry that names no cell is given the company's default at launch, so every row names one.

### Whether a cell is ready

Each node probes the cells `providers.sandbox` declares and publishes the answer on its heartbeat, so the Models page can say, per node, which cells can start a box now and disable the rest with the reason:

| Cell | Ready when |
|---|---|
| `direct` | the engine host runs the local backend (Linux or macOS) and the box root under `local.state_dir` is writable |
| `container` | `local.image` is set, the runtime (`docker` or `podman`, per `local.runtime`) is on the engine's `PATH`, and its daemon answers `version --format '{{.Server.Version}}'` within 5 s |
| `e2b` | `e2b.api_key` resolves on this node, and one authenticated read of the API (`GET /v2/sandboxes?limit=1`) answers within 5 s |

A cell the company does not declare reads `unconfigured`, never not ready. A cell that is not ready carries its fix, for example "Install Docker or Podman on this node and restart the engine: PATH is read at start". The probe runs at boot, at every apply and every five minutes; it never makes a box. The `direct` cell is not probed for host confinement: an executor's direct box is not confined, so a host without bubblewrap or Landlock still runs it.

### Sandbox backends

The provider layer is pluggable behind the `sandbox.Provider` interface (`internal/sandbox/protocol.go`). Two backends run code, and one stands in for them:

- **E2B cloud** (`e2b:`, no `domain`). Sign up at [e2b.dev](https://e2b.dev), export `E2B_API_KEY` from the dashboard. The engine talks to the documented REST API directly — there is no Go SDK to install, and no optional dependency for any of this — and uses E2B's prebuilt [`claude`](https://e2b.dev/docs/agents/claude-code) / [`opencode`](https://e2b.dev/docs/agents/opencode) templates (the harness harness preinstalled), picked automatically per harness when `template` is empty. A run given [repositories on Gitlet](#repositories-on-gitlet) also needs **git 2.40 or newer** in its box, and E2B does not publish what its `claude` and `opencode` templates are built from, so which git they carry is not established here: the launch's own check says, failing the launch with the git it found where a template's is below the floor, and `template:` then names one built with a newer git. The last build definition E2B published for its `base` template — what a harness this build does not know falls back to, and which carries no harness either — installs Debian bookworm's git, 2.39, below the floor. So on a deployment that leaves `template:` empty, a run given repositories **may fail that check** — its setup step `git-version`, naming the git it found — and until a box of each default template has been seen to carry 2.40 or newer, a company hosting code on E2B is safest naming a `template:` built with one.
- **Self-hosted / local E2B** (`e2b:` + `domain`). E2B's infrastructure is open source ([e2b-dev/infra](https://github.com/e2b-dev/infra)); set `domain` to your cluster's domain and the **same code path** talks to it — one field is the whole cloud↔self-hosted switch, because the control-plane address and every box's own hostname are both derived from it. The cluster still issues its own key.
- **[Local](#local-sandboxes)** (`local:`). The engine host itself — `run_in: direct` (a process tree) or `run_in: container` (Docker/Podman). No remote account and no API key, and the harness can use the **subscription login** `crewlet llm login` already established. See below.
- **`fake: true`** — deterministic in-process stubs (`internal/sandbox/fake.go`): an in-memory filesystem, scripted harness results, no network. The unit-test substrate, and a way to demonstrate the flow: it does **not** run real code, and every run it plays finishes at once with a report saying the double played it, so the turn collects a run that submitted nothing and is judged on that. It answers **every** cell, and is refused beside a real backend — the double *replaces* the backends rather than joining them, so a real block underneath it would read as configuration and configure nothing.
- **No sandbox at all** — omit `providers.sandbox`, which only a company with no agent seat, or with no `providers.llm` entry yet, may do. Any other company is refused at `providers.sandbox`, naming its first agent seat.

The closed set `run_in` is checked against is exactly the set the engine can construct — a test walks it, because a value the config accepts and the engine cannot build fails at the first run and nowhere earlier. The engine builds a backend only for the cells your company actually **reaches**, read from the same computation validation uses: built eagerly instead, it constructed a container backend for a company whose seats all run `direct` and failed the apply demanding an image the validator had just refused as a field nothing would read.

> **Two planes, and they behave differently on purpose.** The control plane (`api.<domain>`) mints, kills, pauses and re-times boxes under a bounded timeout, because minting a VM is a real allocation with a real cost when it is abandoned. The in-box plane — running commands, reading and writing files — is reached on each box's own hostname and carries **no overall deadline at all**: a harness's foreground command legitimately runs for many minutes, so what bounds it is the gap *between* output frames rather than the length of the call (ten minutes of silence). A file transfer is bounded the same way and more tightly: envd answers from a file that already exists, so a read or write that moves no byte for sixty seconds — the control plane's own request budget — is abandoned and says so, rather than holding that box's poll for as long as the process lives. (The poll of a box is bounded as a whole as well, and no box's poll waits on another's — see [the completion poll](#durability-completion-and-observability).)

> **Boxes are created with [secured access](https://e2b.dev/docs/sandbox/secured-access).** A secured box's in-box agent runs a command or serves a file only for a request carrying that box's own access token, and the engine sends it (`X-Access-Token`) on every command and file call. E2B hands the token back on the create, on a reconnect and on a read of the box alike, so the engine never stores it. Every box the engine creates is secured. A template whose in-box agent predates secured access (envd below 0.2.0) is refused at the create, with E2B's own message to rebuild it.

### Local sandboxes

The `local:` backend runs the harness on the machine running `crewlet run`. It exists for the case the [LLM backends](llm-backends.md) create: you already logged a harness in on that host, and you want every executor run to use that same login — no E2B account, no API key, no token to mint.

```yaml
providers:
  sandbox:
    local:
      state_dir: ""               # empty → $CREWLET_SANDBOX_LOCAL_HOME,
                                  #   else ~/.crewlet/sandboxes
    default_run_in: direct        # direct | container — no implied answer
```

**`state_dir` is the box root** of both local cells, and of Ask Crewlet's [deep work](../guides/assistant.md#where-a-run-happens) when it runs on the engine host: a seat's box is made under its `boxes/`, and a deep-work run's confined box under its `assistant/boxes/`, which a seat's backend never lists, reattaches or reaps. Give it a volume sized for boxes and never the one holding the store and the stream ([Where a box writes](../guides/deployment.md#where-a-box-writes)).

Both shipped examples pair a `local:` catalogue with `run_in: direct` and run every seat's executor as its entry's [own run in a box](llm-backends.md#the-executors-run-in-a-box) on the engine host — each is a first run on your own machine while its seats take work from you alone, and names the `container` block to move to before a seat takes work from anyone else — and the difference between them is worth reading side by side. [`examples/nimbus.company.yaml`](../../examples/nimbus.company.yaml) runs on an `opencode` entry and has a code host to push to, so its runs open merge requests. [`examples/nimbus-claude-code.company.yaml`](../../examples/nimbus-claude-code.company.yaml) runs on the Claude Code entry's plan login and has **no code host at all**.

That second shape is worth saying out loud, because a sandbox and a code host are often assumed to come together. A sandbox with nowhere to push is still a place to run code: a spike, a benchmark, a reproduction, an SDK example that has actually executed. Add a code host later and the same runs start opening merge requests; nothing about the sandbox block changes.

**`local:` alone still needs a `default_run_in`** (or a `run_in` on every entry a seat's executor launches on), because it serves two cells and choosing `direct` is choosing to run an autonomous harness as the engine user. The block carries no mode of its own: `direct` and `container` are a choice about **one entry's runs** — point the seats that should use the host's login at an entry with `run_in: direct`, and the others at one with `run_in: container` — and a block-level mode could only ever answer for every run at once.

| | `run_in: direct` | `run_in: container` |
|---|---|---|
| Runs as | the engine user, on the host | PID inside a Docker/Podman container |
| Isolates state (per box) | yes | yes |
| Isolates the **host** | **no** | yes |
| Needs an image | no | yes (`image:`, no default) |
| A setup file outside the box's home | refused: it would be the host's | refused: only the home is mounted |
| Setup commands run | on the host, as the engine user | in the container: as the engine's uid on a rootful runtime, as container root on a rootless one |
| Sizing | the host | `run_args: ["--cpus", "2", "--memory", "4g"]` |
| When memory runs out (Linux) | the kernel kills the box before the engine | the same, through the runtime's `--oom-score-adj`; past its own `--memory`, only inside the container |

**Both modes isolate state.** Each box gets its own directory under `boxes/` with two sides: `home/`, which is the agent's — `HOME`, the XDG variables and `TMPDIR` are pointed inside it, its working directory is an empty per-box workspace there (a `direct` command starts in the home and its own shell enters the directory it was asked to run in, so every confinement starts it there and the engine never walks into it), and in `container` mode it is the one directory mounted into the container — and `records/`, which is the engine's: the logins the box was seeded with and a digest of each (so a reconnected teardown knows what to write back, where, and against what), the keepalive stamp, a `direct` job's process group, a `container` job's process group in its container (which the job's start reads, so a kill reaches the whole job and nothing else of the container's), the engine process that last saw the box's job running (see [A job lost with its host](#durability-completion-and-observability)), and the env file. The two used to be one directory, which let a containerised job rewrite what the engine knew about its own box — name any file the engine's user could write as the "shared login" to copy a box file over at teardown, or make the env file a link the next exec followed out of the box. A box's environment is **allowlisted** (PATH, locale, TLS trust, proxy — never the engine's own keyring, its Tier A token values, its `SLACK_BOT_TOKEN` or its collector credential in `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS`). The allowlist is the same one every child the engine starts is handed — a [stdio MCP server](../guides/tools-and-mcp.md#what-a-stdio-servers-environment-is) and a harness get it too. In `container` mode the run environment is handed to the runtime through an `--env-file` in the box's records rather than as `-e KEY=value`, because a process's argv is world-readable on the host (`/proc/<pid>/cmdline`, every `ps`) and that environment carries the seat's LLM key and whatever token `role.sandbox.env` declares; the runtime's client reads it on the host, so the container has no need of it. A `direct` job runs as the engine's own user with no mount, so it can reach its box's records too — which is what `direct` is documented to be below.

**The container runtime's own CLI gets an explicit environment too.** Every `docker`/`podman` invocation — `run`, `exec`, `pause`, `rm`, `ps`, `info` — is started with exactly the environment a [stdio MCP server whose `command` is `docker` or `podman`](../guides/tools-and-mcp.md#what-a-stdio-servers-environment-is) gets, which that table lists name by name: the host allowlist, the engine user's home, temporary and XDG directories (where the runtime keeps its config, and `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR`, where a rootless daemon's socket lives), and the runtime's own configuration — the `DOCKER_*` family, Podman's, and the few settings they read by name. Nothing else of the engine's reaches it, or any plugin and credential helper it runs.

**Whatever else the runtime needs, declare in `local.runtime_env`.** What a runtime's helpers read is open-ended, so the fixed list cannot carry it: the credential helper that pulls your image from a private registry needs its cloud's role, profile or key file (`docker-credential-ecr-login`, `docker-credential-gcr`), and a runtime plugin may read settings of its own. Name them there, exactly as you would an MCP server's `env:` — a `${VAR}` resolves from the [secret store](secret-store.md) first and the engine's own environment behind it, when the backend is built on an apply — and a declared name wins over the fixed list's value for it:

```yaml
providers:
  sandbox:
    local:
      image: ghcr.io/acme/crewlet-coding:1
      runtime_env:
        AWS_ROLE_ARN: "${REGISTRY_PULL_ROLE}"         # docker-credential-ecr-login
        AWS_WEB_IDENTITY_TOKEN_FILE: /var/run/secrets/eks.amazonaws.com/serviceaccount/token
        GOOGLE_APPLICATION_CREDENTIALS: "${GCR_KEY_FILE}"   # docker-credential-gcr
```

It is the **runtime's** environment and never the box's: nothing in it reaches the harness except through an argument you add to `run_args`. That is the one consequence for `local.run_args` worth knowing — a bare `-e NAME` copies `NAME` from the runtime harness's environment, so it copies only a name the fixed list carries or `runtime_env` declares, and anything else arrives in the box unset. `crewlet validate` warns about each such `-e NAME` where it is written. Put what a box needs in `role.sandbox.env` (or a setup step's `env`), which reaches the box directly and is what that is for.

**Boxes are removed at teardown, and a crash-orphaned one is reaped on the next create, but only when it is genuinely orphaned.** Two independent questions are asked. *Is anything running in it?* A live process group that is still the box's own job (`direct`) or an existing container, read from the operating system rather than inferred. *Has anything touched it?* The completion poll refreshes a keepalive stamp on every tick, which is what `Sandbox.SetTimeout` means on this backend, exactly as it refreshes an E2B box's TTL — and a launch refreshes it on the box it is still provisioning, which has no job yet to answer the first question for it. A box is reaped only when both say no: nothing running, and nothing has touched it for a whole `default_timeout_seconds`. A run blocked on a human answer is covered by the first (its process group is stopped, not gone) and bounded by `pause_ttl_seconds`, which is the knob that owns that wait. A reaped box still gives back any login the run refreshed before it was abandoned.

**A pid is not an identity.** A `direct` box records its job's process group as the leader's pid *together with the leader's start time*, read from the kernel's own process record (`/proc/<pid>/stat` with the boot id on Linux, `sysctl kern.proc.pid` on macOS) while the engine still holds the unreaped process. Every later act on that record, from a different process or hours on, first asks whether the group is still that one: the kernel hands a pid out again once its process is gone, and a bare pid read back from disk may by then lead somebody else's processes. A recorded group that is gone or now belongs to a later process keeps no box alive and receives no signal, so a teardown, a kill or a clarification pause can never reach a stranger's tree. When the kernel's record cannot be read, the reaper keeps the directory and a signal is withheld and logged (`local_sandbox_signal_withheld`), because neither can tell a live job from a recycled pid.

**Only `container` isolates the host.** In `direct` the harness runs fully permissioned as the engine user, so it can read what that user can read and reach what its credentials reach. That is the normal bargain of a local cell, and it is the right one on a dedicated host you are willing to hand to an autonomous agent and for a first run on your own machine while its seats take work from you alone, but it is not a boundary around the host, and Crewlet does not present it as one. **For any other local instance, a workstation included, and before a seat takes work from anyone else, run code work in `container`**, with [an image of your own](#bring-your-own-image), and keep `direct` for the entry that has a reason to need the host itself. Give a `providers.llm` entry `run_in: container` (or `e2b`), and every executor run launched on it runs there, or point the whole company at it with `default_run_in`:

```yaml
providers:
  sandbox:
    local:
      image: registry.example.com/coding:1   # must have the harness installed:
                                             #   see "Bring your own image" below
      runtime: auto                          # auto | docker | podman
      network: ""                            # "none" cuts the box off entirely —
                                             #   including from its own LLM
      run_args: ["--cpus", "2", "--memory", "4g"]
    default_run_in: container
```

A `local.network` you set has to reach the engine host too: `none` cuts the box off from its [tool bridge](#the-tool-bridge--a-seats-own-tools-from-inside-a-box) as well, and with it from every tool of its seat.

The box directory is bind-mounted at `/home/user` — the same home E2B uses — so in-box paths, setup steps and briefs are identical across the two backends. The container is started with `--init` so a finished detached job is reaped rather than left as a zombie (which the completion probe would read as *still running*).

**The container runs as the engine's own user, and the mount is proved before the box is used.** A bind mount is one directory shared by two processes, so both of them have to be able to manage what the other writes. Under a **rootful** Docker or Podman the container's root is the host's root, and every directory the box creates in the mount comes out root-owned — the engine, which is not root, can then neither write a run's files into it nor reclaim the box's disk at teardown, so box directories accumulate on the host with no error anywhere. Crewlet therefore passes `--user <uid>:<gid>` on a rootful runtime and **not** on a rootless one, where the user namespace already maps container root onto the invoking user and `--user` would map it into the subuid range instead. The runtime is asked which it is (`docker info` / `podman info`); an unanswerable probe is treated as rootful, because that mistake fails loudly at creation while the other is silent.

If your image genuinely needs its own user — one that installs packages at run time, say — put `--user 0:0` in `run_args`. Operator flags are spliced last and both runtimes take the final occurrence of a repeated flag, so yours wins. Be aware you are taking back the problem above.

Every container box is then proved once, at creation, before anything runs in it: the engine writes a token the box reads back, and the box creates a directory the engine writes into and removes. That catches the ownership case and one other that is otherwise invisible — a `runtime` pointed at a daemon on **another host** (`DOCKER_HOST`, a forwarded socket) bind-mounts *that* machine's filesystem, so the agent finds no brief, no credentials and no tools. A box that fails the proof is torn down and the error names the cause; use the `e2b` provider for a genuinely remote runtime.

**A box reaches the engine host by the runtime's name for it.** The engine hands a box addresses written in its own network: the [tool bridge](#the-tool-bridge--a-seats-own-tools-from-inside-a-box) (`CREWLET_MCP_BRIDGE_URL`), the telemetry receiver (`CREWLET_SANDBOX_OTEL_RECEIVER_URL`), the URL of every HTTP [MCP server](#mcp-inside-the-sandbox) the seat scopes into the box, and the run's [notes route](#notes-to-a-running-run) on the bridge, which OpenCode's plugin polls. A single-host install sets those to loopback (`http://127.0.0.1:8000`), and inside a container loopback is the container itself. So where a URL the engine hands a `container` box names the engine host's loopback (`127.0.0.1`, any `127.x` address, `::1` or `localhost`), the box is given the same URL with the host replaced by the runtime's own name for the engine host: `host.docker.internal` under Docker, which the engine defines for every box it starts (`--add-host host.docker.internal:host-gateway`, which Docker Engine on Linux needs and Docker Desktop answers the same way), and `host.containers.internal` under Podman, which defines it itself. Any other host is handed on as written, since it already names a machine both sides can see, and so is every value you declare for the box yourself (`role.sandbox.env`, a setup step's `env`), which is in the box's network to begin with: a box reaching a service on the host names `host.docker.internal` there. `direct` shares the host's network and is handed every address as written — and for the bridge it needs none on a node that serves the API, dialling that node's own listener on loopback, which the node derives from the socket it binds — so one company runs `direct` entries and `container` entries side by side with one loopback `CREWLET_MCP_BRIDGE_URL`, which only the containers read. Docker Desktop, and Podman's machine on macOS and Windows, deliver that name over the host's loopback, where a loopback bind hears it and the [tool bridge](#the-tool-bridge--a-seats-own-tools-from-inside-a-box) serves it: a seat's run under any name, and an Ask Crewlet deep-work run held to the host's loopback under a loopback host name or one of these two runtime names alone. Docker Engine and Podman on Linux deliver it from the container's bridge network rather than the host's loopback, so the engine's API must listen there too: `api.host` empty (every interface) or the bridge's address, never `127.0.0.1`. `crewlet validate`, given both files, and every apply on a Linux node with both the `ingress` and the `seats` role — the one that serves the bridge and the receiver and starts the boxes that dial them on this host; a node without `seats` starts no box here, so whatever dials its routes comes from another machine — warn (`config_advisory` at `api.host`, or `api.public.host` when the public listener serves the bridge) when a company that reaches the `container` cell meets a loopback bind.

**Setup commands get a provisioning budget, not a control one.** Each command may run for `timeout_seconds` (default 600). Real provisioning — a dependency install, a cold image pull, a large clone — takes minutes, and without its own budget these inherited the backend's control-plane timeout and were killed, failing the whole acquisition. Raise it for a step you know is slow; lower it for one that should be instant, so a hung command surfaces as a named setup failure rather than eating the turn.
