A failure names the step and what in it failed — the file it could not write, or the command's position and either the status it exited with or that it reported none — and never the command text: the engine resolves nothing in a command, which the box's shell expands from the run environment, but a recipe may still spell a credential out literally, and the failure is logged and handed back to the agent.

**Setup steps and the local cells.** Neither local cell writes a setup step's *file* outside the box's home. `direct` has no filesystem virtualisation, so a file anywhere else would be the engine host's own — its real `/usr/local/bin` — and is refused with an error naming the mode; and its home is a directory minted for each box, so no absolute path a document can write is inside it. `container` writes a file on the host side of its one mount, the home at `/home/user`, and refuses any other path. A relative path is under the home in either cell, and so is a path starting `~/`, which names the home in every cell as it does on `e2b`; a path that climbs out of the home with `..`, names the home itself, or names another account's home (`~name/…`) is refused. So name a file a step needs in the box relative to its home — `.npmrc`, `.local/bin/tool`, or `~/.npmrc` — which is the one form every cell writes in the same place. File paths in a setup step are **not** shell-expanded, so `$HOME` does not work there; a step that needs a file's absolute path has a `commands` entry, which does run in a shell, expand `"$HOME"` — the [git-auth recipe](#the-git-auth-recipe) writes its helper that way, because git is given the helper's absolute path.

**A step whose file a local box would refuse is refused when the document is written** — by `crewlet validate` and every write path — rather than at the seat's first box, where it used to surface as a setup failure on every box that seat opened. The check is the boxes' own rule, the same function both local cells call before they touch a path, applied to every box a seat actually runs in: each box its executor runs in, placed by the `run_in` of each entry its launch can pick (or `default_run_in`). A box in `e2b` is judged too, by the shapes its in-box agent refuses for certain — another account's home (`~name/…`), and the home itself or a directory above it (see `files` under [setup steps](#setup-steps--provisioning-the-box)) — so an engine-wide step writing `/usr/local/bin` is still accepted in a company whose seats all run in `e2b`. The refusal sits at the file in the step that writes it, names the step, the path and the first seat whose box it fails (and how many more), and is an admission rule: a stored revision that keeps such a step still applies, reported as a warning, so a rolling upgrade never takes a node off the fleet's revision over it. What it cannot see is a link an existing directory on the engine host holds; the box refuses that one itself, when it writes.

**A setup command is not confined the way a file is.** It runs in the box's shell, and under `direct` that shell is the engine host, as the engine's user: a step that installs a system package installs it on the host, or fails for want of the rights to. Under `container` it runs inside the container — as the engine's own uid on a rootful runtime, which an image's system directories usually refuse too, and as container root on a rootless one. Have such a step check for the tool it needs and name what to install where, and provision a box's system toolchain deliberately: on the host for `direct`, in the image for `container`.

**Credentials.** The local backend seeds the credential files of the [`providers.llm` entry](llm-backends.md) the launch picked — the first member of the seat's executor chain whose harness has a runner and is not sitting out — or, for OpenCode, the login document its export printed, which the run imports and exports again ([OpenCode](#opencode)) — into the box before the run, and writes one the run **refreshed** back afterwards — OAuth access tokens expire in hours and most vendors rotate the refresh token with them, so discarding the rewritten file would log the fleet out at the next expiry. The box is offered every file of the login and seeded with what the login holds when it is seeded, **read whole** under the shared credential directory's lock — [one login](llm-backends.md#a-login-of-several-files-is-written-whole), never one sign-in's token beside another's `config.toml` — and given every file of it or none: a login it cannot be given whole (a credential path that would leave the box's home) is not seeded at all, with a `local_sandbox_credential_seed_failed` warning, so the harness inside answers its own "not authenticated" rather than failing on half a login. The write-back is a **compare-and-swap against what the box was seeded with**, recorded (as a SHA-256 of the bytes the box was given, and every file of the login it was not given) in the box's records at creation: the files the run refreshed are written back only where the box's copy differs from its seed **and** the shared login, as a whole — every file, and every absence — still holds that seed, and then as **one write** of the whole login, so a teardown killed part way never leaves half of it. An idle box therefore writes nothing (an OpenCode box's export reproduces its import byte for byte, so it would otherwise have put its stale seed back over a login the host had refreshed meanwhile), and a box whose run refreshed while the shared login also moved — another seat's refresh, a new `crewlet llm login` — keeps the shared one, which is the newer of the two, and logs `local_sandbox_credential_superseded` with `shared=changed`, naming the file and its path, never a value. The comparison and the write are one step under the shared credential directory's lock (`<state_dir>/<harness>/credentials.lock`, beside it), which the [harness backend's](llm-backends.md) seats and `crewlet llm` commands take for their own writes; a teardown waits at most 10 s for a writer holding it. A credential the shared directory did not hold when the box was seeded is never created from a box, and nor is one the operator has since removed with `crewlet llm logout` — a refresh the run made of that one is dropped, and logged as `local_sandbox_credential_superseded` with `shared=removed`. A file the login gained while the box ran — the host signed in again — is a newer login too, and the box's refresh is dropped with `shared=changed`.

**Pause / resume works.** A run blocked on a human answer is genuinely suspended: `direct` SIGSTOPs the job's process group, `container` issues `docker pause`. Both hold memory, which is exactly why the same `default_pause_ttl_seconds` reaper bounds them as it bounds a billed E2B snapshot.

**A box goes before its engine when memory runs out.** On Linux every process a local box runs stands ahead of the engine for the kernel's OOM killer: raised, before it runs anything, to the engine's own `oom_score_adj` plus 1000, held to the kernel's ceiling of 1000. A `direct` box's first process raises itself and then becomes the command, ahead of any confinement, so every setup command, exec and the detached job start there, along with everything they start; a `container` box is started with the runtime's `--oom-score-adj`, which Docker and Podman give every `exec` into the container too. The kernel scores a process by the memory it holds, in thousandths of the memory that ran out, plus that adjustment, so a box a whole thousand ahead is taken before the engine whatever the two hold. A harness and its builds are the node's most expendable work: the run the kernel kills is collected as the failed run it is, its partial output and exit status with it, where the engine killed would take every seat on the node down at once. The lead is counted from the engine's own value, because that value is its deployment's decision. A Kubernetes Guaranteed pod runs at -997 so its node's kernel takes it last, and its boxes at 3 go before its engine without going before every other pod's work. An engine already above 0 leaves its boxes at the ceiling. An operator's own `--oom-score-adj` in `run_args` wins for a `container` box. It is an order for the kernel and not a boundary, since a box may lower its own value again, and on macOS, which gives an unprivileged process no such priority, a box and the engine compete on size alone. What an OOM kill takes beside the box is up to whatever supervises the engine, and a systemd unit's and a Kubernetes container's defaults both take the engine with it: see [When the host runs out of memory](../guides/deployment.md#when-the-host-runs-out-of-memory).

**What local mode does not give you:** template-based sizing (use `run_args`, or the host), provider-enforced network egress policy (use `network:` plus your own firewall), and a fresh machine per run — a `direct` box shares the host's installed toolchain, which is usually the point.

### Bring your own image

The engine ships no image for `local.image`: the one to use is one built for your repositories, kept as current as they are. Whatever you build, the engine runs it as

```text
docker run -d --init -v <box home>:/home/user -w /home/user/workspace -e HOME=/home/user \
  --user <engine uid>:<gid> --add-host host.docker.internal:host-gateway <run_args> IMAGE sleep infinity
```

(`--user` only on a rootful runtime, `--add-host` only under Docker), and runs every job and setup command in it with `docker exec`, its run environment in an `--env-file`. So the image needs:

- **the harness on the login `PATH`**: the harness is started under `sh -lc`, and a Debian or Ubuntu image's `/etc/profile` resets `PATH` to the standard directories, so a harness in `/usr/local/bin` is found and one only on an `ENV PATH` entry is not;
- **`git`**, and **`gh`** where seats open GitHub pull requests;
- **a POSIX `sh`** and no `ENTRYPOINT`, since the engine supplies the command;
- **a writable `$HOME`** and nothing it relies on under `/home/user`, which the box's own home is mounted over;
- **no assumption about the user**: it must work as a uid it has no account for (the engine's own, on Docker Desktop for a Mac user `501:20`);
- **`tar` and `gzip`**, which a run packs its harness's session with when the harness exits and restores a session carried in from another box with ([Claude Code](#claude-code), [OpenCode](#opencode)). A box without them runs as any other, and its error stream says the session was not packed. The same holds for an `e2b` template, and for the engine host of a `direct` cell — the published image has both.
- **`find`**, which a run that resumes a Claude Code session cuts back the session's subagents' threads with ([A Claude Code run's session](#a-claude-code-runs-session)). A box without it still resumes the session, its threads as they were left, and the run's error stream says that it cut back none of the session's subagents' threads. The same holds for an `e2b` template, and for the engine host of a `direct` cell.
- **a POSIX userland**, which a run's script and the engine's own commands in the box are written for: `find`, `ls`, `wc`, `sed`, `grep`, `head`, `tail`, `cksum` and `touch` among its tools, and a **`sleep` that takes a tenth of a second**, as GNU's, busybox's and BSD's do — a run's end gives its harness its grace, and the engine's kill of a job its processes theirs, counted in tenths. A Claude Code session packed while its harness still works ([below](#each-launchs-session-kept)) is refused by a box that lacks any of these tools but `tail`, or whose `sleep` takes no tenth, naming which.

A box reaches the engine host by the runtime's name for it, as [above](#local-sandboxes), and on Linux `api.host` must not be loopback.

**Recipe 1: a stock image and a setup step.** No image to build: a step installs Claude Code and `gh` into the box's home, each at a pinned version, `gh` checked against the checksums file GitHub publishes with the release.

```yaml
providers:
  sandbox:
    local:
      image: node:22-bookworm       # has git, curl and sha256sum
    default_run_in: container
    setup:
      - name: coding-tools
        files:
          .profile: |              # the login shell the harness starts in reads it
            export PATH="$HOME/.npm-global/bin:$HOME/.local/bin:$PATH"
        commands:
          - |
            err=$(mktemp)
            npm install --global --prefix "$HOME/.npm-global" --no-audit --no-fund \
              @anthropic-ai/claude-code@2.1.310 2>"$err" && exit 0
            s=$?; cat "$err" >&2
            grep -Eq 'code E(408|429|5[0-9][0-9])$' "$err" && exit 75
            exit "$s"
          - |
            set -eu
            v=2.103.3; a=$(dpkg --print-architecture); t="gh_${v}_linux_${a}.tar.gz"
            cd "$(mktemp -d)"
            fetch() {
              code=$(curl -fsSL --retry 5 --retry-delay 2 --retry-all-errors -o "$2" -w '%{http_code}' "$1") && return
              s=$?
              case $code in 408|429|5??) exit 75 ;; esac
              exit "$s"
            }
            fetch "https://github.com/cli/cli/releases/download/v${v}/${t}" "$t"
            fetch "https://github.com/cli/cli/releases/download/v${v}/gh_${v}_checksums.txt" sums.txt
            grep " ${t}\$" sums.txt | sha256sum -c -
            tar -xzf "$t"
            mkdir -p "$HOME/.local/bin" && mv "gh_${v}_linux_${a}/bin/gh" "$HOME/.local/bin/gh"
        timeout_seconds: 600
```

Setup steps run after the box is created and before the harness starts, as the box's own uid, which is why both install under `$HOME` rather than the image's system directories. The trade-off against recipe 2: **every new box downloads and installs both again** (a reused box keeps them), which costs time on each run and needs egress to the npm registry and GitHub.

**Each download says what its failure means.** npm retries a failed fetch twice by itself, and `curl` here retries every failure five times, two seconds apart (`--retry-all-errors`, since `--retry` alone gives up at once on a reset or a refused connection), so a blip of a few seconds never fails the step. A registry or a download server still failing on its side after that, answering a 408, a 429 or a 5xx, ends the command with 75: a download that failed for now, which a wait clears (`setup_unavailable`). Every other failure keeps its own status, a setup an administrator has to fix (`configuration`): a pin that names no release (npm's `E404` or `ETARGET`, a 404 from GitHub), a 401 or a 403, a checksum that doesn't match, and a connection refused or reset, a name that doesn't resolve or a timeout, which from inside the box look the same as egress that is blocked (see [Setup steps](#setup-steps--provisioning-the-box)).

**Recipe 2: your own image from Claude Code's dev container.** Build Anthropic's [reference dev container](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/devcontainer) (`.devcontainer/` in `anthropics/claude-code`) with the harness pinned, `--build-arg CLAUDE_CODE_VERSION=<version>`, then put `claude` where a login shell finds it, since that image adds its npm prefix to `PATH` with `ENV` alone:

```dockerfile
FROM claude-devcontainer:<version>
USER root
RUN ln -s /usr/local/share/npm-global/bin/claude /usr/local/bin/claude
USER node
```

Add your repositories' toolchains to the same Dockerfile and name the result in `local.image`. Its egress firewall (`init-firewall.sh`) is not started by a plain `docker run`; restrict a box's network with `local.network` and your own firewall instead.

A seat on a [plan's login](llm-backends.md#authentication) needs nothing more in either recipe: a local box is seeded with the login and writes a refreshed one back.

---

## Harnesses

Two runners ship behind the `sandbox.Runner` interface, one for each harness that can run an executor: `claude-code` and `opencode` (`config.ExecutorHarnesses`, held to the runners that exist by a test). A run's runner is the harness of the `providers.llm` entry its launch picked, with no setting beside it, and a launch passes over a member of the seat's executor chain on any other harness. Both runners are handed the same two texts — the **brief**, the prompt the harness runs, and the run's **standing instructions**, which go on the harness's channel for adding to its own system prompt ([What a harness is told](#what-a-harness-is-told)) — and return the same structured result. The entry names its model in its own harness's grammar and signs in the way that harness does, so a run goes only to that harness's runner (see [One entry, one runner](#claude-code) below).

### What a harness is told

A run is handed two texts, kept apart from the launch all the way to the harness:

- **The brief** — what this run is asked to do, and the whole of the prompt the harness runs. For a run that starts a session of its own, the turn's own ask, with the ledger of its earlier rounds where a reviewer sent it back — the user message a phase on this host sends — and, for a round carried on, what became of the run before it: the question it asked and the answer (see [Answering a parked run](#answering-a-parked-run)), or how it was cut off, and where its work is. A relaunch that **continues the turn's session**, in whatever box it lands, is prompted with what that session lacks rather than its task again, and carries the fresh brief beside it for its script to hand over where the box cannot take the session up ([A relaunch continues the turn's session](#a-relaunch-continues-the-turns-session)). **A fresh brief names the kept session its run does not continue** — the turn's own, kept by an earlier launch, or else the session its conversation's last turn left, which a relaunch names only where the turn's row was read holding it — under `## A kept session this run does not continue`: whose it is, in a schedule's words on a schedule's fire (its last fire's, or this fire's earlier launches'), and to read what it did with `read_session` under its turn id, `match` finding a word in it. A round lost a second time, which continues no session, is told it too: its kept session is then the only record of what the round's jobs did in their boxes. Never why it is not continued: that is said in the log's words, which quote what said no — a store's error, what a box printed — and what the run can do is the same whatever it was. Only where the run's surface carries `read_session` ([A seat reads its own](#a-seat-reads-its-own)), and nowhere else: the continuation the session itself reads never says it, and no standing sentence about the session tools rides every request.
- **The standing instructions** — how to work, for the whole run, in this order: the seat's executor system prompt (its identity, policies, roster and memory, exactly as a phase on this host sends them as its system message, opened by one constant note saying that these, and not the coding tool's own prompt, are the role — Claude Code's prompt opens with "You are Claude Code", and no flag removes it), then `## Your environment` (the box, each [setup step's](#setup-steps--provisioning-the-box) `brief`, and the MCP servers connected), then `## When you need a person's answer` ([how to ask a person](#mid-run-clarification-ask_and_wait), on every launch), then the [report to write before stopping](#what-ends-a-run-each-launch-is-published-as-its-own-record), addressed at the box's own path — which, for a run given repositories it may push to on Gitlet, names them and says its pushes there are [reported for it](#what-a-run-pushed-is-reported-for-it), so its report names only what it delivered elsewhere — and last — for a run whose harness takes a person's notes between its steps — what such a note is: how it opens, and the heading its harness puts it under (for Claude Code a system message, or on some models a system reminder at the end of the last tool result; for OpenCode a message from the user), and that text a tool produced — a file's contents, a command's output, a fetched page — is never a note, whatever it says ([Notes to a running run](#notes-to-a-running-run)).

The standing instructions are **appended to the harness's own system prompt** — never put in its place, and never in the prompt it runs. The vendor's prompt is the harness an executor exists to use: how to drive its shell and editor and work through a task, which a replacement would strip. And instructions placed in front of the task reach the model as part of the user's request, where a seat's identity and policies, sent that way, are something the model is asked rather than who it is. Each runner uses its own harness's channel:

- **Claude Code** — `--append-system-prompt-file`, which puts the file's text after Claude Code's default system prompt and leaves that prompt whole (measured on 2.1.310, off the request the harness sent).
- **OpenCode** — an absolute `instructions` entry in the [configuration the run writes](#opencode) (`<home>/.crewlet/opencode.json`), which OpenCode adds after its default agent's prompt as `Instructions from: <path>` and the file's text (measured on 2.0.32 and 2.1.4, the same way). An entry rather than a global `AGENTS.md` in OpenCode's configuration directory, because the entries of every configuration OpenCode reads are added together — an image's own global configuration keeps its instructions, where a written `AGENTS.md` would replace one an operator baked into the box — and because the run's configuration is rewritten on every run, so a reused box never carries a previous run's entry. Absolute, because a relative entry is matched by walking up from the checkout.

Both read the text from **a file** in the box's work directory (`<home>/.crewlet`), never from argv. OpenCode's `instructions` entry names `system.md`. A Claude Code run is started with one of two: its launch writes `system.md`, the instructions as they stand, and for a run wired to its turn's notes — every seat's executor run — `system-notes.md` beside it, the same text with what a note is after it; the run's script names `system-notes.md` on a release that reads notes and `system.md` on an older one ([Claude Code's hooks](#claude-codes-hooks)), and a run not wired to notes, Ask Crewlet's deep work, is started with `system.md`. The text carries a seat's org chart, policies and memory, and a command line is readable by every process that can list it — on a `direct` box, every account on the engine host — and bounded by `ARG_MAX`. Each file is written afresh for every run, at the mode a box writes every file with (`0600` on a local box) even where a previous run on a reused box left one wider, and outside the checkout, so a `git clean` cannot remove it and a commit cannot carry it into a repository. A run with no standing instructions writes no `system.md` and passes no flag for it; a runner always has at least the report instruction.

Neither text is recorded anywhere the engine reads back: the box is started with them once, and the run's record holds the task and the turn's parked state, which a resumed turn reads instead. The brief lives on only in the harness's own session, which is [kept](#each-launchs-session-kept) for a relaunch of the turn to continue. A run that continues a session is handed its standing instructions afresh, as every run is — Claude Code renders them anew on a resume from 2.1.267 (`--system-prompt-snapshot off`, [below](#a-claude-code-runs-session)), and an OpenCode session carried into a box reads them as its first ([An OpenCode run's session](#an-opencode-runs-session)) — so a continued run is told how to work by its own launch, never by an earlier one's.

### Claude Code

Runs headless — `claude -p --output-format stream-json --verbose --permission-mode bypassPermissions --settings <home>/.crewlet/claude-settings-notes-pinned.json --append-system-prompt-file <home>/.crewlet/system-notes.md` (the [settings every run has](#a-claude-code-runs-session) with the run's [hooks](#claude-codes-hooks), and its [standing instructions](#what-a-harness-is-told) told of notes, which every seat's executor run is started with on Claude Code 2.1.163 or later; on an older release, and for a run not wired to notes, `--settings <home>/.crewlet/claude-settings.json`, the settings every run has and nothing else — `claude-settings-skills.json`, the same with hooks switched on, where the run starts [its own skills](#a-runs-own-skills)' hook — and `--append-system-prompt-file <home>/.crewlet/system.md`; from 2.1.310 each of those settings files is handed as its pinned copy, its name ending `-pinned.json`, which adds [the compaction point's pin](#a-claude-code-runs-session)), with `--model` from the entry, `--max-turns` from the run's turn cap, `--mcp-config <file>` for the bridge — the engine's own tools and each of the seat's MCP servers — and the seat's in-box servers, beside every server its own configuration declares ([MCP inside the sandbox](#mcp-inside-the-sandbox); `--strict-mcp-config` only for Ask Crewlet's deep work, which is held to its bridge), `--plugin-dir <home>/.crewlet/claude-skills` for the seat's [own skills](#a-runs-own-skills) on Claude Code 2.1.118 or later, and the session it runs in — `--session-id <id>` for one it starts, `--resume <id>` for one it continues ([below](#a-claude-code-runs-session)) — the brief on its standard input — so it prints every message as it happens, one JSON object a line, into its own **event stream** (`stream.jsonl` in the box's work directory). That stream is what a person watching the run sees and what its transcript is built from; `--output-format json` printed one object when the run ended and nothing before it, and in print mode the harness writes nothing to stderr either, so a run on it showed nothing while it worked and left no transcript after. (`--verbose` is not optional: print mode refuses stream-json without it.) The stream's **last line is the result message**, which the wrapper copies to `result.json` after the harness exits — never through a pipe beside it, whose exit status would be the copy's rather than the harness's — and that line maps directly onto the result (final text, session id, token usage, cost; an error subtype such as `error_max_turns` and its `errors` are the failure). A stream that ends on anything else is a run that stopped before it said how it ended, and reads as one rather than as a silent success. A run whose wrapper never made the copy — its process group killed, by an OOM or a host restart, so the poll ended it on the wrapper's liveness — is read from the stream's own last line, which is what the copy would have held: the result, where the harness printed one before the wrapper died, and otherwise what it was saying when it stopped. It used to read as a run that "produced no output" beside a transcript of everything it did. A result file the box refuses (one that is [not a regular file](#what-ends-a-run-each-launch-is-published-as-its-own-record), say) is read from that line the same way, and the refusal is still said; a harness that exited before printing anything at all — not installed, a bad flag — leaves both empty, and reads as what it is.

**The bridge's own server is always loaded.** In the file `--mcp-config` names, the engine's `crewlet` server is marked `alwaysLoad`, which exempts it from Claude Code's tool search, so what the run reports and asks a person through is in front of its model on every request. Every other server — each of the seat's over the [bridge](#the-tool-bridge--a-seats-own-tools-from-inside-a-box), and those that run in the box — is left to the tool search, which keeps a server's tools out of a request until the model looks for them (on Claude Opus 4, Sonnet 4, Haiku 4.5 and later; an earlier model is sent every tool on every request).

**Every server the bridge serves a run carries the bridge's wait.** Its entry's `timeout`, in milliseconds, is the longest the bridge can take to answer a call ([The tool bridge](#the-tool-bridge--a-seats-own-tools-from-inside-a-box)): Claude Code's own tool timeout for the server, at which it gives a call up and cancels it whatever progress flows (measured on 2.1.310: `"timeout": 360000` cut a call the bridge kept going at 360 seconds with `notifications/cancelled`), and on 2.1.310 its HTTP transport's budget for a response's first byte too (measured: 360000 in place of 60000) and its check on a call that has sent nothing (by reading its bundle). A release whose configuration schema has no such key, as 2.1.118's does not, drops it and keeps its own clocks (by reading its bundle), which the bridge's progress still answers. A server that runs in the box carries none.

**The brief is read on stdin**, from `brief.md` in the box's work directory, never from argv — `-p` with no prompt reads its prompt there, verbatim (measured on 2.1.263 and 2.1.310). A brief carries every note the turn has read, so it grows; one argument is capped at 128 KiB; and a command line is readable by every account on a `direct` box's host, the reason the standing instructions are a file too. **The wrapper unsets `CLAUDE_CODE_SIMPLE` and `CLAUDE_CODE_SAFE_MODE`** before the harness starts: bare mode drops every hook (with `CLAUDE.md`, subagents and a plan's login) and safe mode drops hooks and the `--mcp-config` bridge, and either can arrive from the seat's `role.sandbox.env`, the image or a profile the login shell sources — only the wrapper, running after all three, reaches every one, so they are removed for every Claude Code run rather than refused in configuration. **A run a hook stopped is a failure.** Claude Code reports a loop that a hook of the repository's or the image's own settings stopped — one that exited 2 or returned a block — as a success subtype with an empty result and `terminal_reason: hook_stopped` (measured on 2.1.263 and 2.1.310); the runner reads it as a failed run saying so, never as a run that succeeded and said nothing.

The run's input is its **whole** prompt: the vendor's `input_tokens` counts only the uncached remainder, so the engine adds `cache_read_input_tokens` and `cache_creation_input_tokens` to it and carries both as the cache's share, the same way the engine counts a call on a Claude Code entry on its own host. Read alone, the uncached figure put a fraction of every launch's prompt on the budget counter.

**A run is charged what it spent, never its session's spend.** From 2.1.277 a run that continues a session (`--resume`) starts from the totals the job before it saved, so the result's `usage` and `total_cost_usd` are the **session's** — charged as they are, every continued run paid for the jobs before it again. From 2.1.310 every result also states, per model, what the calls this process made since its previous result cost (`model_usage_since_last_result`), which nothing another process counted is carried into, so a run is charged the sum of those figures over every result it printed (a person's note handed to the run as it finished carries its loop on to another result). A result whose figures name more than 64 models, where a process calls a handful and every result measured names one, or that writes them as anything but an object, null among it, is read for none of them, so the run is charged as on a release that states no such figures, never a sum that leaves some of its calls out. On a release before 2.1.310, a continued run keeps the result the job before it left in the box as it starts (`base-result.json`, beside the run's other artefacts), and is charged its result less that one where the release its script recorded (below) restores the totals; before 2.1.277, and for a run that started a session in place of one its box could not give it, the result is the run's own. Ask Crewlet's deep work, which continues its session the same way, is charged the same way.

The run's model and its sign-in come from the `providers.llm` entry the launch picks — the first member of the seat's executor chain (its `llm`, else the company's fallback entry) whose harness has a runner and is not [sitting out](llm-backends.md) — which for this runner is a Claude Code entry (`harness: claude-code`, or no `harness` at all). Its model goes to `--model` exactly as the entry's calls on the engine host pass it, an alias (`sonnet`) or an id, and the entry's `env` goes into the run environment, so an entry that reaches its model through a gateway (`ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`, and the `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN` it takes) or a cloud provider (`CLAUDE_CODE_USE_BEDROCK` and its region) runs its executor the same way ([Credentials and the run environment](#credentials-and-the-run-environment)). A seat may sign Claude Code in itself in `role.sandbox.env` — `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` (a Pro/Max plan), `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN`, or a `CLAUDE_CODE_USE_BEDROCK` / `CLAUDE_CODE_USE_VERTEX` / `CLAUDE_CODE_USE_FOUNDRY` toggle — and a variable it names there wins over the entry's under the same name.

**One entry, one runner.** An entry's model is written in the grammar of the harness it drives and its sign-in is that harness's, so a run on one is that harness's own run: a `claude-code` entry runs under Claude Code, an `opencode` entry under OpenCode. There is no runner setting beside the entry to disagree with it — the runner *is* the entry's harness. The model is handed to the run exactly as the entry's calls on the engine host hand it to the harness. A harness with no runner at all (`codex`, `gemini-cli`, `kimi-code`, `hermes`, `pi`, …) cannot be an executor: a launch passes over it in the seat's chain, a seat whose chain has no member that can run is [refused on the write](#failure-modes), and a `run_in` on such an entry is refused as a placement nothing would read. It still serves the seat's reviewer, workers and side passes (`llm_review`, `llm_subagent`, `llm_auxiliary`, `llm_judge`). When the run launches, the entry's environment is exported into the box: its whole `env` — a gateway's address and the token it takes, a cloud provider's switch and its region, a proxy, and, for `opencode`, which reads its model provider's key from its own environment, [that key](llm-backends.md#6-a-provider-key-in-env-opencode-hermes-pi) — and what signs that harness in: the headless subscription token (`CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN`, minted by `crewlet llm login <key> -capture-token`, so Claude Code there bills your Pro/Max plan — no API key anywhere), the key of an `auth.mode: api-key` entry, the variables `inherit-env` forwards. This works on **every** backend including remote E2B, because each is a variable, and a credential among them one scoped, revocable variable. The login itself — Claude Code's credential files, OpenCode's exported login — deliberately never leaves the engine host for a remote box: it carries a refresh token whose rotation is shared fleet state. So a `claude-code` entry with no headless token and no API key (nor a gateway's token or a cloud provider's switch in its `env`), or an `opencode` entry signed in only by `crewlet llm login`, needs a [local cell](#local-sandboxes) — `run_in: direct` or `run_in: container` — which is seeded with the login and writes a refreshed one back.

#### A Claude Code run's session

**Where the session is kept is pinned.** The wrapper exports, after it unsets the two variables above and for the same reason — over whatever the seat's `role.sandbox.env`, the image or a profile the login shell sources set — `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` as the box home's `.claude` (the default location, named explicitly; the login stays where a local box is seeded with it and writes it back from, `.claude/.credentials.json`), `CLAUDE_CODE_PROJECT_DIR_NAME=crewlet`, so a session's transcript is `.claude/projects/crewlet/<session>.jsonl` whatever the box's working directory (a `direct` box's holds its own id; from 2.1.234, which reads the name), `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_AUTO_MEMORY=1`, so no notes the harness wrote for itself ride a session into a later run unseen — the rule a call on the engine host keeps too ([LLM Backends](llm-backends.md#the-executors-run-in-a-box)) — and `CLAUDE_CODE_AUTO_COMPACT_WINDOW` as the context window of the model the run works under ([What a continued launch costs](#what-a-continued-launch-costs)), whatever window the seat or the image set — and it unsets `DISABLE_AUTO_COMPACT` and `DISABLE_COMPACT` beside the two above, either of which would switch compaction off, and `CLAUDE_AUTOCOMPACT_PCT_OVERRIDE` and `CLAUDE_CODE_BLOCKING_LIMIT_OVERRIDE`, either of which would move the point it compacts at. A repository's own Claude Code settings could set any of these again once the harness has started, since Claude Code applies a settings file's `env` block to its own environment; so from 2.1.310 the run is started with its settings file's pinned copy, written beside it with its name ending `-pinned.json`, whose `env` block sets `CLAUDE_CODE_AUTO_COMPACT_WINDOW` to the same window and both overrides to nothing, and Claude Code applies it after the repository's. A managed settings file in the image or on the host still wins over it, as its operator's own policy. An agent definition's own `autoCompactWindow` (a repository's `.claude/agents/<name>.md`, for one) can lower the window of the subagent it runs, from 2.1.312, and never raises it; nothing caps the main conversation's. A repository can still switch compaction off (`autoCompactEnabled: false`, or `DISABLE_AUTO_COMPACT` or `DISABLE_COMPACT` in its settings' `env`), and that run's requests then grow to its model's own window ([What a continued launch costs](#what-a-continued-launch-costs)). Set, `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` also moves the harness's own state file, `.claude.json`, from the home into `.claude/`, so an image's own `~/.claude.json` is no longer read. The run's script turns `CLAUDE_CODE_TRANSCRIPT_LOCAL_GC` on from 2.1.304 — a `-p` run then trims a transcript of 5 MiB or more that holds a compaction, after each compaction and once 20 MiB has been appended since its last trim, keeping only the history from its last compaction on, which a resume restores either way: it writes that to a new file and renames it over the transcript — and unsets it on an older release, whose trim could lose messages.

**Every run names its session before the harness starts.** A run that starts one is started with `--session-id` and a UUID the runner minted, which the launch records, so a run killed before its result line still names its session. A run that continues one — a relaunch of its turn ([below](#a-relaunch-continues-the-turns-session)), the assistant's deep work after an approval — is started with `--resume <id>`, never with both (the harness refuses `--session-id` beside `--resume` unless the session is forked), and **with `--system-prompt-snapshot off`** from 2.1.267: by default a conversation records the system prompt its first request rendered — the first launch's standing instructions, their prefetch blocks and the box's report path among them — and every later request and resume sends that record until the conversation is compacted, so a resumed run would otherwise be told what an earlier launch's instructions said. 2.1.263 refuses the option, so the script passes it only from 2.1.267. The script continues the copy of the session the box **holds** — its transcript under any project directory of `.claude/projects` — unless an archive of it was carried in that the copy does not hold (below); where the box holds none it restores the session carried in from another box; and where it can do neither it starts the session the runner minted in its place, saying why on the run's error stream and in what the run reports back — asked to resume a session it does not hold, the harness would fail the run — and hands the harness the **fresh brief** the run carried beside its continuation, so a new session never answers a brief written for one that remembers. **A resume leaves the session as it stands.** The script unsets `CLAUDE_CODE_RESUME_INTERRUPTED_TURN` on every resume, whatever the seat's environment set: set, a resume runs the turn its last process was cut off in again before it reads the brief, repeating a call that may already have run, which the brief tells the run to check instead. A tool call the previous process died in is kept: 2.1.310's resume records an error result for it, saying its outcome is unknown and to check whether it took effect, and then "No response requested.", before it reads the brief. And a transcript whose last line its harness was killed while writing, or that a pack read while its harness still wrote it — a line with no newline, which no reader of a JSON-lines transcript can use, and which the next record would be written onto, losing that record too — is cut back to its last whole record before the harness resumes it: **the session's transcript and every subagent's thread under its sidecar directory**, at any depth, since 2.1.310's resume takes a thread cut back so (measured: a subagent continued through its last whole call) and loses the record it next writes to one left torn. A file with no whole line at all is left as it is — the session's own transcript, and a subagent's thread a pack read as its subagent wrote its first record, which 2.1.310 continues from nothing a restore could make of it (measured: a fragment, an emptied file and a removed one alike are answered that no transcript was found for the agent, and the run goes on), so no record is ever written onto that fragment — and a box without `find` cuts no thread and says so on the run's error stream. Before the harness starts, the script records the session it took up, whether that is the one it was asked to continue, why not where it was asked and could not, and the harness's `--version` (`session`, in the box's work directory), which the collection reports as what the run did.

**The session is packed when the harness exits.** After the harness exits, whatever its status, and before the done marker, the script packs the session it ran into one gzipped tar, written in a staging directory and moved over `session-capture` in the box's work directory whole: the transcript, its sidecar directory (`<session>/`: the subagents' transcripts and the tool results spilled to files) and its task list (`.claude/tasks/<session>/`), each named relative to `.claude`. **Never** the login (`.credentials.json`) or the harness's own state (`.claude.json`) beside them, never `file-history/` — the file-edit snapshots of the files a run edited, which carry the checkout rather than the conversation and are off — and nothing else under `.claude`. A box without `tar` and `gzip` packs nothing, and its error stream says so; a harness that wrote no transcript has nothing to pack. The run's status is always the harness's own. The collection is told where the archive is, which session it holds, the release that wrote it and its size, and the runner streams it out of the box; the clear before the next run removes it. The box keeps the archive's digest (below).

**A run ends on request, and packs as its own end does.** Before it does anything else, the run's script traps `SIGUSR1`, and only then records its own pid in the box's work directory (`job-script`, as `1 <pid>`), so a request sent through that file always meets the script's handler. The harness runs in the background, waited for, since a shell runs no handler while it waits on a command in the foreground. The read that ends a stopped or lost run, or one its turn's wall-clock cap passed while it still worked ([Each launch's session, kept](#each-launchs-session-kept)), sends `SIGUSR1` to that pid alone — on a `direct` box, only while it is a living process of the job's own recorded group, since the job runs as the engine's user and could write any of that user's pids there — and the script tells the harness to stop (`SIGTERM`, then `SIGKILL` five seconds on), packs the session as above, records that the job was ended and how (`job-ended`: `asked`, `killed` past its grace, or `before` its harness started — a request that comes while the script is still setting the run up starts no harness), and exits with the harness's own status, which the wrapper writes beside the done marker as on every exit. A run ended so never reads as a success, and its failure says first that it was ended. A second request once the end is under way is ignored, so it cuts neither the wait for the harness nor the pack, and a pack killed part way leaves only its staging directory, never part of an archive where the session is read. `SIGTERM` to the job's group is still a teardown's, which the script does not trap: it goes with its harness, as it always did.

**A session packed in another box is restored before the harness resumes it.** A run handed one has the archive written into its box as it starts — after the previous run's artefacts are cleared, streamed in rather than held whole — and its script restores it: unpacked in a staging directory, and only the transcript (a regular file), the sidecar directory and the task list (directories, never links) moved under `.claude/projects/crewlet` and `.claude/tasks`, the transcript last. **A box that already holds the session is compared by id and by the archive's digest.** It keeps the POSIX `cksum` of every archive of a session it packed or restored, for as long as it lives (`session-digests`, in its work directory, which the clear before a run leaves) — **a pack reports its archive made only once that digest is recorded**: written before the archive is moved to where it is read, and read back, so a pack whose digest the box cannot keep (its disk full, a box without `cksum` or `grep`) fails and moves nothing, where it once moved its archive, reported it made and then appended the digest, a write nothing checked; **and a restore is continued only once its digest is recorded**, written and read back once the archive is restored, so a run whose box cannot keep the line starts a session of its own and says why, where it once continued the restored copy with no line of its archive, which carried back in later was restored over the copy that went on past it — and its own copy is never written over with an archive it already holds: that copy is the archive's or newer, since a run went on in the box after it whose own capture may have failed. **Except a session its harness was cut off in**: an archive the engine packed rather than the script at its harness's clean exit replaces the copy whatever the copy holds, since the copy may end in a record its harness never finished, and the run that continues it is told what came after the archive ([below](#a-relaunch-continues-the-turns-session)). An archive its copy does not hold comes from a box the session went on in after this one's copy, and replaces it — the box's transcript, sidecars and task list removed once the archive is known to hold a transcript. A box that cannot take a digest keeps its copy, and continues nothing it restored where it holds none. A box continues a carried session only where its Claude Code is **2.1.310 or later** — the first whose results state each run's own spend, and above every release a carried session needs (2.1.304's trim, 2.1.267's snapshot option, 2.1.234's project directory, and the resume fixes between) — the session was written by **2.1.300 or later**, from which a compaction's summary survives a resume, and the box's release is not older than the one that wrote it; and only on the harness that wrote it, in the archive format the runner restores. The runner answers that before a run is started in a box, by reading the box's `claude --version` with one login-shell exec, its bin directory first on its PATH as the wrapper puts it; the script holds a restore to the box's floor too.

**The box's own retention sweep is held off.** Claude Code sweeps its configuration directory about five seconds into every run, print mode included, deleting what is older than its `cleanupPeriodDays` setting (thirty days by default) by each file's own modification time: a transcript past it with its sidecar directory, a living transcript's spilled tool results one by one, and a task list whole. A session restored into a box keeps the modification times it was packed with, and a session is as old as its conversation, which the engine continues for as long as its turns come within [`retention_days`](conversation-sessions.md#configuration) of each other. So at the default a continued conversation lost its oldest tool results and its task list to its own box, five seconds into a turn. Every Claude Code run is therefore started with a settings file of its own, `claude-settings.json` in the box's work directory, written afresh at `0600` and named with `--settings` on every release (from 2.1.310 as its pinned copy, `claude-settings-pinned.json`, which adds [the compaction point's pin](#a-claude-code-runs-session)): it holds `cleanupPeriodDays: 36500` and nothing else, and a run that starts a hook of the engine's carries the same setting in the file that switches hooks on for it — its [skills](#a-runs-own-skills)' or its [notes'](#claude-codes-hooks). The value is a hundred years, because nothing the engine keeps bounds how old a session can get, and the harness takes no "never": `0` is refused, and in print mode a settings file the harness cannot validate is ignored without a word. Its cutoff falls in 1926, before any file a box holds, and a real date, where a cutoff past the date range made releases before 2.1.310 delete recent files. The box is disposable, and the session is the engine's to keep and to delete ([Each launch's session, kept](#each-launchs-session-kept)). Measured on 2.1.310: a resumed session's tool result and task list forty days old, deleted five seconds into the run without the setting and kept with it.

### OpenCode

Drives **OpenCode 2** — the `@opencode/cli` package, whose `opencode --version` prints `opencode v2.…`. 1.x (npm's `opencode-ai`) cannot run it, and a box that has 1.x is refused by name before anything runs (`this box runs OpenCode 1.18.35; the opencode runner drives OpenCode 2 …`) rather than failing on 1.x's usage text. A box with no `opencode` on its PATH says so and exits 127; one whose `opencode` is there but fails `opencode --version` (it can't be executed, or crashes) says that instead, with the exit status and what it printed, which is the run's transcript when nothing else was written. Install 2 in your box image; E2B does not say which release its prebuilt `opencode` template carries, so a template that refuses this way needs `template:` pointed at one built with `@opencode/cli`. OpenCode is **provider-agnostic**: an `opencode` entry names `<provider>/<model>` for any provider OpenCode speaks to, a local server included, so a seat on one runs its executor on the same entry, the same model and the same key — no extra secret. 

**A run is a short script around a server of its own**, because 2.0 cannot be driven correctly in one command. `opencode run` alone attaches to a *background service* — detached from the run, on a port compiled into the binary that every box on a host shares, outliving the run and keeping the environment (so the credentials) of whichever run started it. With `--standalone` it starts a private server inside the run instead, and sends the brief at once — before the run's MCP servers have connected, and 2.0 fixes a step's tools as the step starts, so measured on 2.0.32 the first step of every run with a server declared was offered none of the seat's bridged tools. So the run:

1. refuses a 1.x box, imports the box's login if it was given one (below), and settles the session the run works in ([below](#an-opencode-runs-session));
2. starts `opencode serve` on a loopback port of its own and waits — at most 60 s — until it has printed both where it listens and the password it minted, naming whichever of the two it never printed. The server's password is always its own: `opencode serve` takes one from `OPENCODE_PASSWORD` or `OPENCODE_SERVER_PASSWORD` and then prints none, so the run unsets both before anything starts — a box environment carrying either (a seat's `role.sandbox.env`, an image built for OpenCode) used to wait out the whole minute and fail saying the server never listened — and hands the minted one to its own client calls as `OPENCODE_PASSWORD`, the variable OpenCode's client reads first. The server is started without it, so nothing the agent runs inherits it;
3. asks it (`opencode api mcp.list`) until every MCP server the run declares has left `pending`, for at most 60 s, twice the 30 s OpenCode gives one server to list its tools — past that it starts without the straggler and says so on stderr. Each server is looked for by its name exactly as OpenCode's answer spells it, `&`, `<`, `>`, U+2028 and U+2029 literal, so a server named `R&D tools` is seen the moment it settles. OpenCode names a server's tool `<server>_<tool>`, writing every character but letters, digits, `_` and `-` as `_`, and keeps one tool per name, so a server the image's or the repository's own OpenCode configuration declares as `crewlet_x` or `crewlet.x` would take the names of the engine's own tools (its `y` is `crewlet_x_y`, as the engine's `x_y` is). A run whose server lists one ends here, before the brief is sent, naming the server to rename; `crewlet-x` and `crewletx` keep names of their own;
4. runs the brief against that server, **on stdin** — OpenCode re-quotes an argv prompt, so a brief passed as an argument reached the model wrapped in quotes with every quote inside it escaped — under a **session it names**, with a title, which stops 2.0 making a second billed model call to write one;
5. prints the session's usage report as the stream's last line, exports the session to be carried to another box, writes the box's refreshed login back, and stops the server. The run's exit status stays the harness's own.

**A run ends on request as a Claude Code run does** ([above](#a-claude-code-runs-session)): its script traps `SIGUSR1` before it records its pid, and runs `opencode run` in the background. On the request it first **interrupts its session through its own server** (`opencode api session.interrupt`), which settles the step in flight — the tool call it was running marked interrupted, the step finished — so the export step 5 makes holds it, and then ends the run's client within the same five seconds, and goes on to step 5 as usual, the session and the login exported through the server before it stops. Measured on 2.0.32 and 2.1.4: the interrupt answers in a tenth of a second and the client exits by itself within a fifth; a client ended alone left the server working the step, and the session's export, at once and five seconds on, held nothing of it (2.x answers `session.abort`, the name the first release's API used, with "Operation not found"). A request that comes before the server listens starts no server and no run, and one while the run waits for its MCP servers starts no run.

The script is plain POSIX `sh`, written for every `sh` a box image is likely to have — dash (Debian, Ubuntu), bash in its POSIX mode (Fedora, RHEL, Arch) and busybox ash (Alpine, which OpenCode 2's musl builds are for).

The run's `--model` is the `opencode` entry's model exactly as written — the same `<provider>/<model>` (`anthropic/claude-sonnet-5`, `openrouter/anthropic/claude-sonnet-5`) every call on that entry on the engine host passes, which OpenCode splits at its first slash into one of its own providers and a model — signed in by that provider's own key from the entry's `env` or the seat's `role.sandbox.env`, or by the entry's login in a local cell.

**A login follows a run into a local cell as OpenCode's own export.** OpenCode 2 keeps its logins in the database it keeps its sessions in, so the entry's login travels as the document `opencode auth export` printed ([LLM Backends](llm-backends.md#1-broker-the-vendors-own-login-any-harness)): a `direct` or `container` box is seeded with it at `~/.crewlet/exported-credentials`, and the run imports it before its server starts. A token OpenCode refreshes mid-run then lives only in that database, and every teardown of a run still in flight — a seat retired mid-run, a launch settled as failed after its job started, the pause reaper — kills the job's group outright (`SIGKILL`, or `docker rm -f` for a `container` box), so nothing in the box runs at its end; only a person's stop, a lost run's ending and the collection of a run its turn's cap passed while it still worked ask the script to end the run first (above). So the run **exports the database back over the document while it works**, through its own server every 30 s, as well as once more when it ends; a `SIGTERM`, `SIGINT` or `SIGHUP` to the run — a teardown's, never an end request's — ends its client, exports once more before it goes, exiting 143, 130 or 129, under an engine started with nohup too, since `crewlet run` doesn't hand a hangup it ignores on to the boxes it starts ([Force stop](agent-runtime.md#graceful-shutdown)). Thirty seconds is the window a kill can still lose a refresh in: an OAuth access token lives an hour or more, so under one refresh in a hundred falls inside it, and an export through the server costs about 0.14 s (measured on 2.0.32). An export replaces the document only where it differs and holds a login — a whole, non-empty JSON array, never the `[]` a database that dropped its credential prints — and nothing is exported where the box was given none. The box's teardown then writes the document back to the shared login under the rule every local box's login follows ([Local sandboxes](#local-sandboxes)). A remote box is never given it.

The runner **writes OpenCode 2's own configuration to `<home>/.crewlet/opencode.json`**, beside the run's other artefacts and never in the checkout, and the run's script hands its content to the run's server alone as **`OPENCODE_CONFIG_CONTENT`**. OpenCode takes no config flag, and of its two variables for a process's own configuration, `OPENCODE_CONFIG` (a file's path) ranks *below* a repository's `opencode.json` and `.opencode/`, while `OPENCODE_CONFIG_CONTENT` ranks above them: 2.x reads managed policies, well-known, global, `OPENCODE_CONFIG`, the project's `opencode.json`, its `.opencode/`, `OPENCODE_CONFIG_CONTENT` and managed settings in that order, a later document winning (the same at 2.0.0, 2.0.32 and 2.1.4). So **a repository's own `opencode.json` is not written to**: it stays as it is and loads beneath the run's configuration, the way Claude Code loads a repository's `.claude/settings.json` beneath `--settings`. Measured on 2.0.32 and 2.1.4 with a repository whose own `opencode.json` and `.opencode/opencode.json` disable the bridge and deny every permission: the bridge connected and the shell ran. Every server the run declares, and the agent its turn cap is set on, states `disabled: false`, so a document beneath the run's cannot switch one off under the same name. The login's import and export and the run's own client calls are handed none of it, and a run whose configuration is missing is refused before a login is imported or a server started. For a run wired to its turn's notes, the same file names the run's [notes plugin](#opencodes-notes-plugin) and the server alone is handed the run's session as `CREWLET_STEER_SESSION`.

It declares **no provider**: the model names one of OpenCode's own, which reads its key from the run environment under that provider's own variable, so a credential is never written into the file where the agent could read it back into its report, and a provider declared beside the one the model names would shadow it. The same file sets `permissions` to allow every action and deny only `question` (headless runs have no human to approve a prompt — a gate left at `ask` is auto-rejected — and the one tool that asks a person is replaced by `ask_and_wait` on the run's bridge; the rules of every document OpenCode reads are added together in its order, so these come last and, the last match winning, decide), `share: disabled`, `update: disable` (a harness that updates itself mid-run swaps the tool under the brief while the seat's turn is suspended waiting on it) and `snapshots: false` (a git snapshot of the checkout at every step, for an undo a headless run never offers). Each MCP server is declared under `mcp.servers`, a remote one with **`oauth: false`**, since the bridge authenticates with the run's token and a headless run cannot complete an OAuth flow, and each of the bridge's with **`timeout.execution`**, the longest the bridge can take to answer a call ([The tool bridge](#the-tool-bridge--a-seats-own-tools-from-inside-a-box)), at which OpenCode gives a call up and cancels it whatever progress flows (measured on 2.1.4: 120000 cut a 150-second call at 120 seconds with `notifications/cancelled`, with progress and without); unset, OpenCode waits twelve hours for a call (2.0.32 and 2.1.4, by reading their bundles), and it keeps no other clock on one (measured on 2.1.4: a call silent for 330 seconds was answered whole). **The seat's MCP servers are left to Code Mode**, OpenCode 2's own default and its way of keeping a large tool list out of each request: its model is handed one `execute` tool, a catalogue of about two thousand tokens and a `search()` over the rest, and calls a server's tools from the short JavaScript it writes — each one still its own MCP call, which the [bridge](#the-tool-bridge--a-seats-own-tools-from-inside-a-box) decides and records for a server it serves. **The engine's own server, `crewlet`, is declared `codemode: false`**, so what a run reports through (`submit_work`) and asks a person through (`ask_and_wait`) is a direct call `crewlet_<tool>` on every model, whether or not it takes to writing the script. A server's calls through Code Mode read as `execute` in the run's own transcript; the engine's record of the run names each call.

**A server's values are referenced, not written into the configuration.** Its `url`, each header value and each `environment` value is a `{file:<path>}` reference to a file of its own under `<home>/.crewlet/opencode-values/`, which OpenCode reads in the reference's place — the file's content with the whitespace at either end trimmed, so an `environment` value that begins or ends with whitespace arrives without it (a URL or a header value has none that counts) — so the configuration and the variable carrying it hold paths and no value. The files are written before the configuration that names them, at the mode a box writes every file with (`0600` on a local box). OpenCode replaces every `{env:…}` and `{file:…}` in the configuration's text before it parses it, and the configuration names the instruction file, the notes plugin and every value under the box's home, so a run on a box whose home that text cannot name — a relative one, or one holding a brace, a quote, a backslash or a control character — is refused, saying so and naming `providers.sandbox.local.state_dir`, which places a local box's home.

Its `instructions` names the file holding the run's [standing instructions](#what-a-harness-is-told), by its absolute path, and its `skills` the directory of the seat's [own skills](#a-runs-own-skills), whose loads a plugin of the run's own reports. The configuration is written on **every** run, including one that names no model and no server, and the previous run's configuration and values are removed before it is: a run is configured by its own file or not started at all, never by the vendor's defaults or by a file an earlier run on a reused box left, MCP block and dead per-run bridge URL included.

**Its answer is its last message's text.** Each step of a run is a message of its own, and `run --format json` prints every step's text, so the result is the text of the last message that wrote any — a run handed a [note](#opencodes-notes-plugin) after it had answered answers again, and its result is the later answer, not the two joined — while the transcript keeps every step's. The report the run writes is what the result prefers anyway.

**Its tokens are what its session gained during the run.** `run --format json` drops the final step's counts, so the run asks its session (`opencode api session.get`) once it ends and the collection reads that: the whole prompt, the cache's two shares included, and the output with the reasoning OpenCode reports beside it added back, as every provider bills it — plus OpenCode's price, as it reported it (zero where it has no pricing for the provider, which the record reads as no price). The report is the session's lifetime totals, so a run that continues a session asks for it before the run as well — after an import, so the totals the import restored are taken off too — and is charged the difference: charged the report as it is, every continued run paid for every step its session ever ran. A run whose report is missing reports zero rather than an estimate.

#### An OpenCode run's session

**The session is settled before the run's server starts**, because the server is handed it: a run wired to its notes names it to the notes plugin. A run that starts a session works in a `ses_` id the runner minted. A run that continues one continues the copy the box's database **holds** — the session answers `opencode api session.get` under its id, as it does in the box its last run ran in — compared, where an export of the session was carried in, by the export's digest as Claude Code's copy is: an export the box's copy holds (one the box exported or imported) is never imported over it, and one it does not hold replaces it — and so does one taken of a session its run was cut off in, whatever the copy holds, since the copy may hold a step that never settled, which an export leaves out. Where the box holds none, or a copy the carried export replaces, the export (below) is **imported**: unpacked in a staging directory, the box's own copy deleted (`opencode session delete`) only once it unpacked, and handed to `opencode session import --directory "$PWD"`, which files it under the directory `opencode run` works in, keeps its id and restores its totals. All three are asked of a private server of their own (`--standalone`), as the login's import is — never the background service a bare command reaches. The import has to say it imported that session (`Imported session: <id>`): OpenCode answers an id its database already holds with "Session already exists" and success, and `run --session` on an id it does not hold creates an empty session under it, which the brief would then be answered in as if it remembered. Where the session is neither held nor imported, the run starts the session the runner minted in its place, says why on its error stream and in what it reports back, and points `opencode run` at the **fresh brief** the run carried beside its continuation. As Claude Code's does, the script records the session the run took up, whether it continued the one asked, why not where it could not, and the harness's `--version` before the run.

**The session is exported once the run ends** — after the steers the run left are cancelled, before its server stops — with `opencode session export` through the run's own server, gzipped, and moved over `session-capture` whole, its digest kept by the box. **Never the database**: OpenCode keeps its logins in the same file as its sessions, and a file carried to another box would carry the login with it, to a remote box too. The export is the session and its settled messages. Only the run's own session is carried, never the child sessions its tasks ran in: each task's result is in the parent's conversation, which the export carries, and a child's import needs its parent first. A box without `gzip` exports nothing, and says so. A box continues a carried session only where its OpenCode is **2.0.32 or later**, the session was written by 2.0.32 or later, and the box's release is not older than the one that wrote it.

**A carried session's instructions are the run's own.** OpenCode keeps, per session, the instructions it last told the model: the system prompt is rendered from the ones it held when the session's instructions were last settled, and a session whose instruction file changes is handed the change as an "instruction update" message in the conversation instead. That state is a row of its own, and a session with none reads its instructions as its first, into the system prompt with no update (OpenCode's `session/instruction-state.ts` at 2.0.32 and 2.2.5). An import writes the session and its messages and no such row (`session/transfer.ts` at the same two), so the first request in the box a session was carried into reads its instructions as the session's first: the run's standing instructions, written to its instruction file on every run as on a fresh one, are its system prompt, and no earlier turn's are in the conversation as updates. In the box that already holds the session the state is kept, and an instruction file that changed between two of its runs is handed over once, as OpenCode hands over any change to a running session's instructions.

### What ends a run; each launch is published as its own record

A run is **never stopped by its box's TTL**. The waiter refreshes the box's TTL every poll tick (keepalive), so a legitimately long run is free to finish; the TTL only reclaims a box the engine has stopped heart-beating for. A run ends when it finishes, and what else can end it is yours to set: a **round cap** — `role.sandbox.max_turns`, else `providers.sandbox.default_max_turns`, uncapped unless one of them is set — handed to both runners as their own turn cap (Claude Code's `--max-turns`, OpenCode's agent `steps`); and a turn whose trigger carries a wall-clock cap (a schedule's `timeout_seconds`) ends its run at that deadline, the collection ending its job as a stop does and recording what it did up to then. A person's stop ends it at once ([Pausing a seat](agent-runtime.md#pausing-a-seat)), by asking its script to end it: the harness told to stop, its session packed as its own end packs it, and the job killed where it will not go ([A Claude Code run's session](#a-claude-code-runs-session)). Completion is detected by tracking the job itself — see [Durability and completion](#durability-completion-and-observability).

Because its standing instructions tell the harness that it is *not* the last step, it writes a structured **findings report** to a known file before stopping; `collect` prefers that file for the result text (it survives a run whose process group died before it printed its final message, and a tool-only run that streamed no final message). **The report names what the run delivered**, a line of its own for each branch it pushed and each pull or merge request it opened — `Delivered: fix/retry-backoff`, `Delivered: https://git.example.com/acme/api/pull/42` — and those lines are the run's **delivered refs**, in the report's order: a branch as well as a pull request, on any host and any scheme. The engine keeps no list of code hosts to match against, because which hosts a box can push to is set by the seat's own [environment and setup steps](#credentials-and-the-run-environment), which it deliberately never names, and a seat can push to a host no integration block mentions. A line counts when what follows the label is a URL carrying no credentials, or a name git accepts for a branch (valid UTF-8 and at most 244 bytes, as a parked run's branch is — [below](#mid-run-clarification-ask_and_wait)), alone on the line — a list marker, emphasis or backticks around it are fine, and `Delivered: the fix for the flake` is a sentence, not a ref. A report that names none is read for pull and merge request URLs by their **shape**, on any host and port (`/pull/N`, `/pulls/N`, `/-/merge_requests/N`, `/pull-requests/N`, `/pullrequest/N`) — a guess, since a pull request the run only read looks exactly like one it opened, which is why a named ref always wins over a scraped one, and the report's over the harness's final message. The refs ride the run's phase record and the collected executor's record, which the reviewer reads under `## What the agent delivered`; nothing else judges the turn on them. Both are JSON, where a byte that is not UTF-8 is replaced, so a URL — named or scraped — is listed only if it is valid UTF-8, for the branch's reason: listed, it would arrive as a URL the run never delivered. It is counted instead, with the refs past the record's bound (*the delivered refs*, below). The runner also reconstructs an **activity transcript** from each harness's event stream — OpenCode's line-flushed `--format json` events, Claude Code's `stream-json` messages — in one shape: what the agent said, one line per tool call naming the tool and the first line of its subject (`[tool] Bash: go test ./...`, `[tool] Read: <file>`), a failed call marked with the first line of its error, and how a run that did not succeed ended. **Never a tool's output**: a successful result's body is a whole file read or everything a command printed, and it is what the transcript summarises. Nor the model's thinking, nor a message type the build does not know (a harness release adds them). The error stream is the transcript only where the event stream says nothing — a harness that failed before it could stream says why there.

**Each collected launch is a record of its own.** When the completion is claimed and the result read back, the coordinator publishes one `executor_collected` — the launch's own record, beside the executor's. It is **no phase**: the executor's phase is the round's own `agent_phase_completed` with `phase: execute`, which states the launch and its answer as one round and carries no tokens, and a launch is that round's work — a round can take more than one, when a person's answer carries a parked run on in a second launch, or a launch lost with its host is relaunched. Every spend reader files a launch under `execute`, its calls its harness's own turns:

| Field | What it holds |
|---|---|
| `turn_id`, `iteration`, `launch_id` | The identity. `iteration` is the executor round that launched it, written onto the run's record beside the turn's parked state; a round can launch twice (a run carried on with a person's answer is the same round), so `launch_id` tells the launches apart |
| `started_at`, `duration_ms` | From the launch, dated by the store when it named the job, to the collection on the node that read the result — so the completion poll's interval is inside it |
| `input_tokens`, `output_tokens`, `total_tokens`, `cache_read_tokens`, `cache_write_tokens` | What the run spent, as its harness reported it (for OpenCode, its session's totals — see above) |
| `cost_usd` | What the launch's harness said it billed, where it says so. On this record only — it is the launch's fact, and the collected executor's record is one a run that parked on a question does not get until it is answered |
| `provider_key`, `model`, `harness`, `sandbox_id` | The `providers.llm` entry the launch picked, the model it pointed the agent at (empty when it named none), the harness and the box |
| `harness_turns` | The harness's own count of the turns the launch took, where it reports one (Claude Code's `num_turns`; OpenCode reports none) — what every spend figure's `calls` counts the launch as, one where it reports none |
| `response`, `delivered_refs`, `activity_transcript` | The findings report, what it delivered, and the transcript |
| `delivered_refs_elided`, `activity_transcript_elided_lines`, `activity_transcript_elided_bytes` | What the record's bounds left out: refs it does not list (past its bound, or not UTF-8), and the lines and bytes of the transcript's middle. Absent when nothing was left out |
| `pushed_refs`, `pushed_refs_elided`, `pushed_refs_unread` | What the run's push record says it moved on Gitlet, for a run given a repository there to push to — each ref `{repo, ref, old, new, pushes, change}` as it ended, with the change its branch heads, how many more the record names than are listed, and why the record could not be read where it could not ([What a run pushed is reported for it](#what-a-run-pushed-is-reported-for-it)). On the collected executor's record too |
| `failed`, `error`, `error_kind`, `notes` | `failed` and `error` are set when the launch did not succeed, with the kind `context_exhausted` where its harness said the conversation no longer fits its model's context, `out_of_memory` where the kernel killed its harness for memory, and `launch_failed` for every other cause: a failure of its turn, as a failed phase's is. A launch that stopped to ask a question, one a person stopped and one its host cut off are **not** failed; their `notes` say which |
| `memory_kills`, `memory_bytes` | How many processes the kernel killed for memory in the job's memory domain while its harness ran, and that domain's ceiling (the smaller of its cgroup's limit and the machine's), on any ending; absent where it killed none. The job's script reads the domain (its cgroup, else the machine where the box is a machine of its own) before the harness starts and after it exits, so a kill is counted for this job alone and never guessed from an exit status: a harness ended by SIGKILL with no kill counted is not `out_of_memory`, and a run that survived a killed command still says the kill. A `direct` box shares the engine host's domain, so its kills are recorded and never make its run `out_of_memory` |
| `work_item`, `work_key`, `conversation_key` | As on the turn's phase records |
| `harness_session`, `harness_version` | The harness's own session the launch **ran** — as its script recorded it, so a launch asked to continue a session its box could not is named by the new one it started — and the harness's release, as its `--version` gave it. Each is absent where the box named none of the shape a harness gives it: an id of letters, digits, `-` and `_`, at most 128 of them, and a release of at most 64 bytes |
| `brief`, `harness_session_continued`, `harness_session_fell_back` | Which brief the launch handed its run — `continued` (the turn's session, in the box it was last worked in), `continued_new_box` (the turn's session, restored into another box), `continued_conversation` (the session the turn's conversation's last turn left, restored into a box of its own; [A conversation's next turn continues its last turn's session](#a-conversations-next-turn-continues-its-last-turns-session)) or `fresh` (a session of its own) — whether the run did take up the session it was asked to continue, and, where it did not, why its script started one of its own instead, handing it the fresh brief ([A relaunch continues the turn's session](#a-relaunch-continues-the-turns-session)) — one sentence, redacted and cut to **1 KiB**, marked `…`. `brief` is absent where the launch recorded none |
| `session_taken_bytes`, `session_not_kept` | What the read that collected the launch [took of its session](#each-launchs-session-kept): the size of the sealed session taken out of the box to be kept, or why none was taken, redacted and cut to **1 KiB**, marked `…`. Whether it then landed is said after the read, by `executor_session_kept` or `executor_session_not_kept` in the log and on the run's record. Both absent where nothing was taken — a harness that carries no session, or `turn_engine.conversation_session.enabled` off |

It is published **once per launch**, however often the tail is retried: a resume that fails hands the completion back and the retry collects the same job again, so the run's coordination record says the launch's record already went out, in the same write that hands the claim back — exactly as the [charge](#budgets) is recorded.
