A publish the broker refused is not recorded and is offered again by a retry, if one comes; telemetry never fails the resume. Because every spend reader folds these records — the spend rollup, each data node's daily usage and the turn list — the executor's run's tokens are counted where every phase's are, in the Execute band; before this record existed they reached the budget counter and nothing a person reads.

**The whole record fits its event**, because the broker refuses one over 8 MiB whole and this record is the launch's only spend record — refused, it would take its tokens, cost, report and refs with it. Every piece of text a box wrote that rides it has a bound, in bytes: the report, the failure's detail and the transcript **256 KiB** each (`sandbox.MaxRunTextBytes`), measured redacted, as the record carries them, since a redaction can lengthen a text (a password's value written in pieces between escaped breaks becomes a marker a piece, seven times its bytes), the question in its `notes` one sentence of at most 300 bytes, the delivered refs and the refs its push record names **16 KiB** together (`sandbox.MaxDeliveredRefBytes`), the reason that record could not be read, where it could not, **64 KiB**, and the two reasons, why the run started a session of its own and why its session was not kept, **1 KiB** each (`sandbox.MaxFellBackBytes`, and the bound the executor log holds the second to). Each listed pushed ref also carries its two whole object ids, under 140 KiB for the most a record lists, less than the push record's reason's worst, which it never rides beside; the session and the release the box names are held to the shapes a harness gives them, at most 128 and 64 bytes, where the box is read and again as the record is published. JSON can grow text six-fold (a control byte becomes `\u00XX`, `<` and its kin `\u003c`, an invalid byte `\ufffd`), so the worst case is (3 × 256 + 16 + 64 + 1 + 2) KiB × 6, about 5.0 MiB, inside the ceiling with room to spare. What happens past a bound depends on who reads the piece:

- **The report and the failure** are what the collected executor's record carries and the reviewer judges, so one past the bound is **condensed by the seat's auxiliary model** (`llm_auxiliary`, falling back to `llm`) and labelled as condensed — never cut, because a report's opening read as the whole report is a run that seems to have said less than it did. Both are redacted before they are measured, and so is the model's rewrite, which no runner saw: a rewrite the redaction puts past the bound is refused as one that came back too long is. The failure's detail is any piece the engine could not read, the exit status, the harness's own error and the **end** of its stderr, in that order — the stderr read from its end, with its unread start said by size (see *What is read whole, and what is read as a stream* below). The whole of it is held to what one condensation reads (`sandbox.MaxCondenseBytes`, 2 MiB) less 1 KiB the engine keeps for a sentence of its own it may put in front: the engine's sentences are taken as they are, and the stderr gets what is left after them. A failure still past what a condensation reads — a harness error as long as the file it came from — keeps whole lines from its start and its end up to that, with the lines between counted where they were, and that is what the model is shown. Where no model can condense one, it keeps **whole lines** up to the bound and says how many it left out: a report from its start (it is read from the top), and a failure from both ends — its first 32 KiB, an eighth of the bound, where the engine states the exit status, the harness's own error and any piece it could not read, and its end for the rest, because the line naming what broke is the last one printed. Kept from its end alone, a failure with a long stderr lost every one of the engine's own sentences.
- **The question** a run parked on is never condensed: the run asks it through `ask_and_wait`, which refuses one past its bounds (a one-sentence question of at most 300 bytes, a context of at most 16 KiB, the decision's own caps) before anything is recorded, so the run rewrites it while it can, and what the person reads is what the run wrote.
- **The delivered refs** are read from the whole report with no count to them — every `Delivered:` line it writes, or every pull-request URL its prose holds where it names none — so they are redacted, deduplicated and listed up to 16 KiB — more than a hundred and sixty pull-request URLs of the usual length, and the bound the refs its push record names share, after these ([below](#what-a-run-pushed-is-reported-for-it)) — with the rest counted (`delivered_refs_elided`) and said where the reviewer reads the refs, never dropped unsaid. A ref that is not valid UTF-8 is counted with them and never listed, whichever runner read it: JSON would replace its bytes, so the record would name a ref the run never delivered — and two refs that differ only there as one.
- **The two reasons** — why the run started a session of its own (`harness_session_fell_back`) and why its session was not kept (`session_not_kept`) — are a sentence each, so one past its kibibyte keeps its opening, where it says what happened, cut on a character and marked `…`. Each is redacted whole before it is cut, so a credential across the cut is never left as a fragment no pattern recognises, and is held again as the record is published, after the publish's own redaction. The first is read off one line of the run's session record, a file its script writes and the box's own agent can rewrite at any length, so it is held too where the runner builds the run's result, the record's one reader that keeps it, and the result carries no more of it, to a node that asked for the read as well; its whole is on the run's error stream, where the script prints it. The record itself is read where it lies, a line at a time and only as far as its four keys, on every collection and every take of the box's session, so a record the box's agent made long costs the engine nothing past its own bytes.
- **The session and the release** the box names are identifiers, so neither is ever cut: a session id the harness reports that is not one a harness mints — letters, digits, `-` and `_`, at most 128 of them — gives way to the session record's, and one the record holds that is not is none; and a release longer than the 64 bytes the executor log records one at is none, which every reader takes for a current release, as it takes one it cannot read. The record holds both to those shapes again as it is published, as it holds the two reasons, whatever runner or host of another build read the box.
- **The transcript** is read by a person — no model and no prompt reads it — so it is not condensed: a long one keeps **whole lines from its start, up to 64 KiB, and from its end, up to 192 KiB**, with one note line where its middle was, naming how many lines and KiB were left out. The end gets the larger share because it is where the run's conclusion and what broke sit; the start is kept because it is the run's plan and its first look at the code — some 370 tool lines at their longest, since a tool line is the tool's name and at most 160 bytes of what it ran, never its output — which a log cut to its end loses entirely. A single line longer than its half keeps its own start (or end), on a character, marked `…`. The counts also ride the record as `activity_transcript_elided_lines` and `activity_transcript_elided_bytes`, so the dashboard's Activity fold says the record is not the whole log. The bound is applied once, by the coordinator, after it redacts the whole transcript again — never by a runner, so a runner that forgot it cannot publish an unbounded one.

Everything read out of the box is **secret-redacted whole, before anything is bounded** — a secret straddling a cut would otherwise survive as a fragment the pattern no longer recognises — and again at the publish. That includes the preview a transcript line shows of what a tool call ran, why it failed and how a run ended: each is redacted whole before it is cut to its 160 bytes, because a token whose start fell just before the cut kept most of its body, too little for any later pass to recognise and enough to identify it. A private key — PEM's armour, or OpenPGP's `-----BEGIN PGP PRIVATE KEY BLOCK-----` — is the one shape that spans lines, and it is redacted as a block, **read by its structure** whether or not its END line was written: its headers (RFC 1421's `Proc-Type:`/`DEK-Info:`, OpenPGP's `Version:`/`Comment:`), its base64 lines at the encoder's width and one shorter last line — then its END, when it has one — with any number of blank lines among them (a key printed double-spaced is one key) and at most one other line between two of its own (a log line another process wrote in the middle of it). A key is read **forward from its BEGIN, and back from an END** that no BEGIN's block reached: a body cut off from its BEGIN by two such lines in a row, or whose BEGIN is not in the text at all — a stream's end read from inside a key. Read back, a full line also has to read like a key's, carrying upper and lower case letters as base64 of random bytes does, digit or none: a line of random base64 lacks a digit about once in fifty thousand, and lines a key's encoding fixes in part (an EC key's second line, a last line of forty-odd characters) far more often — so a few lines of CamelCase identifiers or mixed-case paths directly above a bare END line are read as its body and redacted with it. The END's own line is read both ways where what comes before its armour ends in base64 — a diff's `+` is a prefix and a line of base64 both — and the block reaches as far back as either reading does. Two lines in a row end a block, and an END after them reads back **across them** to a line of the key exactly as wide as an encoder writes one — 64 characters (PEM as OpenSSL and Go write it, PKCS#8, OpenPGP), 70 (OpenSSH) or 76 (MIME's base64, which Java's, Python's and .NET's encoders write), one more under a diff's `+` — from a full line of the same width, from the body's last line or from the END itself, with the body's last line and one more line another process wrote above the two; OpenPGP's checksum line (`=` and a CRC-24, after the body) is read back from as its END is. A password prompt — a line ending in a password's key and its separator (`Password:`, `hunter2 Retype password:`, `pwd=x password:`), that key neither the value of a key before it (as in `pwd= password:`) nor part of a token run into it (`sk-…Pwd:`) — is not one of the two: the read back passes over it, so another process asking for a password three times between a key's lines and its END no longer publishes the key above, and the prompt is redacted with the key. So a key read from inside, its BEGIN not in the text, is redacted whole when another process broke it with two lines, wherever they fell, and with two and one more. What no read reaches is published: where three lines in a row, two whose nearest full line of the key above them is at no encoder's width (a key wrapped at some other width), or two and two more with only the body's short last line between them part a key, the part above them is published when its BEGIN is not in the text, and the part below them when it holds no full line — the short last line alone, BEGIN or none. Read back, a blank line under a wrapping that marks every line — a line number, a grep's context mark — counts as a line between two of the key's, so a key printed double-spaced under one and broken by one line as well is broken by three. Where the key's BEGIN is in the text, the block read forward from it and the one read back across the two are one, and so is their marker: the two lines are redacted with the key, as a single line another process wrote into it is. A line that is not a key's straight after a BEGIN ends its block before it has a body, so an END after it closes nothing: a BEGIN line with no key under it, such as a grep's match for the armour line, is left as it is and takes nothing after it, even when a later grep matches the END — and read back from that END, the same prose is where the read stops. Every wrapping a key reaches a transcript in is read the same way: a JSON string with its line breaks escaped (once or twice), a line number, timestamp, grep prefix or diff `+` on every line (shaped like the armour line's own), a string concatenated across lines, a line `echo`ed at a time, or the whole key on one line, its encrypted headers too — and two whole keys flattened onto one line (`echo $A $B`, an env dump with the breaks stripped) are two keys, each closed at its own END rather than the first abandoned the moment the second's BEGIN appears. Read forward, the body's short last line that ends in the text around it — the quote closing the string the key was written into — ends the block at its base64 unless it reads like a piece of a key (below), and what follows a full line on its line ends nothing, since only the last line is short. **A cut is read wherever it lands.** A tool that bounds what it shows keeps a key's line up to a point (`MIIEow…[...truncated]`, or `MIIEow…` before a bare ellipsis), from a point (`[… 812 bytes truncated]q3O4Ou…`, a window's first line) or both, with its mark between them (`MIIEow…[… 2,345 characters truncated …]…Xyz`) — on one line, on every line, or every line cut short with no mark at all. What is read is the piece of the key such a line still holds where a key's line would stand — the run of base64 that opens the line after its prefix, or the one that ends it before its suffix, eight characters or more in upper and lower case with a digit — and never the mark, which no two tools spell alike; once a piece has said a cut wrote into the line, the run at its other end is the key's too when it reads like one, digit or none, since the first sixteen characters of an RSA key's line (`MIIEogIBAAKCAQEA`) hold none. Read forward, such a line is a line of the body, its key material running to that run or to its last piece, and the block goes on from it; so is a short line that is a piece straight after the BEGIN or with text after it (the head a cut kept and a short last line a quote closes read alike), and a short piece with another below it, since a body has one last line and two in a row are a key cut short on every line; and so is the tail a cut kept between a BEGIN's armour and its line break. Read back, every line a cut wrote into is a line of the body, however narrow and whichever end of it the cut kept, and so is a short line that is a piece — so a key cut on every line, from its start or its end, behind a mark or with none, is read back from its END whole — and since the cut hid the width the line was at, a read across two lines another process wrote lands on it from a line at an encoder's width below, and goes on from it to one above. And a key written on one line is read back from its END on that line as it is read forward from its BEGIN, across one cut in it — once, as a block carries one line that is not the key's between two that are — to the key's runs beyond the mark, when they hold one at a full line's width, or, where nothing written up to the END reads as the key's, one exactly as wide as an encoder writes or wider than any, since a path in mixed case before a grep's match for the END is forty characters too. Nothing another rule redacts is a piece of a key: a password's key, its separator and its value, and a token, are left to their own rules, so no cut's reading takes part of one into the key's redaction and publishes the rest. What a cut leaves that reads like no key's is published: a piece shorter than eight characters, or one with no digit or in one case alone, where it is all a line holds of the key — and that line is one that is not the key's, so straight after the BEGIN it ends the block before it has a body, and two of them together end a block anywhere, the key's lines below published unless an END reads back to them; a piece between two marks, at neither end of its line; on a key written on one line, what lies across a second cut, or across a cut beyond which no run of the key is that wide; with no END below, the tail a mark on a line of its own kept after a head cut short, and the key's lines after it; and, read from inside, the lines above two another process wrote into a key cut short on every line. A line of prose beside a key that opens or ends in such a run — `=== RUN   TestSHA256Sum` above an END — is read as one a cut wrote into and redacted with the key, and lines of prose that each open or end in one, straight above an END, are read as a key cut on every line, as far up as they go; a line holding such a run anywhere else is not, except an END's own line, where such a run before the armour, up to one shorter run after it, is read as the body a window kept, key or none (`=== RUN   TestSHA256Sum x -----END PRIVATE KEY-----`, a wrapping's gutter `[Worker1A] ` before the END). And after a key whose END was never written, a short line after a line a cut wrote into is read as the body's last line, as it is after any full line, so a word the tool printed next is redacted with its mark. A line another rule names is no line of the key — a token, or a password's key and its separator: under a key whose END was never written, `Password:`, or a `pwd=` whose `=` ends its line, is a line interrupting the key rather than its last, so the value on the next line is redacted with the key, or by the password's rule when it cannot be the key's last line — never published with the separator left behind. What that costs is a key's last line that ends in a password's key and `=`, about one key in fifty thousand, which is published when no END follows it. Another block's armour, a certificate's, ends a key's block in either direction, on a line of its own or on the key's, and so does a key already redacted, whose marker stands where its armour stood — which is what makes text redacted twice read as it did once: a one-line key whose END was lost is redacted up to the certificate or the next key written after it, and so is one whose END comes after a word. A password's key is read **as the text writes it**: closed by the quote or the markup it is written in (`{"password": "x"}`, `**Password**: x`, `` `password`: x ``) — a quote only where it closes the key's own string, a name such as `"db_password"`, so a longer string ending in the word (`"POST /auth/password": () =>`) is no key — and separated from its value by `:`, `=` or the operator a language assigns or compares with (`:=`, `==`, `=>`) — read with a bare `:` or `=` straight after the key, a JSON object's password was no key at all, and an operator's second character was taken for the value. The markup that closes what the key's line opened before its label is the key's too (`**Root password:** x`, `| Password: | x |`, `| **Password:** | x |`), and the value is read after it — on its line, or, where nothing follows it there, on a line below as after a separator (`**Password:**` over the value, `- **Password:**` over the value indented under it); a quote is no such markup, since a logged string closes with one and what follows it is the next field. A password's value is read **where its wrapping puts it**: when what follows `password:` on its line is only the punctuation the wrapping writes after every line — the quote closing an `echo`'s string and its redirection, the `" +` of a string concatenated across lines, a table's ` |` — or nothing, the value is on the first line after it that is not blank, read past the **gutter** that line opens with — the start of what the key's line has before the key (a reader's line number, a grep's context mark, a diff's `+`, a log's timestamp, the quote opening a concatenated string), shaped so that a number moving on from line to line, or a timestamp's fraction written on one line and not the next, is still the gutter, and ending where a word of both lines does — and ended before the same punctuation, or before what a list's or a concatenation's last line closes with instead, or before the `>>` that appends to a file the key's line opened with `>`. The wrapping's blank lines between — an empty numbered line, `echo "" >> key.pem` — are passed over; a line holding a password's key where the value would be ends the read, since its key names what is there, while a key further along the line is the line's own and the value before it is still read; a line that does not end in that punctuation says it was no wrapping's, so the punctuation is the value, as `***` is; and a word in any script, or an empty quoted string, is a value, never punctuation. The value alone is replaced, so the gutter, the punctuation and the blank lines stay. Taking the first word after the separator wherever it stood, the rule redacted the wrapping's own syntax — an `echo`'s closing quote, the quote that closes a concatenated string's piece, the number of a numbered line a tab follows or of a blank one — and published the value on the line after. What is still published: a value under a quoted key whose name is more than letters, digits, `_`, `-` and `.` (`{"Admin Password": "x"}`); a value `printf '%s\n'` writes a line at a time (the escaped break is inside every line's own prefix); a blank numbered line that lost the tab after its number, or a log's blank line that lost the space after its timestamp, which is read as the value; a value shaped like the word the key's line opens with, a space after it on both (`pass2 next` under `pass1 Retype password:`), which is read as the gutter as one timestamp is under another; a value on a line that opens with the punctuation the key's line has straight before its key, after words that line does not repeat — a list's dash after a logged message (`msg: - Password:` over `- hunter2x9`), a string's first line (`{"text": "> Password:\n> hunter2x9"}`) — which is read as the value, since the gutter is read from the line's start and ends where the two lines' words part; where the markup that closes the key ends its piece of a string (`{"text": "| Password: |\n| hunter2x9 |"}`), no value is read at all; a value written over several lines — a YAML block scalar (`password: |`, `password: >-`), a YAML sequence under the key (`- hunter2`), a heredoc — of which the first word at most is read; a diff whose key is on a context line and whose value changed, of which the removed line's first word is read and the added value published (where the values are indented, that word is the `-` alone, and both are published); a value under a gutter that holds an armour, or under a blank line of the wrapping whose gutter holds a password's key; under a wrapping's suffix, a value on a line that holds a password's key of its own (`echo "hunter2 password:" >> f` under `echo "Password:" >> f`), the key's line taking its own punctuation for its value instead, or the closing of the markup that alone ends it (`| Password: |` over `| > Retype password: |`), since redacting the value would change how that line ends once the second key's value is replaced, and a second pass would read the first key's value elsewhere; the part of a value after an escaped break inside it when that part is punctuation alone (`pwd=ab\n!!`), which reads as a string's closing quote; and a key's piece on the line of a value read below a password's key that a key's END line goes on to (`-----END RSA PRIVATE KEY-----|passwd:`): the keys are read with that value claimed, in the text as written, so the piece is no line of a key, while the rule reads the value in the text the keys' blocks leave, where the END's line is a marker that can join the value's line to it or take the gutter the END's line opened with, and reads another value, or none — and a second pass, the block gone, can read the piece as a key's and redact it. What it costs is a line after the key that ends as the key's did and holds no value: a prompt logged as `msg="password:"` over `msg="connected"` has `connected` redacted, where the rule used to replace the quote; a key a quote closes whose separator ends its line — a Go `case "password":` — has the next line's first word redacted, and so does a JSON key whose value is on the next line, as it should; a code span in prose, `` `Password:` and a value ``, has the word after it redacted; and a name in a string or a code span that is no key where it stands is read as one, so a choice between two strings that opens with one (`masked ? "password" : "text"`) has the other redacted, and the shell's `` `pwd` `` before a colon (`` -v `pwd`:/app ``) the path after it. What the other rules write once the keys are read leaves a second pass nothing new to read either: a password's rule replaces its value alone, so `password:` and the line break before a value on the next line stay where they were, and an escaped line break inside a value stays too, the value redacted on either side of it (`pwd=ab\ncd` becomes `pwd=[REDACTED:password]\n[REDACTED:password]`, since whether the `\n` is a break or two characters of the password the text does not say); a value or a token stops at an armour or a marker; and an armour line that holds a marker — before its armour or after it, which is what a pass leaves beside an armour it read no key from — holds no key read across its lines, and a key written on it is read back from its END no further than the marker. So the coordinator, redacting again a transcript the runner redacted, changes nothing — save the key's piece beside an END line that goes on to a password's key, above. A block is never read more than 64 KiB from its armour (`redact.MaxKeyBlockBytes`, the largest key there is more than twice over in any wrapping), so a BEGIN on a line that runs on past that — a minified bundle naming the armour — holds back nothing after that line. And the pass stays linear on a line of many armours: each one's scan stops at the armour next to it. So it does on a password's value written in pieces between escaped breaks with no space among them (`pwd=x\nx\nx…`, or a key after every break): whether the value runs on across a break is read only as far as the first letter or digit after it, never again to the end of the value, which cost the breaks times the length — days for a file of the 32 MiB the engine reads whole.

**What is read whole, and what is read as a stream.** A file meant to be read whole — the findings report, the result line, the exit status — is read whole, and one past **32 MiB** (`sandbox.MaxFileBytes`) is refused rather than read in part, on the engine host exactly as on a remote box, because a clipped report reads as a finished one. A refusal degrades **only that piece**: it is described by its size, the run reads as not succeeded with that as the reason, and its tokens, delivered refs and transcript are still collected, charged and published. A run's **machine streams** are never refused for their size, because the engine keeps a bounded share of each anyway: the event stream is decoded a line at a time in bounded memory (a single line past 32 MiB is skipped and counted where it was), and the error stream is read **from its end** — at most what one condensation takes beside the engine's own sentences in front of it (2 MiB less 1 KiB, and less those sentences), with its unread start said by size, because a process's conclusion is the last thing it prints. The read takes **64 KiB more** before what it keeps (`redact.MaxKeyBlockBytes`) and never shows them: a private key whose BEGIN came before what is kept is read with them as the key it is, and what is kept begins after whatever they decide. Read from inside, its BEGIN not in the text, such a key is redacted only as far back as a read from its END reaches, so its lines above three that part it are published — and bounded by the size of the redacted text, what was kept used to reach back into those 64 KiB whenever the keys after them collapsed into their markers. It used to be read to the whole 2 MiB and carried behind those sentences, which put every failure with a long stderr a few hundred bytes past what a condensation reads, so none of them was ever condensed. Reading them whole used to refuse a run for the size of its own log: a poll that could not read a 32 MiB stdout never reached its liveness probe and held a finished-but-hung or dead job's seat for good, and a collection that could not read it lost a result its report file held. On the engine host **every access to a box's files goes through the box's home opened as a root** (Go's `os.Root`): a read, a write, a directory made and a file removed alike, by a name relative to the home, walked a component at a time. A link on the way is followed only to somewhere beneath the home; one that leads out of it — an absolute link, or a relative one climbing past the home — is refused at the access, whenever the box put it there, in the same words as a path named outside the home. The engine's records about a box are reached the same way, through the box's `records/`. And a box's file is read only if it is a **regular file where it lies**: the other side of a `container` box's mount is the agent's, and a named pipe made at the done marker used to block the completion poll for good (an open waits for a writer and cannot be cancelled), so anything that is not a regular file — a symbolic link at the path included, whether it leads nowhere or was put there after the path was checked — is refused, naming the path and what it is, and never read as an empty file. So is anything but a directory where a directory on the way to the file was — a named pipe in place of `.crewlet/`, say — which is refused at once, naming that path and what it is, rather than opened. The poll reports it; a collection degrades **only that piece**, exactly as for one past the size cap — described for what it is, the run not succeeded with that as the reason, and the rest collected, charged and published (a refused result file is read from the event stream's last line, which is what the wrapper copies into it) — because what a path names is the box's own doing, and a collection retried over it would be refused again until the run was settled as lost. A **write** takes the same rule, and **replaces the file whole**: what the engine writes into a box on its host — a setup step's files, a harness's configuration, each written after something may already have run there, and the token file the completion poll rewrites under a running job — is written to a fresh file beside the path, synced, and renamed over it in one step, so a process in the box reading the file meanwhile reads the old file or the new one, never an empty or half-written one (written in place, the token file was empty for a moment on every renewal, and a push whose credential helper read it then had nothing to authenticate with). The rename writes through nothing at the path, and it replaces only a regular file, or puts one where nothing is: a link at the path is refused rather than followed (one that led nowhere used to have its target created on the engine host) and a named pipe is refused rather than waited on, each left as the box made it, and the launch fails naming the path and what it is. A write that fails leaves nothing staged beside the path, and the new file is `0600` whatever mode the one it replaced had. An `e2b` box's file is replaced the same way, through its envd: uploaded under a fresh name beside the path, then moved over it, because envd writes an upload in place. A **streamed** write — what a session archive carried into a box is written with, since it grows with its session — is the same replacement fed a piece at a time, never held whole: copied into the staged file on the engine host, and on `e2b` sent as the upload's body as it is read (chunked, and so sent once: a connection envd closes before reading it fails the write rather than being retried). A source that fails part way fails the write, and the file at the path is the one that was there. The engine's own records about a local box are kept beside its home rather than in it (see [Local sandboxes](#local-sandboxes)) and read and written the same way, so a record that is not a regular file answers at once rather than blocking the poll, a collection or a reaper: an unusable record of the box's logins is logged (`local_sandbox_credential_map_unreadable`) and costs only the write-back of a login the run refreshed, and an unusable job record reads as not knowing whether the job runs, which keeps the box.

The dashboard renders each launch **inside the card of the round that launched it**: how it ended, what it spent, the report, and the transcript as a closed **Activity** fold. Every turn therefore reads as the executor — one card per round, from its launch, with the prompt the run was sent — the fresh brief the started record carries, and where the launch continues a session, the continuation it handed the run, published as the round's opening frame once the launch has its box ([A relaunch continues the turn's session](#a-relaunch-continues-the-turns-session)) — to its collection, with what the run submitted and its bridged calls inside it, and the round's launches under them — then the review. A launch whose round has no record of its own — a run that parked on a question and ended before an answer resumed it — is drawn on its own, in its round's place. In the company's list of recent phases a launch is a row of its own, drawn as the executor's: the `execute` phase word with the harness that ran it. Where the record's bounds left something out, the screens say so rather than showing the part as the whole: the Activity fold counts **kept of all** lines (`412 of 4,000 lines`, or the bytes left out of a single over-long line), and a run's **Delivered** — on its page and on its span in the turn trace — names how many more refs its report holds than the record lists (`… (+41 more its report names)`).

---

## Each launch's session, kept

A run's harness keeps its conversation in its box — Claude Code as a transcript and its sidecars, OpenCode in its database — and a box does not outlive its turn. So every launch's session is **taken out of its box and kept** in the fleet's [object store](object-store.md), one sealed object per capture, named by the turn's row of the `executor` state log ([ADR-0073](https://github.com/crewlet/crewlet/blob/main/adr/0073-a-seats-executor-session-is-a-sealed-object-its-turns-row-names.md)), for a launch in any box on any node to continue: what is kept is the whole session, scrubbed, and the turn's row names the newest one the turn kept.

**Where it is taken.** At every point the box can still be read, on the node whose host the box is on — a `direct` or `container` box is touched only there, and a node that holds the seat but not the box asks that host to read it — before the box is paused or reclaimed, and, for a stop, a loss or a job its turn's cap passed while it still worked, once the read has **ended the job** (below):

| Point | What is taken |
|---|---|
| A completion's collection | The archive the run's script packed when its harness exited ([Claude Code](#a-claude-code-runs-session), [OpenCode](#an-opencode-runs-session)); for a job that ended without packing — one that failed before it got there, one [lost with its engine](#failure-modes) whose box is still there — one **packed now**, by the runner's own pack, which keeps the archive's digest in the box as the script's does. A job its turn's cap passed while it still ran is **ended first**, as a stop's read ends one (below): the archive its script packed on its way out — an OpenCode run's exported through the run's own server — or one packed now where it left none, final wherever the job ended |
| A person's stop | The archive the run's script packed as the stop's read ended its job, or one packed now where it left none — final wherever the job ended — in the read that charges what the stopped job spent, after the job's bridge session is closed and before its box is reclaimed |
| A run lost with its box still there | The archive the run's script packed as the loss's read ended its job, or one packed now where it left none — final wherever the job ended — after the job's bridge session is closed and before the loss's ending reclaims the box (a launch whose engine restarted before its turn parked), on this node's host or asked of the host the box is on |

A box that is **gone** — lost from its host, or with a node that left the fleet — has nothing left to read, and keeps nothing: the launch's session is lost with it, and the newest capture the turn kept stays the one its row names — but the relaunch that follows that job has no capture of its session to continue, so it starts a session of its own — or, where the turn has kept no session at all, continues the one its first launch was handed ([below](#a-conversations-next-turn-continues-its-last-turns-session)).

**A stop, a loss or a turn's cap ends the job before it takes the session.** A job still working when its session is taken is packed as it stands — a step in flight half recorded — so the read that takes a stopped or lost job's session, and the collection of a job its turn's wall-clock cap passed while it still ran, first asks its script to end it ([A Claude Code run's session](#a-claude-code-runs-session), [OpenCode](#an-opencode-runs-session)): the harness is told to stop and the session packed as the run's own end packs it, and the take is of that archive, final. The cap is an end like a stop: the run is ended as if it had finished, and its bridge session is closed before its job is asked. Its collection asks first whether the job finished, so a job that finished by itself before the read is collected as any finished job is; one whose poll at its collection cannot say is asked to end all the same: a poll that cannot say is no reason to leave the job working past its cap. A run whose job the collection saw still working past its cap — its poll answered that it ran, or its script heard the request to end — still says it was cut off at its turn's cap, and is never parked on a question it asked on its way out; the collection records that on the run's record before it asks or kills anything, so a collection made again after one cut short as it ended the job — its node draining, the seat moving — says so too, though the kill that outlived the first may have taken the script before it recorded the request. The read waits for the job to be over for at most **2¼ minutes** — the harness's five-second grace, the pack's two minutes, and five seconds each for the script's own stops of its login exporter and its server — and then kills what is left of it (`SIGTERM`, five seconds, `SIGKILL`, five seconds more): a sweep of stray processes after a clean end, the escalation past the wait, and at once for a script that answers no request (one an older build started, one not yet at its first line), whose session is then packed by the read, final. Only a job the kill could not reach — a box whose runtime would not run it, a kernel record a `direct` box could not read, so the signal was withheld, a `container` job whose box can name no process group of the job's own — is taken as it stands, not final, and its reclaim ends it. The kill keeps the box for the take: a `direct` box kills its job's recorded group, a `container` box the job's process group in its container — recorded as the job started, or, for a job an older build started, which recorded none, read off the job's first process where an exec's own session leads it, as every runc exec's does; never the container's main process, beside which the take still runs, and never the job's first process alone, which is the runner's wrapper rather than the job, so a job whose group neither names is signalled nothing — and an `e2b` box every process of its user, since E2B starts a job in its agent's own process group.

A job packed while it still ran — one its box would not end, the only such pack now that a turn's cap ends its job first, as a stop does — is packed as it stands, and **its capture's save point is not the calls the run's log had taken before the pack** — nor is that of any capture of a job cut off ([below](#a-relaunch-continues-the-turns-session)): a harness can make a call, and have it answered, before its own record holds it — Claude Code writes a message only once the whole of it has streamed, so one killed while a message streamed never wrote the calls it had made, and OpenCode's export leaves out a step that has not settled — so the capture is placed at the newest save point its launch is already known to hold, that of its own last capture that landed, or the round's start where none that landed states one, and a run that continues it is told every call of its round since then, some more than once and none never. **A Claude Code pack made beside a harness that may still be working** (that of a job its box would not end; one of a job that is over, its harness gone, is packed as the run's own end packs it) **refuses a file its harness rewrote under it**, since Claude Code rewrites more than it appends — a task file as its task tool changes it, a subagent's metadata as the subagent ends, its own transcript renamed over by a trim or rewritten in place where it withdraws a message — and `tar` cannot be taken at its word: GNU `tar` exits 1 on a file that grew as it read it and pads one that shrank with NULs, while busybox's says nothing of the first and cuts its archive inside the second. So the pack asks the box for the tools it reads the session with (`find`, `ls`, `wc`, `sed`, `grep`, `head`, `cksum`, `touch` and `sleep`, naming one it lacks); sets every file and directory of the session stamped in the box's future to the box's present, leaving every earlier time as it is and setting none through a link, which `touch` would follow out of the session (a box cannot hold a file from its own future, and the pack's checks would read one as written until the box's clock passed it, whether a box whose clock ran ahead stamped it, the session carried here with its times, or this box before its clock stepped back; done before the marker, it leaves a file the harness writes once the marker is down read as written all the same, and one written before the marker is whole by the time `tar` reads it; and set to the present, never to an earlier time, it hides no write from another pack of the same session running beside this one, as a stop's read and a collection's of one job may each make at once, each in a staging directory of its own — which at most refuses a file this one set after its marker); writes a marker, waits until the box's own `find` reads a file written after it as newer (a second at most, where busybox compares whole seconds; a box whose `find` never does is refused after fifty sleeps of a tenth of a second, five seconds, rather than packed blind, and one whose `sleep` takes no tenth of a second is refused saying so), lists every file of the session with its inode and size — refusing at once a session whose files add up past the quarter of a gibibyte the scrub reads, which the take would refuse once packed — and runs `tar`; then, whatever `tar`'s exit 0 or 1 said, it refuses the pack, naming the file, where the archive does not read back (busybox's archive cut inside a file that shrank among them) or holds no file, a file is gone, its inode changed or it shrank, a file the list named is not in the archive, a file other than a transcript was written since the marker, the session's own transcript (read back on every pack, since Claude Code rewrites it in place and a clock stepped back as the pack ran could hide that rewrite from `find`) or a subagent's thread written since then is not, byte for byte, the start of the live file — its copy read back out of the archive — or, looked at last, a file the archive holds is gone, which a file removed while the checks ran is. No check rests on a tool's word that what it wrote landed: a tool that checks no write it makes exits 0 having written nothing, or half, to a full disk, which a harness still writing can fill under the pack. So every list and every answer a check reads reaches the shell through a pipe, each `find`'s own exit held, and the shell writes it to the pack's staging directory and reads it back, refusing the pack where the file does not hold what was written. A check that cannot be made refuses the pack as a difference would, never reading as nothing having changed: a list that cannot be made or kept, or a comparison `grep` cannot make (its exit 2, or an exit 0 that names nothing). A transcript or a subagent's thread that only grew is packed as `tar` first saw it, its last line perhaps one its harness had not finished writing, which is kept as the text it is and cut off before a later run resumes the session. A pack refused so leaves nothing behind: its staging directory goes with it. And an OpenCode export, made by the box's OpenCode itself (`--standalone`) since only the run's script can reach the run's server, leaves out the message still being written, and can find the database busy under a server still running. **Every pack made now stages its archive in a directory of its own**, of a job still working or of one that is over, so two reads of one job at once — a stop's and a collection's — each packing it, never clear each other's files or move each other's archive, and the clear before every run removes any one a pack cut short left. **And every pack made now is its read's alone**, of a job that is over as much as of one still working: its archive is moved to a place of its own beside its staging directory, streamed from there and removed once the read is done with it — never moved over the job's capture, which only the job's own script writes, moving the job's final archive there once, the moment its harness exits. A Claude Code take reads its archive twice, opening it again by its path for its second pass, and over one capture a take was refused whenever another archive was moved there between its two passes: another read's of the same job, or the job's own end pack's, which can land while a pack still checks what it read; and the archive of a job as it stood, moved there after the final one, was taken by a read of the job once over as everything the job did. So each read streams the archive its own pack made, and one of a job that left its archive streams that archive, which nothing moves over while it reads it. The removal once the read is done is held to the take's own time, so a box that stops answering once its archive is read keeps the read no longer than the take was given, leaving its leftovers to the clear before the next run. A pack that **fails** — a box without `tar` or `gzip`, an export that would not run, an OpenCode that could not be asked whether it holds the session (its private server would not start, its database was busy, it crashed) — is a capture refused with what the pack said, never "no session"; a harness that wrote no transcript, or an OpenCode that answers it holds no such session, has nothing to take. A job read again after it was taken whole — a collection retried after a resume that failed — is not taken a second time once that take has settled; one still being kept is another read's, which this one stands by for (below).

**What is scrubbed.** Everything that leaves a box passes the engine's secret redaction, and a kept session more so — file by file, each as what it is, so what is kept is still the archive its harness restores:

1. **By value**, every secret the engine itself put in the box is taken out wherever it appears, raw and as a JSON string escapes it, whatever its shape — everything the launch handed the box from a **credential position**: every value a `${VAR}` resolved to, in the run's environment (the seat's `role.sandbox.env`, the setup steps' env), in a setup file that is one `${VAR}` whole, and in an MCP server's url; every variable of the run's environment whose name says it carries a credential (`…_TOKEN`, `…_KEY`, `…_SECRET`, `…_PASSWORD` and their kind) and the sign-in of the `providers.llm` entry the launch ran on, written or referenced alike; its MCP servers' env and headers; the run's own telemetry token and its credential on [Gitlet](gitlet.md); every string of the login the box was seeded with; and **every token the completion poll renews in the running box** (below). Each is taken out whole and, where it is a JSON document, by every string it holds, as the login is: a seat's AWS session reaches its box as the document its `credential_process` prints, and a transcript prints the session's token alone. **Every string, not only those under a credential's key**, since the engine can tell a document's credential from its settings by nothing but the names they are written under: so a JSON document you hand a box through a `${VAR}` — a Google Cloud service-account key, say — gives up every string of eight bytes or more it holds, its project ID and its client email among them, which every kept transcript of the seat loses wherever it names them. A format's own published constants are no secret, and stay: of a Google service-account key — a document whose `type` is exactly `service_account` — the values of `type`, `auth_uri`, `token_uri`, `auth_provider_x509_cert_url` and `universe_domain` are fixed by Google's format, the same in every key, so every googleapis.com URL the seat's tools print stays in its transcripts, while the key's private key and that key's ID, its client email and ID, its project ID and its client certificate's URL, which names the client email, go. Its members' names, numbers and booleans are never taken out, nor is a string under eight bytes, as no value that short is: a `true` or a `1` in the document, or the registry host a Docker config files its credentials under, stays. Keep a setting beside the secret instead, written in the document or in a variable of its own; the [Google Cloud integration](../integrations/google-cloud.md) issues a seat a bare token. **Nothing else**: not the seat's handle or email, a setting written in the document, a setup step's script or a file it writes in its own words — none is a secret, and taken out by value each was lost from every kept transcript that named it; a credential written in the document is the shapes' below. They are collected **at the launch**, from the very request the job was started with, and kept sealed under the fleet keyring **beside** the run's record, under a key of its own named for the job, written once the attach that starts the job has landed — never on the record, which every git request of a coding box and every listing reads whole, though only a capture, the next launch and the poll's record of a renewed token (below) read this — so a rotated secret or a renamed entry is the value the box was handed; a job that **continues in the box** the turn's previous job ran in carries that job's sealed set forward beside its own, since the box still holds those values and the session it resumes their lines; and the login is read again at the capture, in the box and on the engine host, since a refresh mints values the launch never saw. **A token the engine issues the seat** — its GitHub App token, its AWS session, its Google Cloud token — is renewed in the box's files while the run works ([the git-auth recipe](#the-git-auth-recipe)), and read again at the capture it would be another issue, never the one the box holds; so the poll **records each renewed value in the job's sealed set, beside the run's record, before it writes the file**, and the launch of a job that continues in its turn's kept box seals what its own refresh wrote with the job. A renewed value the store does not take is not written on that poll — the run keeps the token its files hold, said as `sandbox_token_refresh_failed` — and the next poll, fifteen seconds later, tries again, well inside the five minutes an AWS session's renewal leaves. So every capture of the run taken once its job has ended — by itself, asked or killed — is scrubbed of every token the poll put in its box; a capture of a job its box would not end, taken as it stands, reads the set before its pack, so a token the poll renews while that pack is made, printed meanwhile by the job still working, goes only by its shape. A renewed value is dropped from the set an hour past its expiry, since a lapsed token opens nothing, which keeps a day of an AWS session's renewals — one every ten minutes — to the few still near their lapse. In a fleet mid-upgrade, a node running an older version of the engine takes the renewed values out as it takes every other, and its own polls record none of the renewals they write. A capture that cannot know them — a launch whose secrets could not be sealed or whose write of them beside the record did not land, a continued box whose record keeps nothing of what the jobs before it were handed, past the 64 KiB a launch may keep, or a set the poll's renewals take past it once the lapsed ones are dropped (whose token is written all the same: the run needs a good token more than a kept session; `executor_session_scrub_incomplete`) — is **refused** rather than kept scrubbed of less; so is one whose login, read again, is past what a whole launch may hand (the box's own code can write that file), or whose values come to more than four times that in all. A value under eight bytes is left, as ordinary text, to the shapes below, and so is a PEM armour line — every key of its kind carries it, and the shapes read a key by it. The run's bridge token, which no value the launch handed holds, is caught by its shape instead (`crewlet_rt1…`), as every token the engine mints for a run is.
2. **By shape**, the denylist every surface redacts with — the vendor prefixes, a cloud session's among them (an AWS session's access key ID, `ASIA…`, and a Google access token, `ya29.…`, which a run can obtain by itself), a JSON Web Token, private keys, passwords, and an AWS session's secret access key and session token, which have no shape of their own and so are read as a password is, by the key AWS writes each under (`SecretAccessKey`, `aws_secret_access_key`, `AWS_SESSION_TOKEN`, `X-Amz-Security-Token` and their kind), whoever issued the session — on every transcript line string by string, where a key's lines are lines again, every JSON document a token at a time, every key of every object as well as every value — a tool's structured result can name a member by a secret — a string or a number under a credential's own key (`password`, `api_key`, `token`, an AWS session's `SessionToken`, a Docker config's `auth`, `identitytoken` and `registrytoken`, and their kind) taken out whole, and every other file as text, a line at a time as the redaction settles it. A credential's key is read with the separators between its words dropped, so `aws_session_token`, `SessionToken` and `sessionToken` are each the session token's key, and AWS's `X-Amz-Security-Token` header is read by its own words, the security token's. An AWS session's secret access key or session token printed bare, under no key, is kept as printed where the run obtained the session by itself, though its access key ID goes by its shape wherever it is; a session the engine issued the seat, at its launch or renewed since, is taken out by value (above), whole and by each of its strings. A `.json` file that is not one JSON value — a task file a pack of a working job tore — is kept as text, and so is a transcript's torn last line.

**And the archive's own words.** A member's name is scrubbed by value and by shape, as the transcript's paths to it are, its header carries only its kind, name, mode, size and time — no owner, no link target, no extended record of the box's — and anything but a file or a directory (a link the box's own code made) is left out.

**Within bounds, whatever the box wrote.** The archive is the box's own, and its code can write a gzip that expands a thousandfold, a line with no end or a value nested a million deep. So the scrub reads at most eight times the archive's 32 MiB bound of plaintext, holds no line, string or stretch awaiting its redaction past 8 MiB, and rewrites no JSON value nested past ten thousand levels as JSON; a session past the first two is **refused**, its stamp saying so.

Then the archive is **sealed as it streams** under the fleet keyring, bound to the seat, the turn and its object, into a spool on the host's disk — sealed there too, and removed once its keep is done, refused or dropped; a spool a crash left behind is swept by the first take on that node once it is older than a capture can take — and the box is paused or reclaimed; the **upload and the record come after**, off the read, so no read and no ending waits on the object store's pace. No step holds more than a piece of the archive. The scrub changes the archive's bytes, so the box records the kept archive's digest beside its own: carried back into the box it came from, it is never restored over the copy that box went on with.

**Recorded through the estate router, on every node.** The capture is recorded on the executor log through the [estate router](../guides/satellite-nodes.md), so a seats-only node records one through a data node as a data node records its own; under the operation the turn, the launch and the capture's place derive, asked again under that same operation while the log does not answer — for thirty seconds, which outlasts a broker's leader election — so it lands once however often it is asked.

**Every capture is stamped on the run's record.** Each launch of a turn is numbered (1, 2, …) in the write that opens it, and each capture takes its place — the launch's number and its own among that launch's captures — on the run's record before a byte moves, beside what the job had been answered and told when it was packed — its **save point**, how many of the round's calls the run's tool log had taken when the capture read it, where the job was over and its harness had exited cleanly by itself, a read made after its script packed it and before the take — every other capture, of a job still working or cut off, reads no log and takes the newest save point its launch is already known to hold (above), so a log the store cannot read refuses none of those — and the last note read, where its harness ended its own run, stopped before the first read the record marks [unanswered](#notes-to-a-running-run) — a take whose answer never reached the harness, whose notes the session lacks while it holds the answered ones after them (a capture cut off states none: it may lack its job's last take of the notes, and is not known to hold its job's brief, which told the notes the session it continued lacked) — so a later launch that continues it knows what came after. The tool log numbers every call of a round as it takes it, so each call it takes after the read that placed a save point is one the session may not have been answered, wherever it sits among the calls the run made: a call still in flight at the read is taken once its tool answers. Off the same read — or off the capture whose save point it takes — the stamp also carries the highest bridged call and the places below it the log held no call at, which an older version of the engine reads its place by in a fleet mid-upgrade and this one does not, since a long round's dropped middle fills the places it keeps and hides a call in flight below them. Each stamp also says whether its harness was cut off — the engine packed it, or the run's script packed it after a harness that did not exit cleanly, killed by a signal or its memory limit — so it may end mid-record; and, once it is spooled, what its last request carried, read off its own transcript as it was taken, kept or not, which a launch that continues it — restored, or as the job's own box holds it — is held to ([What a continued launch costs](#what-a-continued-launch-costs)). How it came out is stamped beside it: **landed** (the executor log names its object); **refused**, with why — a pack that failed, a session past its 32 MiB bound or the scrub's, secrets the scrub could not know, the store's reservation spent, the seat's [budget](../getting-started/configuration.md) its live turns already hold, a record the log will never take; or **unresolved** — uploaded, and its record sent and not answered, which the turn's end asks again under the same operation before it ends the turn, and a relaunch before it decides which session it continues, and which is read as refused once its object is past the twelve hours an object may be named in. Every capture of a turn that read its conversation also carries that read as the turn's first launch froze it, which is what the turn's end absorbs ([below](#a-conversations-next-turn-continues-its-last-turns-session)). **And the turn's end carries the newest capture where it was not kept, and says where the turn went on past the session its row names** — wherever the run's record does not hold the turn's last word, the final capture of its last launch that ran a job, kept: a capture taken while its job still worked, an earlier launch's, the jobs after it keeping nothing newer, or one beside a newer capture that was not kept. The executor log's row keeps whatever session it names — a capture whose taker stopped, which the log took after a relaunch had read it as refused, among them — so the conversation's next turn continues what the turn did up to it rather than an older session or none, and the row says the turn went on past it, never that it is the turn's last; where the turn's newest capture was its last word, the end names that capture (`last_word`), and a row that holds it after all — the log took its record although the run's record held it refused — is read as the turn's last word ([Turn Engine](turn-engine.md#how-a-turn-ends-on-the-executor-log)).

**Of two reads of one job at once, one holds the place and the other stands by.** A stop's read and a collection's of one job can take its session at once, each streaming the archive its own pack made, or the job's own capture where its script left one (above), and the place both ask for is the coordination store's to give one of them. The other — its reservation refused, or never answered and its place found another's when it settled it, or the job's capture found begun by the first — takes the session all the same and **stands by** for that capture rather than walking away from it: named on the capture's stamp (`standby_taker`, `standby_taker_epoch`, `standby_at`) before it spools, its session sealed on its own node's disk, and its keep waiting to see what the capture comes to. Where it **lands**, the standby keeps nothing: the session is uploaded once. Where it does not — the read holding the place had its scrub refused, its seal or its upload fail, or its node stop before it uploaded — the standby takes the place after it and keeps its own session there; and where that read's record went unanswered, the standby asks it again under its own operation, as the turn's end does, keeping nothing where the log holds it and its own where the log refuses it, so the session is never uploaded twice beside a record that landed. A read that walked away once left such a failed place to keep nothing, though it held the session whole. A loss's read does the same: it once passed the box over wherever the job's final capture was on the record, one still being kept included, and reclaimed it unread beside a keep that could still fail; it now passes over only one that has settled. **A read of the job ended beside a capture taken as the job stood** — a stop's read whose end could neither end its job nor poll it — takes the place after it instead of standing by, since its session is the more whole, and is kept whatever the other comes to. The turn's end waits for a standby as for a capture still being kept, and for as long as its takeover and its keep can take: the twenty-one minutes a capture can take and, past them, the standby's keep in the place after it, about thirty-nine minutes in all at the bounds, where it once waited the twenty-one and recorded the end ahead of the standby's keep. A standby that cannot keep what it took — its own spool failed, its node is stopping — withdraws, and one whose taker lease has lapsed is withdrawn by the wait, as a begun capture whose taker stopped is settled (below). A standby or a takeover the coordination store took and never answered is settled rather than left on the record with nothing keeping it: the read goes on as one that may stand, and its keep reads the record before it keeps anything. One read stands by for a place at a time: a third read of the same job at once stands down. On a version without it, a standby is dropped by that version's own stamp of the capture and not waited for, and the session is left to the read that holds the place, as before.

**A capture whose taker stopped is settled, not waited out.** A take dies with its engine process, leaving its place stamped begun and its spool on that process's disk; read by its age alone, such a capture would hold the turn's end for the whole twenty-one minutes a capture can take, and a relaunch that followed it for its fifteen seconds before it started a session of its own beside a record the executor log may well have taken. So every engine process holds a **taker lease** of its own in the [coordination store](coordination.md#the-three-valued-answer) — claimed as its sandbox coordinator starts, named by its incarnation (`<node>:<uuid>`) so a restarted process never waits on its predecessor's, for the seat lease TTL (`coordination.lease_ttl_seconds`, 45 seconds by default, and never past the three hours the store keeps a lease outside seats and presence), renewed four times a TTL (every 11.25 seconds by default), **held through a drain** until its last keep has ended, then released; a process that dies leaves it to lapse on its TTL. Every stamp names that lease: its holder and its fencing token (`taker`, `taker_epoch`), read again right before the take reserves its place, so a lease claimed afresh while the session was packed is the one named. A wait for a capture — the turn's end's and a relaunch's — asks whether the lease still holds at that token, one coordination read, and a store that cannot answer is never a taker gone. Where it has **lapsed or moved to another epoch**, the capture is read off the turn's row on the executor log at `linearizable`, which has seen every record the log took before the read: **landed** where the row names its place, stamped from the row and kept; **refused** where it does not — "its taker `<node>` stopped before it was kept" — which the turn's end then names. That settle is written only over the begun stamp it was read from, so a stamp the taker wrote meanwhile stands: an unresolved one, whose record was sent and may yet land, is asked again under its own operation rather than refused. The keep is **fenced**: it checks its own lease before its upload and again before every attempt at its record, from its own renewals rather than a store read — held at the token its stamp names, inside the window its last confirmed renewal covers, three quarters of the TTL, which closes a renewal interval before any peer can read the lease lapsed — and **every attempt at the record is cut at that window's end**. One renewal the store does not answer costs a keep nothing: the next is still asked inside the window, with an interval to answer in. A keep its lease no longer covers uploads and records nothing more, stamps the capture refused — or unresolved where a record of it was already sent and not answered, which the turn's end and a relaunch ask again — and says so (`executor_session_fenced`, and `executor_session_not_kept` as every capture not kept does). What the fence cannot reach is a record the taker had already handed on — to a data node, or to its connection's buffer while the broker was unreachable — that the log takes only after a peer has read the turn's row: on the turn's end the log orders the two itself (a record it took before the end is kept, one after it is refused by the end's tombstone). **That is decided, and nothing is recorded on the log for it:** a refusal ordered there, as the end's tombstone is, would have the log refuse the late capture and lose a session, and no session history is given up for a stamp. So a late capture the log takes before the turn's end is kept and read as any other — the turn's row names it, a session read and the conversation's next turn take it — and one decision alone takes the stamp's word over the log's: the relaunch that follows the job whose capture was settled, which continues only what the run's record keeps, and the record never takes a refusal back, so every attempt at it starts without that capture — the one whose wait settled it and one asked again after the record landed alike — and a seat's [sessions read](../reference/api-endpoints.md#executor-sessions) says of that relaunch that the log keeps the capture, not that it was not kept, as the relaunch's own reason does, which reads the turn's row for a capture its record refused — and the run that relaunch starts is told the turn's own session is there to read with `read_session`. Nothing else does: the end names it unkept at the row's own place, which releases nothing, and every account of how the turn ended (the sessions read's, the seat's own `list_sessions` and `read_session`, and the one the conversation's next turn is told) says a session was not kept only where that capture is newer than the row's own place. The end, decided off the run's record, also says the turn [went on past](turn-engine.md#how-a-turn-ends-on-the-executor-log) the session its row names; so where that capture is the turn's **last word** — the final capture of its last launch that ran a job — it names it so (`last_word`), and every reader holds the row's place to it: the row that holds it is read as all the turn did, as one whose final capture landed is, so no account says the turn went on past it and the conversation's next turn is led through that turn. That holds whichever side of the end's decision the late record landed on, since the log took it before the end either way. That relaunch starts without it as one does that will not wait out a slow keep ([below](#a-relaunch-continues-the-turns-session)): in the session the turn's first launch was handed, where the turn has kept nothing and its row still held that session when the relaunch read it — the late record landing only after — and otherwise in a session of its own, or the one its job's box holds where it carries the run on there. The lease is not the node's presence, which answers another question: a draining node gives presence up at its first step while its keeps still upload, and a turn's end that read its captures as refused for that would tombstone the turn before the draining node's records landed, losing the turn's last session. So a stopped taker's capture is settled within one lease TTL of its last renewal — a crash, a restart, a scale-down, a lost VM, an `e2b` waiter duty's holder that died — and a draining one's is waited for until it lands. A process that holds no taker lease — its claim unanswered, or its renewals unanswered past their window — begins no capture at all. A stamp an older build wrote names no taker, and is waited for by its age alone.

**How long a read waits.** A collection on another node asks the box's host to read it ([below](#durability-completion-and-observability)), and the session is taken inside that read, before the box is paused: so the asker waits for the read's own minute **and** what a take may cost — two minutes for the pack and two for the scrub, plus a half minute for the take's own stamps and another for the read's pause and its answer — of which the host's read is given three quarters of what the asker waits: about eight minutes in all (60 s + (240 s + 60 s) / 0.75) for the collection of a turn with no wall-clock cap. A stop's or a loss's read ends the job before it takes the session ([above](#each-launchs-session-kept)), and so does the collection of a job its turn's cap passed while it still ran, so their asker waits for that end on top — its 2¼-minute wait and the kill's ten seconds, counted at the same three-quarter share — about eleven minutes in all (60 s + (145 s + 240 s + 60 s) / 0.75), which leaves the host's take its whole four minutes after an end at its bound. Every collection of a turn with a cap waits the eleven, whether or not the cap has passed on the asking node's clock: the host decides on its own, as it reads. A session of a day's work is a mebibyte or two and is taken in seconds; the bound is what a host that has truly stopped costs before its claim is handed back. The upload and the record — up to about seventeen minutes for a session at its bound at the object store's slowest accepted pace — are the keep's, after the read has answered. A stop's read and a loss's ending take the session off the frame that decided them, in the ending's own retry — the pause watch that decided a stop, and the seat's acquisition that found a loss, go on to the next run at once. **The seat's next turn does wait**: its mail is held behind every ending kept for its retry, through the job's end and the take — up to the read's eleven minutes (an uncapped turn's collection's eight) where the box's host is silent, and for a stop asked again on each retry until the box timeout gives that host up — and through the end's wait for the turn's captures to land, up to the twenty-one minutes a capture can take — about thirty-nine where a read stands by for one that does not land, through its takeover and its keep — or for a capture whose taker stopped its lease's TTL and one read of the executor log (above) — since the turn's end is recorded only after them and the conversation's next turn only after its end.

**The switch.** `turn_engine.conversation_session.enabled: false` keeps no session — nothing is taken out of a box or stamped, no launch seals what it handed its box for a scrub, no node asks another's host to read a lost run's box for one, and a relaunch continues at most the session its own box holds, never a capture kept while the switch was on, which a seat's sessions say too; and no turn reads its conversation, is told how its earlier turns ended or continues the session its last turn left — and still records how each turn ended, which is the turn's account and not its session ([Turn Engine](turn-engine.md#how-a-turn-ends-on-the-executor-log)); and a person's stop still ends the job before its box goes, in the stop's own retry, and a collection past its turn's cap still ends a job still working before it collects it, since that is how a stop and a cap end a job rather than anything kept. Whether to prepare a capture is read off the revision each node runs; whether to keep one, off the revision the fleet runs; whether to continue one, off the revision the relaunching node runs, as a capture is prepared.

## A relaunch continues the turn's session

Every relaunch of a turn — a reviewer's send-back, a person's answer carrying a parked run on, a round whose job was [lost with its host](#durability-completion-and-observability), a round refused on a sign-in replaced while it ran — **continues the turn's own session** where it can, in whatever box and cell the launch gets: the box the session was last worked in, or any other. What it continues is **the session of the job it follows** — the job its reviewer collected, the one whose question was answered, the one lost or refused — as that job's kept capture holds it ([above](#each-launchs-session-kept)), named by the turn's row of the executor log, read through the estate router at `linearizable` so a capture another node's keep recorded is seen, and opened against that row alone: the seat's and the turn's own session, never another turn's of the same conversation, whatever ended since. A turn that has kept no session of its own yet continues the one its first launch was handed instead, and a conversation's next turn continues the one its last turn left ([below](#a-conversations-next-turn-continues-its-last-turns-session)).

**Which session, and when none.** Only the job before's session holds everything the turn's jobs were told — the round's task, or the review that sent it back; a person's answer; the job's own work — so a relaunch continues that job's **newest** capture, once it is kept, and nothing older: a capture an earlier job took holds none of what the jobs after it were told. The job's capture is taken by the read that collected it and kept off that read, so a relaunch that follows at once waits for the keep — for about as long as an ordinary keep takes, fifteen seconds, and no longer: the frame that waits holds the seat and a slot of the node's admission, and a keep still under way past that is a slow store's or a stopped node's, which it does not wait out. Within that wait, what is unsettled is settled before the relaunch decides: a capture whose taker's lease has lapsed is settled from the turn's row on the log ([above](#each-launchs-session-kept)), so a relaunch that follows its taker's crash or restart continues the capture that process landed, where the log took its record before that read, rather than starting fresh beside it (one the log takes only after that read is kept on the log, and the relaunch starts without it); and a capture whose record was never answered is asked again under its own operation, as the turn's end asks it, and continued where it lands. So a relaunch continues no kept session where the job it follows left none — its box went with its host, its take or its keep was refused, its record was never answered, its keep was still under way — or where that job's newest capture was not kept, since that session went further than any kept one. It then continues, where the turn has kept no session at all, the session its first launch was handed — the one its conversation's last turn left, frozen on the run's record and held on the executor log, never read again — in a box of its own; else, as it always could, the session the job's own box holds, where a person's answer or a replaced sign-in carries the run on in the box it parked in; and starts fresh anywhere else. A keep still under way when the wait runs out is no kept capture, and goes that way too: the session its first launch was handed, where the turn has kept nothing and its row still holds it, since that holds everything a session of its own would and the conversation besides. Should that capture land after the relaunch read the row, the relaunch's own first capture supersedes it, as any newer capture does, and what that job did is lost to the relaunch whichever session it started in. And a kept session is continued only:

- by a runner that restores it: the harness that wrote it, in the archive format this build restores, written by a release whose sessions another box continues — and in a box whose harness can take it up, which the launch asks the box before it chooses the run's brief (Claude Code 2.1.310 or later, OpenCode 2.0.32 or later, never older than the release that wrote it; [Claude Code](#a-claude-code-runs-session), [OpenCode](#an-opencode-runs-session));
