A checkout is refused, before anything is written and naming the repository and why, for a repository the seat may not read, one deleted or being purged, `write` on an archived repository or on a branch the box could not push — one the seat's grant does not let it push (a seat outside the owning unit pushes only under its own `seats/<handle>/`), one only maintain moves (another seat's namespace), or, in a colleague's turn, one outside the seat's namespace that colleague could not push — a `base` the repository does not hold, a `change` the repository does not hold or that is closed or merged, an `open_change` whose target branch does not exist or is the run's own, whose title is not one line of at most 256 bytes, or that comes to a run that could not push its branch; and for every repository where the company hosts no code (`code.backend` is not `gitlet`), where this node does not serve Gitlet yet (a restart owed), or where this node has no `CREWLET_BOX_GIT_URL` — the address a box reaches Gitlet at, without which a box would be given a repository and no way to clone it (logged `sandbox_git_url_unset`). Each checkout costs a few reads at the seat's level: the repository, a `change` it names, then every ref it needs — the branch, a base named as a ref, the default branch and the branch an `open_change` proposes into — in one, and a base named by its id expanded by one read by object id. A scratch repository the run checked out is held from expiry for as long as the run is unended, whichever later launch replaced its grants (see [Gitlet](gitlet.md)).

**Its git is the engine's to configure.** Every run launched on a node that serves Gitlet gets, in its launch's own environment — minted once the job is named, never written on the run's record — its access to Gitlet, the one tool-specific access the engine gives a box itself:

- `CREWLET_GIT_TOKEN`, the run's credential: a token naming the turn and THIS launch, so a job a later launch of the turn replaced is refused at once, whatever its token's expiry, and which reaches nothing until a checkout grants a repository; and `CREWLET_GIT_URL`, the launching node's `CREWLET_BOX_GIT_URL`, which every clone's remote names.
- git's configuration through the environment (`GIT_CONFIG_COUNT`, `GIT_CONFIG_KEY_<n>`, `GIT_CONFIG_VALUE_<n>`), the run environment's own entries **kept** — a proxy or a CA bundle an operator configured this way — and seven of the engine's appended after them: an empty `credential.<base>/.helper`, which clears every credential helper configured before it for Gitlet's URL, so a `credential.helper store` a setup step configured is neither asked for the run's token nor told to save it; a helper answering git's request for a credential with the run's token, read from the environment when git asks, so the token is never written to a file; `user.name` and `user.email`, the seat's identity exactly as a commit the engine builds for it names it — its role's name and `email:`, else its handle on `code.identity_domain`; `protocol.version 2`, which partial clones and fetches by id are served under; and `index.version 4` with `core.untrackedCache true`, the index settings a wide checkout's `git status` was [measured](#what-a-clone-costs) faster with — what `feature.manyFiles` sets, but not its `index.skipHash`, which writes an index older libgit2 builds refuse — for every repository the box's git touches. The engine's come last, so for the identity, the protocol and the index its value wins. A run environment whose own `GIT_CONFIG_COUNT` is not a count, or names an entry it does not set, is refused naming it: git refuses such a configuration, and every clone would fail.

The identity is a **convention** a cooperating box keeps, never a check: generated code can set any committer it likes, the engine's own included, and the push's record, never a commit header, says which run pushed what. The names the engine sets — `CREWLET_GIT_TOKEN` and `CREWLET_GIT_URL`, and what a [check's box](#check-boxes) is told of its check — are **reserved**: `role.sandbox.env`, every setup step's `env` and the company's check secrets (`code.checks.env`) are refused naming one, since a value there would break a box's clones or tell a check's command another check than the one it runs. A run launched where the company hosts no code, or on a node that does not serve Gitlet, gets none of this.

**How a box is given one.** Nothing is cloned at launch. A checkout's answer carries the commands that give the box the repository, which the agent runs in its working directory, in order, as it would any command: a check of the box's git, then the repository's clone, and in an `e2b` box the monitor's step below. They run in the box's own shell, with the job's environment, so they reach Gitlet with the job's own credential, and they cost the run what they take only when it asks for the repository: a run that needs one repository of eight clones one. An executor's clone is:

- **blobless** (`git clone --filter=blob:none --no-checkout`): every commit and tree of the history it clones, each file's contents fetched as git needs them — the agent rebases, logs and blames, so it needs the history, and rarely every file of it;
- of the **default branch alone** (`--single-branch`): the history of the branch the repository's `HEAD` names, with the tags into it, as a local branch and `origin/<default>`, and **no other branch**. Most of a repository's branches are other seats' work, a branch for each work item they push ([a seat's own branches](gitlet.md#who-may-read-and-push)), and a box sent all of them would carry every seat's commits and trees and pull every other seat's next push on its own `git fetch`, its clone growing with the company's work rather than with its base. The clone's remote tracks the default branch alone, so a later `git fetch` or `git pull` in the box moves that branch and nothing else, and another branch is fetched by name (`git fetch origin <branch>`, which leaves it at `FETCH_HEAD`), which the environment block tells the agent. git still lists every branch's and tag's name to a clone — about eighty bytes a ref — and the host sends no object of a branch the clone does not want: [measured](#what-a-clone-costs), a box beside ten thousand seats' branches receives the objects it receives beside none, and 0.8 MB more of listing;
- narrowed to its **cone** where one is named (`git sparse-checkout set --cone`), so only those directories and the top level's files are checked out and fetched — a directory whose name starts with `!` or holds a glob character is taken as the directory it is;
- at its **base**, fetched by id — a base off the default branch, such as the tip of the seat's own branch a run continues or a deleted branch's commit, arrives like any other, with only what the clone lacks sent — on its **branch**, made there (`git checkout -B`);
- never **treeless**, which would carry every commit of the history and fetch each tree a walk touches one request at a time, and never from a **bundle URI**, which a filtered client still downloads whole.

An empty repository is cloned with its branch named as the unborn `HEAD`, so the run's first commit starts it. The checkout's answer tells the harness, after the commands, what they make true: where the checkout is, on which branch from which commit, that the clone holds the default branch alone and how another is fetched, its cone, and how it pushes — or, for a repository given to read, that a push is refused. A run is given no repository at launch, so its environment block names none.

**git's file-system monitor, in an `e2b` box only.** After the clone, a checkout in a box in a cell that runs on a machine of its own — `e2b` — is answered one more command, `git monitor`, which turns git's file-system monitor on (`core.fsmonitor`, in each checkout's own configuration) so a status or a commit asks a daemon what changed rather than reading every file of the tree — [measured](#what-a-clone-costs). It does so where the box's git is 2.55.0 or newer and every checkout at the top of the box's working directory — an earlier job's in a reused box too — takes at most **half** of what the kernel lets the box's user hold: their directories together half its inotify watches (`fs.inotify.max_user_watches`, or the box's own user namespace's lower limit), and their count half its inotify instances, a daemon each. Otherwise it turns the monitor off in the box's checkouts, stopping any daemon an earlier checkout started; either way the step never fails. The half is headroom, because a daemon watches every directory its work tree comes to hold — a build's output and `node_modules` included, ignored or not — and a daemon the kernel refuses a watch dies, after which **every** git command starts it again, walks the tree until it is refused again, and reads the tree as it would have without one: slower than no monitor at every command (measured below), while taking the user's remaining watches each time. A `direct` or `container` box is **never** answered the step: it runs on the engine host as the engine's own user, or in a user namespace that user made, whose watches the kernel charges to that user all the same, so its daemons would spend the budget the other seats' harnesses and their file watchers share, of which no box can see the rest.

**Where the checkout is.** At `./<name>`, in the directory the box's commands start in — the box's workspace on the [local backends](#local-sandboxes) — which is where the harness starts too, so the clone and the agent agree on the path without either naming it.

**A reused box keeps its checkouts.** A later launch of the turn that reattaches to the box an earlier one left applies nothing to it — neither the operator's setup nor any clone — so the checkouts an earlier job made, and the agent's work in them, are there as it left them. A round carried on keeps its job's grants, and checking one of them out again answers commands whose own guard skips a repository whose checkout is already there; a box that lost its clone — a fresh one the carried round was launched in — is given it again by the same call, cloned at its branch's head, so it carries on from everything the job before it pushed. A command that fails is the run's to read, as any command's is: git's own words, and the version check's sentence below.

**And none of the files the job before was started with.** Before a job starts in a reused box, the box itself removes what the job before it was handed and left behind: its brief, standing instructions, settings and MCP configuration, its markers and its session's pieces, never the harness's own session store, which the new job continues. The new job's own files are then written as new ones. On a `container` box whose home is mounted across a VM, as Docker Desktop mounts it, that is what lets the harness read them as it starts: the box keeps its entry for a file the engine replaces from the host side for about a second, and a harness that opens the file in that second finds nothing there.

**The box's git.** It must be **2.40 or newer**, Gitlet's floor for every client. The check is the first command a checkout answers, and it stops the clone, naming the git it found and the floor, where the template or image has no git or an older one — Apple's bundled git is 2.39 — rather than leaving the clone to fail on a protocol that git cannot speak. A box image or template is the operator's to keep current.

### What a run pushed is reported for it

A run's own report says what it delivered in its `Delivered:` lines, and a box writes its report as it likes. What it pushed to **Gitlet** is not taken from the report: every push the box makes is recorded under the run that made it ([Gitlet → Coding boxes](gitlet.md#coding-boxes)), and when the run is collected the engine reads those records and writes what each ref became on the collected executor's record, which the reviewer reads beside the run's own refs. So the harness is told, in its standing instructions, that its pushes to the repositories it may push to are reported for it, and asked for `Delivered:` lines only for what it delivered somewhere else.

**What the reviewer reads.** After the run's own `Delivered:` line, one line per ref the run moved, in the order it first pushed each, every repository in the order the run was given them:

```
Pushed to Gitlet (its push record):
- web refs/heads/seats/swe/ENG-42 1a2b3c4d5e6f → 4d5e6f7a8b9c (3 pushes), change web!42
- web refs/heads/seats/swe/spike created at 9f8e7d6c5b4a
- api refs/heads/seats/swe/old deleted (it was 0a1b2c3d4e5f)
```

Each ref is **what it became**, never every step it took: the old value of the run's first push of it and the new value of its last, and how many of the run's pushes moved it — a rebase loop is fifty pushes of one branch, and the seat acts on where the branch ended up. A branch that heads a live [change](gitlet.md) once the run is collected names it — the change the run delivered its work to, whether it was launched on it, [opened it](#a-runs-changes), or anybody opened it — so the change is reported rather than a branch. A command Gitlet refused moved nothing and is left out. The list shares the run's own refs' bound — 16 KiB of lines for both, the run's own first — and stops at the first ref that does not fit, the rest counted (`- and N more ref(s) its push record names, not listed here`).

**Never silence.** A run given a repository it may push to is always told something: the refs, or `nothing — no push of this run was recorded`, or, where the record could not be read, `unknown`, why, and the branches to read with `code_resolve` before reporting them — never an omission a reader would take for "nothing pushed". A record that cannot be read does **not** hold the run back: the collection goes on and says so (logged `sandbox_pushes_unread`), since a collection retried until Gitlet answers would hold a finished run's seat for as long as Gitlet is down. One stopped by the collecting node itself — a drain, a seat moving — is the collection interrupted, and is handed back and collected again. A run given its repositories only to read is told it could push none, with no read at all.

**What it costs.** One read a collected run that could push: the first page **linearizable**, so "nothing" is never a stale answer, and every later page at `session` at the first page's position, so no node behind that snapshot answers one. The read scans the run's own repositories' pushes since its launch rather than keeping an index of every box push on every data node ([Gitlet](gitlet.md#coding-boxes)), and the engine holds at most 1 024 of the refs it finds — more than a record can ever list — counting the rest, so a box that pushes in a loop costs the collecting node no more memory than a record can carry. Past those, a ref the engine had no room to hold is counted once for every push that moved it.

Naming the changes costs one more read and no second barrier: one `branch_changes` read of every branch the run pushed, on every repository it was given to push to, and of the branch of each change it asked to open — one index seek a branch, all of them in one transaction (a run that pushed more than a thousand branches is read in two) — at `session` at the record's first page's position. That page's barrier already came after every push the run made and every change those pushes opened, so the read answers what a linearizable one would, and a collection appends one barrier to the code log, the record's, however many repositories it names. A read that fails names no change and holds nothing back (logged `sandbox_changes_unread`).

**Where it is recorded.** The refs ride the run's own phase record and the collected executor's as `pushed_refs` — each `{repo, ref, old, new, pushes, change}`, with the whole object ids and `change` the change the branch heads (`web!42`, absent for none) — beside `pushed_refs_elided` (how many more the record names) and `pushed_refs_unread` (why it could not be read). A run that parks on a question has them on its own record, and the run that carries on after the answer is collected again for what it pushes since.

### A run's changes

A run works on a change when one of its checkouts names one — a repository's `change`, by its number or its reference — and is given the change's own source branch from the head it holds, so every push it makes is the change's next revision and its reviewers see the work as it lands. It **opens** one when a checkout asks — a repository's `open_change`, `{target, title, draft}` — and the engine opens it when it collects the run, from the branch the run delivered:

| Field | What it is | Left out |
|---|---|---|
| `target` | The branch the change proposes the run's branch into, resolved at the checkout, which must exist and be another branch than the run's | The repository's default branch, as it was at the checkout |
| `title` | The change's title, one line of at most 256 bytes | The subject of the commit the run delivered its branch at — as `git push -o change` titles its change — else the branch's name made one line, else `commit` and the commit's short id: an ask is never left unopened for its title |
| `draft` | Open it as a draft, not yet asking to be merged | Open, asking to be merged |

**At the collection, once, by the seat.** A box could open a change itself with `git push -o change` ([Gitlet](gitlet.md)), but a harness pushes as it goes, and a change opened at its first push asks its reviewers to read work in progress. So the engine opens it once the run is done — and only for a run that **finished**: it succeeded, and stopped on no question. A run that did not succeed is told it opened none and why, so the turn decides whether half the work is worth a review (`code_open_change`); a run that parks on a question opens none, and the run that carries on after the answer keeps the ask, so the run that finishes the work opens it. The change is opened as the seat the run is for — its owner and first author — from the branch's head as the push record left it, into the resolved target, carrying the work item the run was charged to and naming the turn and the run that opened it: the open a `code_open_change` of the same turn would make, decided by the repository's committer. It is held to whoever woke the turn, as the box's pushes are: in a turn a colleague seat woke, the committer judges the open for that colleague too, from its own chart when it decides the open, so a colleague a configuration change shut out of the repository while the run worked has no change opened for them — the seat's own work, a branch under its own namespace or a repository it owns, excepted, as it is for the tool ([Writes made for somebody else](gitlet.md#writes-made-for-somebody-else)). Its operation is a step of the launch's own, derived from the launch's id and the instant it began, so a collection retried — on this node or another, after a restart — asks the same open and opens nothing twice, and names the change as the one it opened. A branch that already heads a live change — the run's own `git push -o change`, or anybody's — opens no other: the run is told that change. A run asking to open changes on several repositories has them opened **at once**, each its own repository's operation, so its collection — which holds the seat — waits for the slowest open rather than for every open in turn.

**What the reviewer reads.** After the pushed refs, one line per ask:

```
Changes on Gitlet (opened at the run's collection):
- web!42 opened from seats/swe/ENG-42 for review
- seats/swe/ENG-7 already headed api!5, so no other was opened
- docs: no change opened from seats/swe/ENG-42 — the run pushed nothing to its branch, so there was no work to propose
```

A refusal is Gitlet's own sentence — a target merged into since, a branch at its target's commit — and an open whose outcome is unknown says so, and why: Gitlet could not be asked, or did not say. Each line is a fact about the open and never a step to take, since the reviewer reads it beside the seat and holds none of the code tools; neither holds the collection back (logged `sandbox_change_unopened`).

**A box's own push opens on the run's work item too.** A box's `git push -o change` opens its change in the push's own record ([Gitlet](gitlet.md)); the box's admission stamps every push it makes with the run's work item — its tracker and key, its identity and its project — so that change carries the item the run was charged to in the same shape the engine's own open gives it, and every notice about the change names the item's project either way.

### Commit trailers

A commit the run makes as itself in a coding clone carries the trailers [Commit attribution](#commit-attribution) adds to every run's own commits — the co-author line, and the seat, the run and the work item its turn is charged to — unless the company set `providers.sandbox.commit_attribution` to `false`:

```text
Co-authored-by: Crewlet <noreply@crewlet.ai>
Crewlet-Seat: swe
Crewlet-Run: 0190d8f0-1111-7000-8000-000000000001
Crewlet-Work-Item: native ENG-7
```

The clone itself writes no hook: the run's own hooks directory, which its environment points git at, is what adds them, so a repository's `core.hooksPath` (husky's) neither takes them away nor is taken away by them. `Crewlet-Run` is the turn the run belongs to, the one its page is addressed by (Live › Executors), and a push's record names the same turn beside the job that made it.

The trailers are a **convention**, never a check: a box can unset the entry, delete the hook or type any trailer it likes, and nothing in the engine reads a pushed commit's trailers. The push's own **record is the provenance** — its seat, its run, its harness, its turn, its work item and its model, every one stamped from the run's record when its box was admitted, whatever its commits say — and a trailer is best-effort provenance only where that record does not reach: in a mirror of the repository on another host, which carries commits and never push records, and for a commit older than the push history's 181 days, after which a trailer its box kept is the one note of the run and the work item left in git itself. A mirror publishes them: the seat's handle, the turn id (an identifier that grants nothing) and the work item's key go wherever the commit goes. A commit the engine **builds** — `code_commit_files`, a revert, a cherry-pick — carries none: its message is the one its caller wrote, its author already the seat or person who acted and its committer the engine (see [Gitlet](gitlet.md)), so past the push history's horizon such a commit names its seat but not its turn or work item.

### What a clone costs

Measured from the box's side (`internal/gitlet/host`'s `BenchmarkBoxClone`, `BenchmarkBoxWorktree` and `BenchmarkBoxDissociate`): a box launched as the engine launches one — its run given the repository, its token minted for that launch, its requests on `/box/git/`, its steps and its git run through its shell under exactly the environment the engine gives it — against a Gitlet node holding the repository warm, its multi-pack-index, bitmap and commit-graph written. On two E-cores of an Intel Core Ultra 7 265U, Go 1.27.2, git 2.56.0 (the node pin, which the image builds) in the node and the box unless a figure names the client floor's 2.40.4, over the engine's in-memory object store (a warm node serves a clone from its own disk; no real bucket was measured), one count of each arm: ten operations, one at a gigabyte, five for the borrowing (`make bench BENCH_PKGS=./internal/gitlet/host BENCH_PATTERN=BoxClone BENCH_TIME=10x`, and so for each). Every history and tree is the benchmarks' own seeded one, files of 4 KiB in directories of 48.

**The two recipes**, a box cloning a history of 100 MiB (44 800 commits; the tip's 138 files in three directories) and of 1 GiB (455 680 commits; 1 421 files), the mean of the arm's clones:

| Recipe | 100 MiB, 2.56.0 (2.40.4) | What the node sent, and the box holds | 1 GiB | What the node sent, and the box holds |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `coding`, the whole tree | 1.25 s (0.94 s) | 16.7 MB, 0.57 MB of it the checkout's files; 134 538 objects; 345 ms of the node's CPU | 14.6 s | 179 MB, 5.8 MB of files; 1 368 461 objects; 4.3 s of CPU |
| `coding`, one directory's cone | 1.03 s (1.19 s) | 16.3 MB, 0.20 MB of files; 134 448 objects; 295 ms | 13.3 s | 173 MB, 0.20 MB of files; 1 367 088 objects; 3.5 s |
| `single-sha`, the whole tree | 0.13 s (0.23 s) | 0.57 MB; 143 objects; 74 ms | 0.99 s | 5.9 MB; 1 453 objects; 0.83 s |
| `single-sha`, one directory's cone | 0.10 s (0.15 s) | 0.20 MB; 53 objects; 33 ms | 0.13 s | 0.23 MB; 80 objects; 52 ms |

Each recipe is two fetches: the clone's (the check's one commit) and the checkout's one lazy fetch of every file it writes, so a build that reads the whole checkout asks git for nothing more. **A coding clone is its history's commits and trees**: a cone narrows only the files the checkout writes, and leaves the clone as long — 13.3 s against 14.6 at a gigabyte — so `paths` is for a tree too wide to write, never for a long history. At about fourteen seconds a gigabyte of history on these two cores, a coding clone of a repository at the 20 GiB `repo_max_bytes` default is about five minutes, inside the ten minutes a setup step is given; at a gigabyte it is well inside a fifth of them, so a run's brief says nothing of a history's size, and the step's timeout stays.

**Beside other seats' branches.** The coding recipe at 100 MiB on the pin, its whole tree, with 0, 1 000 and 10 000 other seats' branches beside the default branch — three commits off its tip each, a work item's few, pushed a thousand branches a push so their packs stand beside the node's indexed one as recent pushes do — against a control that clones as git does unless told otherwise, wanting every branch it is shown; three clones an arm (`BENCH_PATTERN=BoxClone/size=100MB/seats= BENCH_TIME=3x`):

| Other seats' branches | The recipe: the default branch alone | The control: every branch |
|---|---|---|
| none | 1.08 s; 16.7 MB sent, 0.4 KB of it the listing; 134 538 objects; 336 ms of the node's CPU | — |
| 1 000 | 1.06 s; 16.8 MB, 79 KB the listing; 134 538 objects; 344 ms | 1.19 s; 18.7 MB; 143 538 objects; 384 ms |
| 10 000 | 2.58 s; 17.5 MB, 799 KB the listing; 134 538 objects; 441 ms | 2.41 s; 26.7 MB; 224 538 objects; 1 038 ms |

**What the recipe's box receives does not grow with the company's work**: the same 134 538 objects beside ten thousand seats' branches as beside none, the listing — about eighty bytes a branch — the only bytes that do. The control's grow with every branch, nine objects each: ten thousand branches send it 90 000 objects and 9.2 MB more that the run has no use for, at more than twice the recipe's CPU on the node, and its remote would bring every seat's next push into the box on the agent's own `git fetch`. At ten thousand branches both clones took longer, the recipe as much as the control, which neither the objects sent nor the node's CPU account for; three clones an arm, on two cores of a machine other work shared, say no more about it than that.

**The box's own git in a wide tree.** Medians of ten warm runs of `git status` and of `git commit -qam` of one changed file, and of the coding clone's checkout, in a tree of 4 KiB files in directories of 48 — on git's own index settings, on the engine's (`index.version 4`, `core.untrackedCache true`), and with git's monitor on:

| | 10 000 files | 100 000 files | 100 000, one directory's cone |
|---|---|---|---|
| status, git's own settings | 17.8 ms | 142 ms (140–148) | 21.6 ms |
| status, the engine's | 15.0 ms | 99.2 ms (98.0–101) | 19.0 ms |
| status, git's own, monitor on | 8.4 ms | 61.9 ms | — |
| status, the engine's, monitor on | 4.0 ms | 17.2 ms | — |
| status, the engine's, monitor starved | 31.0 ms | 247 ms (232–271) | — |
| commit, git's own / the engine's | 26.9 / 27.2 ms | 172 / 161 ms | 55.5 / 60.9 ms |
| commit with the monitor, git's own / the engine's | 19.0 / 18.5 ms | 88.2 / 87.4 ms | — |
| checkout, git's own / the engine's | 0.83 / 0.81 s | 15.4 / 15.6 s | 0.24 / 0.25 s |

A setting joins a box's git where, at 100 000 files with no cone, it cuts a warm status by at least a quarter, by more than the spread of the runs (the tenth to the ninetieth percentile, in brackets), and lengthens the checkout by at most a tenth:

- **The index settings are the engine's** — entries of its git environment, above: a status 30 % faster (142 to 99 ms), the checkout 1.5 % longer (15.4 to 15.6 s).
- **The monitor is the checkout's**, where the box can keep it: a status 57 % faster (142 to 62 ms, and 17 ms beside the index settings), a commit 49 % (172 to 88 ms), the checkout itself untouched; the first status starts the daemon and pays for it, 417 ms at 100 000 files (70 ms at 10 000). Only in the checkout's own configuration, never the box's environment, which would start a daemon for every repository the box's git touches. Read on 2.56.0 and on 2.55.0, a daemon stops within seconds of its checkout being removed.
- **A monitor the kernel starves is worse than none**, which is why only a box with a kernel of its own is given one, and only while its checkouts take half of what that kernel lets it watch ([above](#repositories-on-gitlet)). With the box's git allowed half as many watches as the checkout has directories — a tree grown to twice what the box may watch, as a build's output grows one — the daemon dies on the watch it is refused, and every status starts it again (one start each of ten), walks the tree until it is refused again, and then reads the tree as it would have without one: 247 ms at 100 000 files against 99 ms with no monitor and 17 ms with one that keeps up, and 31 ms against 15 at 10 000 (the `fsmonitor=starved` arm, its budget a stand-in that refuses the box's git a watch past it, as the kernel refuses a user at `fs.inotify.max_user_watches`, since lowering the kernel's own would starve every other watcher of the user running it). git says nothing of it: the status's output is the same.
- **Git's automatic maintenance is left as git has it, and a long session's lazy fetches gather as packs.** A session of an agent's commands in a blobless clone — status, grep, three of a file's changes with their patches, each pass over the files further back, the recent log, four files changed and committed — ran 200 rounds, its lazy fetches leaving 602 packs (one count of each arm, `BENCH_PATTERN=BoxWorktree/session BENCH_TIME=1x`). git 2.55.0 and 2.56.0 never repack them: their automatic maintenance, which every commit and fetch runs, behaves as `maintenance.strategy=geometric` does, and its geometric repack never fires on a blobless clone's lazy-fetch packs — promisor packs — while gc is no task of it (git 2.40.4, which runs gc after a fetch, kept the same clone at 12 packs). What the packs cost is every command's reading of them: from the first ten rounds to the last ten, a status's CPU went from 2.4 to 10.4 ms, a `log -p`'s from 49 to 157 ms and a commit's from 11 to 32 ms, over a 20 MB history and a 100 MiB one alike (2.7 to 10.4, 49 to 150 and 12 to 35 ms), in a session of 27 to 30 s of git CPU. Every bound git offers by its own configuration was measured against that, and each costs the session more than the 5 % of its git CPU a box's maintenance is held to, and more the longer the history, since each repacks or rewalks what the clone holds while the packs it removes do not grow with the history:

  | Bound, set in the checkout | Its share of the session's git CPU, 20 MB / 100 MiB | The session's git CPU with it, against 27–30 s without |
  |---|---|---|
  | gc (`maintenance.gc.enabled`): every pack repacked into one at fifty | 12.5 % / 40 % (12 gcs, 163 and 786 ms each) | 15.7 / 23.5 s |
  | gc keeping every pack of a mebibyte or more (`gc.bigPackThreshold`) | 30 % / 67 % | 20.2 / 44.8 s |
  | the incremental repack (`maintenance.incremental-repack.enabled`): a multi-pack-index and batches | 32 % / 68 % | 20.0 / 49.1 s |

  The plain gc costs the session less in all than the packs did at both sizes, its commands' cost staying flat, and running detached, as git runs it, met no command's lock in 72 commands beside it — but its own cost follows the history, five times as long a history costing five times as much a gc, so past about 150 MB of history it would cost a session more than the packs it removes. Nothing is set: the packs' cost is the reason recorded here, and whether a box's checkout should run git's gc after all is a decision for repositories of the size a company keeps.

The million-file tree's arm runs only under `CREWLET_BENCH_LARGE`, and was not measured here: the in-process store would hold its 4.1 GB of packs in memory beside the fleet and the box, past the 8 GiB the benchmarks' runner gives a run.

**Borrowing the node's copy.** A box's checkout and a build that reads every file it holds, by the coding recipe, against a clone that borrows the node's own copy of the repository and then copies it (`git clone --reference <the node's copy> --dissociate`, a repack of the whole history), five of each at 100 MiB and one at a gigabyte:

| | 100 MiB | 1 GiB |
|---|---|---|
| the coding recipe | 1.14 s; the box's git 1.03 s of CPU, the node's 0.36 s; 16.7 MB sent | 13.0 s; 12.2 s, 3.7 s; 179 MB |
| a borrowed copy | 2.59 s; 3.40 s, 3 ms; nothing sent | 47.8 s; 58.1 s, 3 ms; nothing sent |

Copying the borrowed copy costs a box several times the clone it saves the node — 3.7 times at a gigabyte, where borrowing would be worth designing only at under half — so no box copies one. A borrow kept for a run's life and never copied is not built either: a [check's box](#check-boxes) fetches its one commit as every box does, on a Gitlet node as anywhere, and lending a container that node's own copy instead is [crewlet/crewlet#496](https://github.com/crewlet/crewlet/issues/496), which would measure such a borrow against the fetch first. How boxes share a node's transfer slots with people and with CI is Gitlet's [fair share](gitlet.md#fair-share).

---

## Check boxes

A repository's [checks](gitlet.md#checks) run in boxes of this same catalogue, but a check's box is no executor's. It runs one command a repository defines — its build, its tests, its linter — against one commit, and nothing of a seat's:

- **No harness.** No harness is installed in it, no model's sign-in is in its environment, no subscription login is seeded into it, and **none of `providers.sandbox.setup` is applied**: those steps provision coding boxes for their agents, their `env` and files are often credentials, and a check runs a change's own code. A box for a check that names a harness or carries a login is refused before any box exists. What a check's box is given beyond its image is its git's floor check (the [coding box's](#repositories-on-gitlet): 2.40 or newer), its checkout, and the secrets the check names.
- **Its cell** is `container` — a container on the Gitlet node that claimed the check — or `e2b`, a remote box. A check runs in the cell its checks file names, among those the company allows (`code.checks.allow_run_in`), or else in the company's own (`code.checks.run_in`, else `providers.sandbox.default_run_in` where that is one of the two). **`direct` is refused by name**, in the company's configuration and in a repository's file alike: a check runs a change's own code, which a `direct` box would run as the engine's own user on the engine host — reading what the engine reads, the keyring every run's token is signed under among it. That is the difference from an executor, where `direct` is [warned about](#security-model) rather than refused: a seat's cell is its operator's choice for its own work, while a check's code is whatever was pushed. A check's cell is reached as a seat's is: the engine builds its backend, and a `container` needs `local.image` — one holding git and whatever the checks build with.
- **Its checkout** is its one commit, detached, on no branch, with the cone its file names (`checkout: [dirs]`) or the whole tree — or no checkout at all (`checkout: none`), for a check that reads its commit [through the API](gitlet.md#check-boxes). It is **fetched**, in a `container` cell as in an `e2b` one, as a setup step named `checkout <name>` in a failure: a repository initialised with Gitlet as its remote, the commit fetched by its id at depth one, blobless, and checked out, its files fetched as the checkout needs them. A container on a Gitlet node is not given that node's own copy of the repository to read from: its checkout is one fetch of one commit Gitlet serves, under the same [fair share](gitlet.md#fair-share) as every box's fetch, exactly as a remote box's is.

**Its environment** is the company's check secrets the check names in its file's `env` — each entry of `code.checks.env`, a `${VAR}` reference resolved when the box is made, and none it does not name — Gitlet's credential and base, as a coding box's (`CREWLET_GIT_TOKEN`, under the user name `check`, and `CREWLET_GIT_URL`) but with no commit identity, since a check acts as nobody — and what the check is:

| Variable | What it is |
|---|---|
| `CREWLET_CHECK_REPO` | The repository's name: the checkout is at `./<name>` |
| `CREWLET_CHECK_SHA` | The commit the check measures, the one checked out |
| `CREWLET_CHECK_BASE` | The commit it is measured against: the base its diff is read from |
| `CREWLET_CHECK_NAME` | The check's name in its file |
| `CREWLET_CHECK_ATTEMPT` | Which attempt of the check this is |
| `CREWLET_CHECK_API` | Where the box reads its commit without a clone (`/box/gitlet/<name>/…`), with its own credential |
| `CREWLET_CHECK_SUMMARY` | A file the check's command may write a one-paragraph summary of its result to, read when it ends |

These names are **reserved** as Gitlet's are: `code.checks.env`, `role.sandbox.env` and every setup step's `env` are refused naming one.

**How long it runs** is its file's `timeout_seconds`, held to the company's `code.checks.max_timeout_seconds` — unset, 50 minutes: a box on E2B's smallest plan lives an hour, and a check's box is given ten minutes past its timeout for its setup, so a check at the default fits the shortest remote session. A company running its checks in containers, or on a larger plan, raises it as far as six hours, the most any checks file may ask ([Configuration → Code](../getting-started/configuration.md#code)).

**How many run** is bounded per cell: a `container` box on a Gitlet node by that node's `gitlet.max_checks`, and an `e2b` box company-wide, by `code.checks.remote` — at most `max_running` at once (unset, 16) and `box_hours_per_day` hours of them in a UTC day (unset, 192). A check either cap holds back waits queued, claimed by nobody, until there is room ([What a check costs](gitlet.md#what-a-check-costs)).

---

## Commit attribution

The recipe above commits as the seat, so the seat is each commit's author. On top of that, every commit a run makes as itself credits Crewlet, and names the seat, the run and the work item it was made for:

```
Co-authored-by: Crewlet <noreply@crewlet.ai>
Crewlet-Seat: swe
Crewlet-Run: 6f0c2a41-…
Crewlet-Work-Item: native ENG-7
```

- **`Co-authored-by`** is the trailer GitHub turns into a participant on the commit. The address belongs to Crewlet's own GitHub account, so the commit shows Crewlet beside the seat that authored it. It is not `Signed-off-by`: that line certifies the Developer Certificate of Origin, which only whoever answers for the code can do, and it credits nobody.
- **`Crewlet-Seat`** is the seat's handle, and **`Crewlet-Work-Item`** the work item its turn is charged to, as its tracker and key (`native ENG-7`, `jira OPS-12`; the tracker alone for an item with no key), or `none` for a turn on no item — written rather than left out, so a commit the run rewrites keeps no other run's item. Each value is encoded as one word of printable characters: a vendor's key holding a space, a newline or a `%` is written as `%XX`, and decodes back to itself.
- **`Crewlet-Run`** is the turn the run belongs to, the one its page in the dashboard is addressed by (Live › Executors), so every launch of one turn names the same run. Ask Crewlet's deep work is no seat's and has no such page, so its commits carry the co-author line alone.

It is on unless `providers.sandbox.commit_attribution` is `false`, which turns all four off for every run the company launches from then on: a company that wants no Crewlet credit in its history wants no Crewlet trailer in it either. The trailers are a convention, never a check — nothing in the engine reads them, and on Gitlet a push's record is the provenance ([Commit trailers](#commit-trailers)).

**Git adds the credit, not the model.** Each run's box is given a `prepare-commit-msg` hook, and the run's environment points git at it: one more `GIT_CONFIG_COUNT` entry setting `core.hooksPath`, added after the box's login profile is read. Nothing is written to a git configuration file, so your setup recipe's `git config --global` and the repository's own settings stay as they are. Git runs `prepare-commit-msg` on every commit, including one made with `--no-verify`, so the credit does not depend on the agent remembering an instruction. A harness may add its own line as well, such as Claude Code's `Co-Authored-By: Claude`; the two sit side by side.

**The repository's own hooks still run.** The run's hooks directory stands in for the repository's while the run works, so every hook in it hands on to the repository's hook of the same name, from `.git/hooks` or from the repository's own `core.hooksPath` (husky's, for example). A repository hook that refuses a commit still refuses it.

**Only the run's own commits.** A commit is credited only when its author is the identity the run commits as. A person's commit that the run amends, rewords, cherry-picks or carries through a rebase keeps its author and gets no credit, because Crewlet did not write it. A commit of the run's own that it amends, rebases or cherry-picks carries **one of each** Crewlet trailer, naming the run that rewrote it, as git itself replaces the committer — never a second set beside the first; the co-author line is added only where it is not already there, so a person the commit already credits stays credited. A message git would refuse as empty, an untouched template, and a message given whole whose every line starts with the comment character (`git commit -m '#42 fix'`) are left to git and carry none.

**What it does not reach:**

- A commit made through a code host's API, such as a GitHub tool that writes a file, runs no git in the box.
- A box whose git is older than 2.31 does not read `GIT_CONFIG_COUNT`, so its commits go uncredited.
- A hook the repository keeps under a name git did not have when this build shipped is not handed on while the run's entry stands.

The credit never stops a commit on its own account: a hook lookup that fails hands nothing on and credits nothing, and a credit that cannot be written lets the commit through with a one-line warning.

---

## Credentials and the run environment

The run env injected into each job is assembled by the engine at launch (`internal/engine/sandbox.go`), and `internal/sandbox/launch.go` adds the telemetry variables. The engine contributes **no credential** of a tool's but the agent's own at an integration it is on at ([below](#an-agents-own-integration-credentials)), only these facts and settings, and for Gitlet: a run launched where it is served is given **access to that surface**, its run's credential and git configuration, which reach exactly the repositories it checks out, as every run is given the tool bridge's endpoint: a credential the box acts as, scoped to the run and dying with it (see [Repositories on Gitlet](#repositories-on-gitlet)). Every other host's credential is still the operator's to declare:

- **The model's entry**, the role's resolved `providers.llm` entry (the chain above), whose own harness is the seat's runner — a launch passes over an entry whose harness has none: its whole `env`, as every call the entry makes on the engine host is given it, and whatever authenticates that harness in its environment — the headless token in the profile's own variable (`CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN`) under `subscription`, the entry's key in the profile's `api_key_env` (`ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` for Claude Code) under `api-key`, the variables `inherit-env` forwards, and, for `opencode`, the credential-named `env` keys of the provider its model names. So a gateway's `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` and the `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN` it takes, a `CLAUDE_CODE_USE_BEDROCK` or `CLAUDE_CODE_USE_VERTEX` switch and the region beside it, or a proxy reach the executor's run in every cell, a remote one included. They are the values the entry's own calls use, its `${VAR}` references resolved through the secret store and the environment when the configuration was applied, with what its `auth.mode` removes from those calls removed here too (a `subscription` entry's `api_key_env`, an `api-key` entry's `token_env`); a value that resolved to nothing is left out. What tunes a call on the engine host stays there: the host's own allowlisted variables, the seat home's isolation (`HOME`, the XDG directories, the harness's configuration directory and switches — a box sets its own) and the profile's fixed `env`. Its login — credential files, or OpenCode's exported login — travels only to a [local](#local-sandboxes) box, never to a remote one.
  **A seat that signs Claude Code in itself gets the entry's `env` without its sign-in.** Claude Code has several sign-ins under different names and ranks them — `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` above a plan's `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN`, and a Bedrock, Vertex AI or Foundry toggle above both — so the precedence below, which is by name, cannot see that a seat is already signed in. When `role.sandbox.env` or a setup step carries any of `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN`, `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN`, `CLAUDE_CODE_USE_BEDROCK`, `CLAUDE_CODE_USE_VERTEX` or `CLAUDE_CODE_USE_FOUNDRY`, a Claude Code run carries that and none of the entry's sign-in: none of those six, not the profile's token or key variable, and no [credential-named](llm-backends.md#6-a-provider-key-in-env-opencode-hermes-pi) variable of the entry's `env` (a cloud's own key, such as `AWS_BEARER_TOKEN_BEDROCK`) — so a seat on its own plan is never moved onto the company's metered key or its cloud. The rest of the entry's `env` still lies beneath the seat's: a base URL or a region says where the run's requests go, not whom they are made as, so a seat on its own token still reaches its model through the company's gateway, and a seat meant to bypass it names `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL` itself. OpenCode reads one key per model provider, so there a variable the seat names simply wins over the entry's under the same name.
  **An entry's `env` and its key sit in every box run on that entry**, remote cells included, readable by the harness, which runs with every permission. What the run reports back is passed through the engine's redaction, but that matches known credential **shapes** (the vendors' `sk-…` prefixes and the like), not values: a key of no recognisable shape that the agent prints into its report or transcript is carried into the phase record as printed. Give a box a key scoped to the work — a provider project with a spend limit, a gateway token of its own — rather than the company's broadest one.
- **Agent identity** — `CREWLET_AGENT_HANDLE` and `CREWLET_AGENT_EMAIL`, the per-launch facts static config cannot know, which setup recipes map into tool shape (git commit identity above).
- **The agent's own integration credentials**, for an integration it is on at: see [An agent's own integration credentials](#an-agents-own-integration-credentials).
- **An integration's box defaults**, the non-secret settings a CLI in the box reads beside the seat's own credentials (Scaleway's organization and default project, Google Cloud's default project), for a seat with one of that integration's lanes on. They are the lowest layer of the box's environment: a setup step's `env`, the seat's own `sandbox.env`, the agent identity and the model entry's `env` each replace one. None ever counts as the harness's sign-in, and one named like a sign-in variable of the run's harness (a Claude Code cloud toggle, say) never reaches the box, so a cloud CLI's region or token file cannot switch, strip or stand in for it. A default whose reference does not resolve is left out and said, as an identity fact is, and never holds the run.
- **An integration's box files**, each a file at a path under the box's home holding one `${VAR}` reference whole, such as a token a CLI reads from a file, or literal text written as it is, such as a CLI's configuration, for the same seats. The launch writes them, resolved through the seat's own resolution (so a credential issued at use is issued for it), in a setup step of their own ahead of every other, never into the environment, so none counts as a sign-in either; a path a setup step writes is that step's, and a file whose reference does not resolve is left out and said, and never holds the run. A file the integration marks as rewritten is kept current by the completion poll, as a [token file](../integrations/github.md#acting-as-the-app-the-seats-token) is, for as long as the launch runs. A file may also name the variable its CLI finds it through: the job is handed that variable set to the file's absolute path in its box, joined onto the home of the box it runs in (a `direct` box's home is minted per box, so no configured value could name it), as the lowest layer of the job's environment, beneath even the box defaults, so a seat's own line for the name wins. It never counts as a sign-in, and it is set only for a file the box holds: one the launch writes, or a rewritten one the poll writes once its credential is issued. The value each file is launched with is taken out of the run's kept sessions by value, as everything a launch hands its box from a credential position is ([What is scrubbed](#each-launchs-session-kept)), and so is each value the poll writes since, which it records beside the run's record before it writes the file. Any of them in what else the run hands back — its report, its transcript, its live output — is taken out only where it has a shape redaction knows ([Security model](#security-model)). AWS writes its CLI profile and session file this way, Google Cloud its token file, and GitHub the agent's app token file. A file holding a reference is a credential file, the integration's: a setup step's file at its path is dropped for it, as a line under one of the agent's credential variables is. A file of literal text, such as AWS's profile, is a setting, and a setup step's file at its path wins.

Everything else tool-specific comes from config: external-service tokens (registry tokens, a test `DATABASE_URL`, a personal access token for a seat off at its code host, …) are declared in `role.sandbox.env` or a setup step's `env` and merely `${VAR}`-resolved: the engine never extracts, names, or special-cases them. One variable is the engine's to answer rather than the store's: a seat's own `${GITHUB_APP_TOKEN_<HANDLE>}`, the installation token of its GitHub App, issued for this seat's run alone and good for at least 50 minutes from launch ([Acting as the app](../integrations/github.md#acting-as-the-app-the-seats-token)). Precedence, later wins: the model's entry (its `env` and its sign-in), then agent identity, then setup-step env, then `role.sandbox.env`, then the agent's own integration credentials, then non-secret OTel values; and, over every one of them, every launch on a node that serves Gitlet gets the engine's access to it, before the run has checked anything out, whose names configuration reserves, with git's configuration appended after any of your own `GIT_CONFIG_*` entries rather than replacing them.

**A seat's own integration credential in its box is a tool's, never the harness's sign-in.** A line whose value references one of the agent's own credentials (its sealed `${<KIND>_<FIELD>_<HANDLE>}` names, or the variable its `mcp_env` block or role credential field names for an integration), such as doctl's `DIGITALOCEAN_ACCESS_TOKEN: "${DIGITALOCEAN_TOKEN_SRE}"`, reaches the box like the rest of the seat's `env` and is never counted as the run's sign-in, so the remote-box check does not pass an `opencode` run on it. And it may not sit under a name a member of the seat's executor chain signs in by: its token or key variable, a Claude Code `sign_in_env` name, or, for `opencode`, a credential-named variable of the entry's `env`. In one box the harness would make its model calls with the agent's tool credential, so such a line is refused on submit and reported as a warning on a stored document. Reach that tool through its MCP server instead, or run the seat's executor on an entry that signs in by another name.

**A reference that resolves to nothing is left out, and said.** A `${VAR}` reference that resolves to nothing — whole (`"${TOKEN}"`) or embedded (`"Bearer ${TOKEN}"`), unset or set to an empty value — is never handed to the box as an empty string, by the rule every tool server is held to ([Tools & MCP](../guides/tools-and-mcp.md#shared-vs-per-role-servers)): its key is left out of the run environment whole. The run is **not refused** for it: it is the seat's turn itself, and refusing it would fail every message the seat could answer over its tool bridge for a token a pass may mint in a minute. It starts without what is missing, and its start logs **`sandbox_env_unresolved`** naming the keys, the files and the variables (never a value or a body), because a run handed no code-host token clones nothing and pushes nothing, and that line is how an operator finds out why. It says why the value is missing: at info while a [provisioning pass](integration-reconcile.md) is minting it for the seat — the same `${VAR}` an `mcp_env` code-host credential points at, for example; a run launched once its seal reaches the node is given it — or while the seat's GitHub App is not yet created or installed, for its own `${GITHUB_APP_TOKEN_<HANDLE>}`, whose token file a running job is handed by the first poll after the app can issue one; and as a warning, with the remedy, where nothing will mint it, that pass cannot until somebody acts, or GitHub didn't issue the seat's token ([each story](#failure-modes) is told under its own field). The engine's own identity facts never refuse a run: each is contributed when it has a value, and a seat `email:` naming a variable nothing sets is left out of `CREWLET_AGENT_EMAIL` and said on the same line, so a setup step's `git config user.email` reading it empty has a reason beside it.

A setting a run can go without — a test `DATABASE_URL`, a proxy — is declared by its **key** under `optional_keys`, beside the `env` that writes it: on `role.sandbox`, or on a setup step, where a file's path may be listed too. Such a key whose value does not resolve is left out of the run environment whole, never handed on half-substituted, as any other is — but quietly, since its absence is expected, so the line names only the gaps that are not:

```yaml
roles:
  - name: Backend
    sandbox:
      env:
        GITLAB_TOKEN: "${SWE_GITLAB_TOKEN}"            # expected: a run started without it has no code-host
                                                       #   identity, and sandbox_env_unresolved names it
        DATABASE_URL: "postgres://${TEST_DB_HOST}/app"
      optional_keys: [DATABASE_URL]                    # left out when TEST_DB_HOST resolves to nothing
```

The declaration that counts is the one beside the value that won: a key in `role.sandbox.env` replacing a setup step's is judged by `role.sandbox.optional_keys`, never by the step's. A value a provisioning pass is still minting for the seat, or the seat's own GitHub App token before its app can issue one, is said at info whatever the declaration says — an `optional_keys` entry does not quiet it, since it is on its way rather than expected to be absent — and the run starts without it; a run launched after its seal reaches this node, or after the app is installed, is given it. An entry that leaves nothing out — a key or path its block does not write, a variable's name where its key belongs, or a key whose values reference nothing — is refused on the write that adds it (an [admission rule](configuration.md#what-a-stored-revision-is-held-to)), because the key it was meant for stays expected. Resolved values are injected only into that sandbox's process env and die with it; the DB stores only the `${VAR}` references. Shared infrastructure secrets — above all the OTLP backend's ingest token — never enter the sandbox at all (see below).

### An agent's own integration credentials

An integration that gives an agent a credential puts it in that agent's box itself, with no line in the company document, for as long as the agent is on at it:

| Integration | In the box | Read by |
|---|---|---|
| Expo | `EXPO_TOKEN`, the robot's sealed token | the EAS CLI |
| DigitalOcean | `DIGITALOCEAN_ACCESS_TOKEN`, the sealed token | doctl |
| Scaleway | `SCW_SECRET_KEY` and `SCW_ACCESS_KEY`, the sealed key | scw |
| GitHub | `GITHUB_TOKEN`, the app's installation token, and the same token in `~/.config/crewlet/github-token`, which the completion poll keeps current | git's credential helper and gh |
| GitLab | `GITLAB_TOKEN`, the service account's sealed token | git's credential helper and glab |
| Google Cloud | `~/.config/crewlet/gcloud-token`, named by `CLOUDSDK_AUTH_ACCESS_TOKEN_FILE` and kept current | gcloud |
| AWS | `~/.aws/crewlet-credentials.json`, read by the `crewlet` profile in `~/.aws/config` and kept current | the AWS CLI |

The sealed credentials are put there where Crewlet provisions the integration and so seals them; where the agents' accounts are made outside Crewlet, the seat's own settings name the variable that holds one. GitHub's token is issued by the engine either way, while the company runs GitHub.

- **Only the agent's own.** Each value is resolved through the seat's own resolution, so it names that agent's credential and no other seat's, and it is taken out of the run's kept session by value, as every credential the launch hands the box is ([Each launch's session, kept](#each-launchs-session-kept)), and out of everything else the run hands back where it has a shape redaction knows ([Security model](#security-model)). It never counts as the harness's sign-in. Under a name a member of the seat's executor chain signs in by (OpenCode on DigitalOcean's models reads `DIGITALOCEAN_ACCESS_TOKEN`), Crewlet withholds it from the box rather than sign the agent's model calls in with its tool token, logs `sandbox_credential_withheld` at each launch, and reports an advisory at the seat's `integrations` block.
- **On and off.** An agent turned on is given it from its next run; one turned off is given none from its next run, and its sealed credential is cleared or its token no longer issued. While a sealed credential is not there yet (a token nobody has pasted), a run starts without it and `sandbox_env_unresolved` says, at info, that the integration is minting it. A file whose credential is issued at use is written by the first poll after it can be.
- **A line of your own under the same name is ignored.** A `role.sandbox.env` line, a setup step's `env` key (the seat's own or provider-wide) or a setup file at one of these paths is dropped for an agent on at the integration, and `crewlet validate`, a configuration write and every node applying the revision report an advisory at that line. The integration's wins, never the line, because the integration's state is the one authority on which identity the box acts as: a line that won could hand the box a broader credential (Expo's provisioner token holds Admin on the account, beyond every level an agent may hold) or one that outlives turning the agent off, while the Agents tab reports the level the agent's own credential holds. A line identical to the integration's changes nothing; remove it. The settings beside a credential (Scaleway's default project, the AWS profile) are the other way round: a line of your own wins.

---

## MCP inside the sandbox

The harness is itself an MCP client, so the engine renders the seat's scoped servers into the box, beside the [tool bridge](#the-tool-bridge--a-seats-own-tools-from-inside-a-box) every box is given as the server `crewlet` (`internal/sandbox/mcprender.go` → Claude Code's `mcp.json` in the run's work directory, via `--mcp-config`, or the [configuration an OpenCode run is handed](#opencode)). Scoping is at the **server level only**: `role.sandbox.mcp.servers` names which of the role's `mcp_servers` run in the box, and the harness gets **every tool those servers expose**. A server named there is the box's: the [bridge](#the-tool-bridge--a-seats-own-tools-from-inside-a-box) serves every other MCP server the seat holds and not this one, so the run never holds one server twice under one name. Name a server here when it has to run where the checkout is (one that reads the box's files, say), and leave it to the bridge otherwise, where every call it serves is recorded as the seat's and held to its required skills. An entry naming a server the company does not declare, or one the seat is not [granted](../guides/tools-and-mcp.md#choosing-which-agents-get-a-server), is refused when the document is written, since it would give the box nothing (a stored revision carrying one gives the box nothing for it, and logs `sandbox_mcp_server_not_granted`), and removing a server takes it out of every list ([Tools & MCP](../guides/tools-and-mcp.md#removing-a-server)). There is no per-tool allowlist in a box: OpenCode has no allowlist flag and Claude Code runs `bypassPermissions` headless, so a curated list couldn't be enforced uniformly and would give a false sense of restriction. So a server whose every tool is not certain to run at `allow` ([Setting each tool's level](../guides/tools-and-mcp.md#setting-each-tools-level): one whose entry says `default: allow` and holds no tool back) is never given to a box, since the box could neither leave a blocked tool out nor ask a person before a call to a tool at `approve`: the listing is refused when the document is written, a level written on a server a box lists is refused at that box's entry, and a stored revision carrying one gives the box nothing for it and logs `sandbox_mcp_server_levelled`. A server with no `tools` at all is such a server, because each of its tools starts at the level its hints give it, most at `approve`. The seat reaches that server's tools over the bridge, where the levels hold. A role that shouldn't reach a surface simply doesn't list that server.

**Beside them, the harness starts its own.** A seat's executor is its harness as a person runs it, so the MCP servers its own configuration declares start too: the box's own user- and local-scope servers (an image's, an E2B template's, or one an earlier run in the same box added), the checked-out repository's `.mcp.json` — which a print-mode Claude Code run connects without the approval prompt it has nobody to show — a plugin's, and the claude.ai connectors of the account the run signs in with; OpenCode adds together every configuration document it reads, the image's and the repository's among them. The engine renders none of these and its bridge sees none of their calls: they act with whatever credentials their own configuration gives them, and what they reach is the cell's to bound — `container` and `e2b` keep them off the engine host, as they keep the harness. A server that does not start holds up the run's first step for at most Claude Code's `MCP_TIMEOUT` (30 s unless the run's environment sets it), and the run goes on without it. Keep the name `crewlet` for the engine: Claude Code does not document which of two servers declared under one name it keeps, so a repository declaring its own `crewlet` could take the bridge's place, and a run with no bridge cannot submit its work. Ask Crewlet's [deep work](../guides/assistant.md#what-a-run-can-reach) is the exception: it acts for a person and is held to its bridge alone (`--strict-mcp-config`).

Credentials from `role.mcp_env` are resolved into the rendered specs (HTTP servers get them as headers, stdio servers as env), so the in-box server instances authenticate as the seat. So is every `${VAR}` in the server's own `env`, `headers` and `url`: the engine resolves each through the secret store and its own environment, exactly as it does for the server's child on the engine host, and never writes a `${VAR}` reference into the box for the harness to expand — OpenCode does not read that syntax, and Claude Code would read it from the run environment, which holds a company secret only where the seat's `sandbox.env` or a setup step repeats it. (What OpenCode is handed is the resolved value, in a file of its own its configuration names — see [OpenCode](#opencode).) So a server's reference means in the box what it means on the engine host: one the secret store and the engine's environment do not answer is not answered by a value the run environment happens to carry either. The connected server names are also listed in the harness's environment brief. A server any of whose values does **not** resolve — the seat's or its own, under a key the server does not [declare optional](../guides/tools-and-mcp.md#a-key-that-may-go-without-its-value) — is not rendered at all, by the rule the server's own child follows ([Tools & MCP](../guides/tools-and-mcp.md#shared-vs-per-role-servers)): handed an empty token, an in-box server refuses to start or acts as nobody, and the agent would spend a round finding that out. A key it does declare optional is left out of what the box is given. The run logs `sandbox_mcp_server_withheld` naming the variables: at info while the integration that mints them is minting them — a run launched once the seal reaches the node, or for the agent's GitHub App token once its installation is recorded, is given the server — and as a warning where they are unset, where that integration cannot mint them until somebody acts, or where GitHub didn't issue the seat's token.

A named server the company's `mcp_servers` does not carry is **skipped**, with a warning, rather than rendered empty — the agent must never be offered a server it cannot reach, because it spends a round discovering that. The same holds for a server whose `transport` is neither `stdio` nor `http`. One skipped entry does not cost the seat the rest of its surface.

Three practical caveats:

- **A cloud sandbox cannot reach your laptop.** An HTTP MCP server (or a git host) on `localhost` / a private LAN address is unreachable from an E2B cloud box. For laptop-local development stacks, use a self-hosted E2B `domain` on the same network, a tunnel, or scope those servers out of the sandbox.
- **stdio servers must exist in the box.** A stdio spec is *spawned inside* the sandbox, so its command must be present in the template (E2B's prebuilt templates cover common cases; anything else belongs in a custom template or a setup step).
- **A container box reaches the engine host by name.** An HTTP server the engine host serves on loopback is handed to a `container` box under the runtime's name for that host ([Local sandboxes](#local-sandboxes)), so a server the engine's own child reaches at `http://localhost:3000` is reached from the box too, on Linux as long as it listens beyond loopback.

---

## The tool bridge — a seat's own tools, from inside a box

Every run is its seat's executor ([LLM Backends](llm-backends.md#the-executors-run-in-a-box)). Beside the servers above, it needs the seat's *whole* tool surface, including the engine's own builtins and the delivery tools the turn is judged on. Those cannot be shipped in: most are MCP children holding the **seat's** credentials, several are engine control, and a box is given only the credentials the seat acts as.

So the box is also given the engine: its own tools as one MCP server, `crewlet`, at the job's endpoint, and **each MCP server the seat holds as a server of its own** beneath it (`/mcp/{token}/server/{server}`), under the name the company gives it, listing that server's tools alone and carrying what the company says the server is for (its `description`) as the server's MCP instructions. Every call on every one of them comes back out:

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    BOX["The run's harness, in its box"] -->|"MCP over streamable HTTP: POST /mcp/{token}"| SURF["The seat's live tools.Surface: grant, skill guard, recording"]
```

**The dispatch is the seat's own.** A bridged call goes through the same `tools.Surface` a phase on this host calls, so the grant, the [required-skill guard](tool-skills.md), the failure shape and the recording are all the ones an operator already knows. `internal/api/mcpbridge` never grows a second idea of whether a tool is allowed — a second copy would drift, and it would drift on the security half. The MCP server behind each route is the one a run on this host reaches over its unix socket (`internal/toolsession`), kept in step with the seat's live tool set.

**Apart, because a harness keeps tools out of its requests by the server.** The run holds every tool its seat does from its launch, a seat's 50 to 150 MCP tools among them, and lists none of them in its prompt. Claude Code defers every MCP server's tools behind its tool search and keeps the one marked `alwaysLoad` in front of its model on every request, and OpenCode offers a server through Code Mode unless the server is declared `codemode: false` — so the engine's own tools, which the run needs on every request, and the seat's, which it needs a few of, are two kinds of server: `crewlet` is always loaded and offered directly, and every other server is left to the harness ([Claude Code](#claude-code), [OpenCode](#opencode)). One server holding both left a choice between sending every definition with every request and making a run search for the call that ends it, and the engine used to make its own: the seat's MCP tools reached a run only after `list_mcp_server_tools` and `activate_tool`, two rounds a harness that defers tools never needed. The servers a run is handed are fixed at its launch, from what its surface offers; a server the seat runs in its box (`role.sandbox.mcp.servers`) is reached there, under the same name, and is not served here.

**Every call is held to the run, as a phase holds its own.** What the bridge adds is what a detached run's calls are owed that a surface cannot know: the **seat's fence**, before and after each call — once this node stops holding the seat, or a person asks the seat to stop, that call and every one after it are refused rather than made as a seat the node no longer speaks for, each written on the run's record as refused; the **order the calls arrived in**, fixed as each is admitted (`Surface.Admit`) rather than as it returns, so a response's calls made at once read the same call log a re-run would; the **run's context, never the request's**, so a box that disconnects mid-call abandons only the answer and never a tool half-way through a write; a **panic contained** — the call answered as failed, logged with its stack, and the run's tools closed to it; and a **run that is over refuses** — a call reaching a session after its run ended, or after a relaunch replaced it, is answered and not run. A call whose arguments did not parse is answered with the same words a phase on this host uses, never run on an empty object — though a box's harness is read by no record, so an object the harness made of what its model wrote (Claude Code's of an array) reaches the tool as the harness sent it, and the tool's own validation is what judges it. What it does not add belongs to a tool loop — rounds, a terminator, a per-request budget — because an executor run's loop is its harness's own, its turn is parked while it runs, and those are settled when the run is collected and its turn resumes (a person's notes reach the run over the bridge too, between its steps: see [Notes to a running run](#notes-to-a-running-run)). And where the run's launch continued a session, the bridge holds the run to the session its box started, which it reads off which of the job's two tokens the box dials, before the run's first call ([Which session the box started](#which-session-the-box-started)).

**A call's answer starts at once and keeps going.** A run's harness reaches the bridge over streamable HTTP, whose server writes nothing on a call's response until the response's first event — with nothing else sent, the answer itself — and Claude Code gives up an HTTP response whose first byte has not come within a minute where the server's entry sets no timeout, answering its model that the call timed out and cancelling nothing, while the call runs on and its effect happens (measured on 2.1.310: a 90-second call given up at 60 seconds and run to its end by the bridge). So the bridge starts every call's answer the moment its tool starts, with a progress notification on the call's own progress token, and sends another every 20 seconds until the tool returns, on every server it serves the run, which answers Claude Code's other checks on a call as well (by reading its 2.1.310 bundle): one that gives up a call that has sent nothing for five minutes, or for the server's own timeout where the box's settings give one (below), and one that, checking every 30 seconds, gives up a call 90 seconds after its transport was marked lost unless a progress has cleared the mark since. That mark is routine: Claude Code loses the session's standing GET stream 360 seconds after opening it, whatever the server's timeout (measured), and marks every call then in flight, so every call that runs past that point leans on the progress that follows within 20 seconds. Measured on 2.1.310, with the bridge's bound in the server's entry: a 540-second call that ran through that loss, at 359.2 seconds, was answered whole, and the same call carrying no progress token was given up at 449.9 seconds, cancelling nothing. Each progress is one small event (116 bytes, measured). A call that lands while no node can answer it waits for one (below) with nothing written on its response, and its answer starts once it reaches the node that runs it. Both harnesses put a progress token on every call (measured on Claude Code 2.1.310 and OpenCode 2.1.4); a call answered without running — refused once the run's tools have closed, or for arguments that did not parse — gets its answer alone, and so does a request with no token. A progress the bridge can no longer write means the harness hung up on the call's response, and the call is recorded as [one nobody waited for](#a-relaunch-continues-the-turns-session).

**A box waits for a call as long as the bridge can take to answer it.** Kept going, a call keeps its harness waiting for as long as it runs, so the box is told how long that can be as each bridged server's own timeout — Claude Code's per-server `timeout`, OpenCode's `timeout.execution` — and past it the harness gives the call up and cancels it, whatever progress flows (measured on Claude Code 2.1.310 and OpenCode 2.1.4), so the bridge records it as one nobody waited for; left unset, a call that hangs would keep its run waiting out the harness's own default, Claude Code's 1e8 ms and OpenCode's twelve hours (by reading their bundles). The bound is the bridge's own, added up as the box is launched: the two seat-lease lifetimes a call may wait for a node that can answer it (90 seconds at the default `coordination.lease_ttl_seconds`, as below), the longest bound the run's tools declare — an MCP server's `request_timeout_seconds`, `delegate`'s `turn_engine.delegation.call_timeout_seconds` — never under the engine's one-call ceiling of 300 seconds, and a minute for the bridge's own work around a call: at the defaults, 1050 seconds on a seat that can `delegate` and 450 on one that cannot. The bridge cuts nothing itself: a call past every bound the engine sets is a defect or a hang, and a tool half-way through a write is the run's. A call still running as its job's session on the bridge ends waits for none of it: the end answers it at once (below).

**A job's session ends without waiting for its calls.** Every way a job's session on the bridge ends — the run settled, parked on its question, was stopped, reclaimed or cut by its cap, a relaunch replaced its job, a recovery opened the same job again, or the seat left this node while its box ran on — closes the box's MCP sessions on it, and the SDK's close of a session waits for every request in flight and writes no answer once it has begun. So the end first answers every call whose tool is still running, in its own words: where the run is over for the box, "This run has ended; this call is still running, so do not make it again. End your turn now."; where the box goes on with the seat's next holder or with this node's new session for the same job, "This call is still running; do not make it again." — neither says where the call's result goes, which would not hold for every call so answered (below). Neither is a failure, as the tool loop's own "still running" is not: a harness hands its model a failed result as a call that failed, and a model makes a failed call again, while this call is running and its effect is still to come. The end waits for those answers to be written, for the answer of a call whose tool returned just before it, and for every answer the session gave without running a call, at most five seconds, then closes the sessions — a box that does not read its answer holds the end no longer, and its sessions are closed once the answer is out or its connection ends — so the end returns once those answers are out, which a box that reads takes no time over, and a seat handed on gives its lease back then, where it used to wait out the call, its box's next calls waiting for a holder meanwhile. The call's tool runs on to its own end, held by the bridge under the run's own context — a node that stops waits for it once it has given its seats back ([Graceful shutdown](agent-runtime.md#graceful-shutdown)) — and its one update is made once it returns, as [one nobody waited for](#a-relaunch-continues-the-turns-session), where the run's log still takes it — its identity is on the log from before it ran ([What a run's record and its log keep](#what-a-runs-record-and-its-log-keep)): the session's end waits for no call, and every collection of the run, on whichever node makes it, waits for the update on the run's log before it reads the log (below); an update after the run's ending deleted its record finds no record of the run. A call whose entry was still being written as the session ended is answered that it was not run, failed, and never runs. The session that made it holds the end's answer, never its result, which every continuation of its round built after it landed is told from the run's log. A call that reaches a job's session after its end runs nothing and is recorded nowhere: where the run is over it is told to end its turn, and where the box goes on, that the session it reached was closing and to call again, which reaches the session that serves the box now — an answer the end waits for as it waits for the others, though a call that reaches the session as its close begins gets the close's failure instead.
