**A collection waits for its run's calls in flight.** Every collection of a run — its completion, a park on its question, the turn's cap cutting it, a job lost with its host and carried on, and the ending of a stop, a loss or a reclaim before it decides how the turn ended — first ends the job's session on the bridge to new calls, then waits until the run's tool log holds no call of the round in flight, and only then reads it, so the turn's outcome, its reviewer and its tool log hold what such a call did: a work item it filed counts in the turn's outcome. A park's collection ends the asking job's session too, so a call its box makes after it is refused, and the answer's relaunch opens a session of its own under its own job. The collection waits on the run's log in the coordination store, watched rather than polled, never on the bridge's own memory of the calls it runs, so whichever node collects — the seat's next holder, after a handoff — waits alike. Each call is waited for until the first of: its update; the turn's wall-clock cap, where the turn has one; the call's own bound, which its entry carries — when the bridge took it, its tool's own bound and a minute after that — since most turns have no cap; and its process gone, read off the taker lease of the node process running it as it first appears and every two seconds after, so a crashed node's call is given up within that lease's lifetime (`coordination.lease_ttl_seconds`, 45 seconds by default) rather than at its bound, while a node that drains keeps its lease until its calls are recorded and is waited for. A call still in flight when the wait stops is named at warning (`sandbox_calls_unfinished`) and read as a call whose outcome is unknown: listed in the round's record, failed, as `This call's outcome is unknown: its turn stopped waiting for it before its result was recorded, so what it did may have taken effect. Check whether it did before making it again.`, counted by its identity, told to a run carrying the round on beside its late calls, and beside it an ending reads the turn's delivery as unknown; its update, landing later while the run's record stands, replaces the unknown for every later reader. An ending reached where it cannot wait — a completion's handler, a seat's acquisition — hands a run whose log holds a call still waited for to the node's own retry of the ending, which waits; a seat's retirement leaves such a run, its session ended, to its own next pass rather than wait for it among the seat's other runs, and says so at info, as a wait rather than a failure ([`seat_mailbox_retirement_waiting`](seat-ownership.md#the-removed-seat)). **What it costs**: the collection holds the run's seat while it waits, as the resumed turn does after it, up to the first of those bounds, so a turn whose run left a call running resumes only once that call is recorded or given up on; one watch of the run's log while it waits, and one read of each running node's lease as it first appears and every two seconds after.

**The credential is per-job and expires with the run.** The box holds no API token; what admits a request is a signed token in the endpoint's own path, the same shape the OTLP receiver uses (`internal/runtoken`). It names the run, the **seat** it acts as and its **job** — the launch id the store mints and keeps on the run's durable row — never a process, and it is signed under the fleet's keyring. Two gates stand behind it: the signature says this fleet minted it, and the session map on the node holding the seat says the job it names is still the run's, so a box that outlived its run keeps no working key, and a relaunch is a new job whose old box's token stops resolving. The session is opened as soon as the job is named and before any box exists, so a launch that cannot be served is refused with nothing started.

**Where a box dials it depends on its cell and its node.** A `direct` box is a process tree on its node's own host, so on every node that binds `api.port` it dials that node's own listener on loopback and needs no setting at all. On an `ingress` node that is the listener the bridge is served on (`api.public.port` where it is set, `api.port` otherwise), so a single node with an API port runs the quickstart's company as written. On a node without `ingress` it is `api.port`, where the node serves the bridge beside its probes to its own host's loopback alone — a caller from anywhere else meets the route's absence — so a seat pool with no address of its own runs its seats too, provided `api.host` takes every interface or a loopback address. Every other box — a `container` or `e2b` box, which is not on the host's loopback, or a `direct` box on a node with no such listener (`api.port: 0`) — dials `CREWLET_MCP_BRIDGE_URL`, set on every node to the fleet's bridge address as the sandbox reaches it (below); a loopback one reaches a `container` box under the runtime's name for the engine host ([Local sandboxes](#local-sandboxes)), which on Linux needs the API to listen beyond loopback. The bridge takes a call under whatever `Host` it arrives with, because the token in its path is the credential and a page cannot make one: a reverse proxy or tunnel agent on the engine host that connects to the node on `127.0.0.1` and passes the public `Host` on, as ngrok, cloudflared and Caddy do unless told otherwise, reaches it as a box that dials it directly does, on its MCP endpoint and the run's notes route alike. The one session whose `Host` is judged is an Ask Crewlet [deep-work](../guides/assistant.md#where-a-run-happens) run held to the engine host's loopback: it admits a call only from that loopback, under a loopback host name or the runtime's name for the engine host (`host.docker.internal`, `host.containers.internal`, the name a call from Docker Desktop or Podman's machine arrives under), and refuses any other with `401`, because a page in a browser on the host, under a name its author points at `127.0.0.1`, can send requests there, and only ever under its own name. A launch whose box has no address to dial is refused naming what to set — `api.port` on an `ingress` node that binds no listener for the route, the variable for a cell it is missing for — rather than starting a run that cannot call anything, and each such cell is said once when a company is applied (`executor_bridge_unreachable`), before any turn is refused for it. A box off its host reaches a node without `ingress` through the ingress nodes, whatever that node's own `api.port`. The phases on this host need none of this: their runs reach their tools through a socket, with no listener at all.

**A bridge address a box cannot use refuses the boot.** A `CREWLET_MCP_BRIDGE_URL` that is not an absolute `http://` or `https://` URL a box can use — a host a URL parser accepts (a name, or an address with no zone: an unfilled `<host>` placeholder or a mistyped IPv4 address such as `10.0.0.300` is refused), a port from 1 to 65535 if it names one, and no user information, query or fragment — refuses the node's boot, naming the variable and never repeating the value: every endpoint minted from it would be one a box cannot dial, and every tool call of every run would fail inside the box with nothing to say why. Plain `http://` is accepted, and what that costs is in [Security model](#security-model).

**Every bridged call acts as the run's seat.** Because the token is the credential, the caller is the seat the run belongs to, and the bridge states that on every call — the same identity a turn's phases on this host carry, with the same four grants — so the [authority table](identity-and-access.md#the-authority-table-one-function-decides) decides a bridged `list_work_items` exactly as it decides the native one. It is set by the bridge itself rather than read off the request, because `/mcp/` is exempt from the credential guard: the guard resolves a box that presents nothing as anonymous, and a development build started with `-dev-principal` resolves it as that principal. Neither may decide a seat's tool call, so the bridge replaces the request's answer with the seat's on this route and nowhere else. A session whose tool surface is bound to no seat is refused at `Open` rather than served.

**Every call is written down where a resume can read it.** A phase on this host keeps its calls in memory and the turn writes them at the end; a bridged run's calls are made by a process outside the engine, minutes apart, possibly across a restart. Each one is appended to the run's tool log, kept in the coordination store beside the run's record ([below](#the-runs-record-and-what-it-costs-to-read)) — with its order, when it started, how long it took and which server answered it, a call refused once the run's tools had closed included — bounded, dropping from the *middle* rather than the start: how a run began and how it ended are what explain it. A resume orders the calls by the order they reached the session in. A call that finishes only after a relaunch that carried the round on has opened the log to the next job is written down too, as a call of the round, marked late, its cost charged to no job ([A relaunch continues the turn's session](#a-relaunch-continues-the-turns-session)). Without that, the reviewer of a resumed run judges a turn whose entire tool log is gone, which the delivery check reads as a turn that acted on nothing. **And each carries its identity**: the digest of its whole arguments the run's call log admitted it under, taken by the bridge before anything is recorded, which is what a resumed segment counts the call by. The log keeps a call's arguments and its answer to the call's share of the log, and a long run's middle only as identities ([what a run's record and its log keep](#what-a-runs-record-and-its-log-keep)), so neither a cut argument nor a dropped call moves the operation id a later call derives. **And the id its harness gave it** (`call_id`): Claude Code hands every call it makes the model's `tool_use` id, in the request's `_meta` under `claudecode/toolUseId`, the id its own session's record files the call under (measured on 2.1.310, on its main thread and in its subagents, over the bridge's transport, a tool its tool search loaded included). The bridge reads it under the key the box's runner declares and no other, and records it on the call's entry and on its record alike, whole or not at all: an id longer than 64 bytes, or holding anything but letters, digits, `-` and `_`, or a value that is not a string, is recorded as none, never cut, since two ids cut alike would name two calls as one. It is the harness's word and no part of the call's identity: the record finds its entry without it, and two calls under one id are two calls. OpenCode sends no id with a call, so its calls carry none, and neither does a skill load a harness's hook reports or the call a person approved, which no harness made over the bridge.

**One address for the fleet; the seat's holder answers.** A session is a live tool surface in the process holding the run's seat (the seat's MCP children, its skill guard, its recording), so only that process can answer a call — but the box need not dial it. `CREWLET_MCP_BRIDGE_URL` is the **fleet's** address: any node that serves the API (the `ingress` role), a load balancer in front of several included, and every node is given the same value. The node a call lands on reads the seat's lease, which the token names, and relays the whole HTTP exchange to the node holding the seat over the broker (`internal/httprelay`), event stream included; the holder answers it from its own session. So a node without `ingress` — one that serves the network its probes and nothing else, a [satellite](../guides/satellite-nodes.md) at the edge of a network included — runs agent seats as well as one that serves the API: nothing a box off its host needs is a route on it, and its own `direct` boxes dial it on loopback (above). A call for a seat that node has since handed on is relayed to the seat's holder from there as from any `ingress` node. Ask Crewlet's [deep work](../guides/assistant.md#where-a-run-happens) has no seat whose lease could name a holder, and nothing takes its session over, so its token names the process that opened the session and a call for it is relayed straight there; once that node runs a later process, the call is refused. With Tier A `api.public.port` set, the route is served on the ingress nodes' public listener instead of `api.port`, so the bridge URL names the public port and a direct box dials it there ([Exposing webhooks without the admin API](../guides/deployment.md#exposing-webhooks-without-the-admin-api)). The token is signed from Tier A `secrets.keys`, under a domain that separates bridge tokens from telemetry tokens; every node holds that keyring — a node without one does not start — so any node can tell a token the fleet signed from a forged one before it relays a call, and a keyring that cannot sign for the fleet is refused by name when the bridge is built. Every relayed request and every frame of its answer is signed under that same keyring ([ADR-0031](../../adr/0031-every-state-log-record-is-signed.md)), because the broker authenticates nobody and both are instructions.

**A call waits for a holder that can answer it.** A call that lands while no node can answer — the seat's holder has died and its lease not yet run out, or a peer has the lease and is still taking the seat — waits, reading the lease again, for up to two seat-lease lifetimes (90 seconds at the default `coordination.lease_ttl_seconds`): one for a dead holder's lease to lapse and one for a peer to claim the seat and open its running jobs' sessions again. A holder that has finished taking the seat and holds no session for the job answers `401`, as for any run that is over. One that cannot be found within the bound is answered `503` with a `Retry-After`, never that the run is over, which nobody said. A holder that took a call and stopped answering before it had written a status is answered `502`, and the call is not offered to another node: it may have run.

**A run outlives a restart, or a move, of the node that holds its seat.** Because the token names the job rather than a process, whichever node takes the seat over — the same node after a restart, or a peer — re-opens the run's session as it recovers the seat, before the seat's mailbox opens: the surface rebuilt from the run's own row — the tools and skills it launched with, and the skills it loaded over the bridge — with its call log and what its bridged calls have cost carried over. Only once the seat's running jobs are recovered does the node answer that a job it holds no session for is over. The node that hands the seat on ends its runs' sessions as it releases the seat, so the box's next request is routed afresh and reaches the new holder. The box's own MCP session lived on the node that let it go, so its next request there is answered `404`, which the MCP protocol answers by connecting again — through the same URL, to the new holder, with the box's unchanged token. A session that cannot be re-opened logs `bridge_not_reopened` and never fails the recovery. A run whose ending is already decided is not re-opened, and neither is one on a seat whose pause asks for its running work to stop: the node that takes that seat ends the run as it recovers it (see [Stop now](agent-runtime.md#pausing-a-seat)), so its box never gets the seat's tools back in between.

## A run's own skills

A seat's [Tool Skills](tool-skills.md) reach its executor as its harness's **own skills**, never as a catalogue in its prompt ([why](tool-skills.md#the-executors-skills-are-its-harnesss-own)): every launch hands the run the skills its phase matches, and the harness lists each to its model by name and description, and hands over the text when the model loads it with its own skill tool. Each is listed as `crewlet:<name>` on both harnesses, so a refusal names the skill to load whichever harness runs:

- **Claude Code** is handed them as a plugin of the run's own, `crewlet`, which the run starts with `--plugin-dir` (`<home>/.crewlet/claude-skills`): a `SKILL.md` per skill, and one `PostToolUse` hook on the `Skill` tool, an `mcp_tool` hook that calls the bridge's `skill_loaded` with the name of the skill the tool loaded — the way the run's [hooks](#claude-codes-hooks) take its notes, so nothing else reaches the box: no credential, no URL to allow, no program. `skill_loaded` is never listed, so no model can call it to unlock a skill it never read. Claude Code 2.1.118 is the first release that runs an `mcp_tool` hook; on an older one a run with a required skill is refused before the harness starts, since every tool the skill covers would be refused for the whole run, and any other run starts without its skills, which its error stream says. From 2.1.118 a run that starts the plugin is started with `--settings <home>/.crewlet/claude-settings-skills.json` (from 2.1.310 its pinned copy, `claude-settings-skills-pinned.json`) — [every run's settings](#a-claude-code-runs-session) with `disableAllHooks: false` — or, where it starts its notes' hooks too, [their settings](#claude-codes-hooks), which hold the same: a repository's committed `disableAllHooks: true` otherwise switches the plugin's hook off without a word, so no load is reported and a required skill never unlocks, and the explicit `false` wins over it (read in 2.1.118's code, which merges its settings user, project, local, `--settings`, managed, a later one winning). It switches the repository's own hooks on with it, as a run with notes always has; a run that starts no hook of the engine's leaves them as the repository sets them. The key is in the settings schema from 1.0.68 — 1.0.67's has none — far below the plugin's floor, and a file holding a key its release does not know is ignored whole.
- **OpenCode** is handed them as a directory its configuration's `skills` names (`<home>/.crewlet/opencode-skills`), a directory per skill named as it is listed — the id OpenCode loads a skill by — and a second plugin of the run's own, beside the [notes plugin](#opencodes-notes-plugin), that posts each completed `skill` load to the bridge's skill route (`POST /mcp/{token}/skill`, [the API reference](../reference/api-endpoints.md#routes)) from an `execute.after` hook OpenCode waits for, so the load is on record before the model's next request.

The bridge records each reported load as the run's own `load_tool_skill` call of the skill's key, which the [required-skill guard](tool-skills.md#required-skills--load-before-use-enforcement) unlocks by and every later segment of the run replays, and it lists no `load_tool_skill` to the run: its harness's own tool is the loader. A skill the run was not handed — the checkout's own, or the image's — loads as the harness loads any and is recorded nowhere. The names a launch handed are kept on the parked turn, so a node that reopens the run's session after a restart or a seat move reads a reported load by them too. Both directories are the run's, rewritten at every launch and cleared before it, so a box never lists a previous run's skills.

A skill's text is the harness's own skill: Claude Code reads it as a template, in which a line's `` !`command` `` runs as the skill loads and `$ARGUMENTS` is a placeholder, and OpenCode hands it to its model as written.

## Notes to a running run

A person can [steer](turn-engine.md#steering-a-running-turn) a turn while its
executor works in its box. From the executor's launch until its turn ends, the turn's notes
wait on the **run's record** in the coordination store, each sealed under the
fleet keyring by the node that filed it, beside a sealed anchor naming the seat
the run serves ([ADR-0067](https://github.com/crewlet/crewlet/blob/main/adr/0067-a-note-to-an-executor-waits-sealed-on-its-record.md)).
The run reads them at four kinds of step, and every read is recorded on the
record by the write that hands the note over — so no two reads hand a model the
same note (a session that may not show the notes it read is told them again
when it is continued, as notes it may already show: [A relaunch continues the
turn's session](#a-relaunch-continues-the-turns-session)), and the record's read
notes are exactly what the turn carries into its review, whichever node resumes
it:

- **Its brief.** A note waiting when a run is launched — the first one, a
  relaunch the reviewer sends back, or the one that carries a person's answer —
  goes into the brief, read by the write that opens the job.
- **Between its steps, through its tool bridge.** The run's bridge serves its
  notes to its harness two ways, behind the run's own token and nothing else:
  - **`steer_take`**, a tool on the run's MCP server that a harness's hook calls
    after a batch of the main agent's tool calls (`PostToolBatch`) or as the
    run is about to finish (`Stop`). It is **never listed**, so no harness offers it
    to its model, and it is answered before the seat's surface sees the call,
    so it is never recorded on the run's ledger: it is the harness asking, not the
    model. Its answer is always text — the hook's answer, with the notes as its
    additional context, or empty for nothing — and **never an error**, because a
    hook whose call failed makes the harness drop and re-initialise the run's whole
    bridge. Only the main agent's loop takes notes: a subagent's batch, and a
    hook whose agent id the harness did not fill in, take nothing, because a note
    handed to a delegated loop binds that piece of work alone. One answer
    carries the oldest waiting notes within **9,000 UTF-16 code units**, measured
    as the harness measures its context, and the rest wait for the next step; it is
    answered within **five seconds**, half the hook's timeout, or not at all. A
    take the store has not answered by then is answered with nothing, and is
    settled off the hook's path (below): where its write landed all the same,
    the notes it recorded are marked unanswered (read by the record's word, so
    no later take hands them over, and published delivered by nothing, since
    nothing was; the turn's ending reports each, below); and where it did not,
    they wait for the next step.
  - **The notes route**, `/mcp/{token}/steer/notes?after=<seq>` and
    `/mcp/{token}/steer/read`, which a plugin inside the harness polls: a poll is
    held for up to **ten seconds** while no note waits, and answered at once
    when one arrives. Once the node is stopping, or the session's opening has
    ended, a held poll is let go and every later one turned away with a `503`
    and a `Retry-After` — never an empty answer a poller would ask again at
    once — and its notes wait on the record for the session's next opening, on
    this node or the seat's next holder. That holds as well for a poll the
    stopping node is relaying to another node that holds the session: the
    stopping node lets go of it at once rather than wait out the holder's hold
    on its listener's grace. Every note a poll answers is first recorded
    **served** to the run's job on the run's record, in a write before the answer,
    so a note its harness may deliver is never one the record says nothing of;
    one the record could not mark is not answered, nor is any after it — the
    plugin's cursor moves past every note it is answered — and they wait for the
    next poll.
    A read names the notes
    (one to ten) the harness put in front of its model, and is recorded read at the
    run's next step. Each note carries an id the harness admits it under, unique to
    the note and the job, so a poller that admits it twice admits it once.

  A harness takes notes here only where its run is set up to ask for them; a run
  whose harness asks for none still has every note read, by its review. Both
  harnesses are set up to: **Claude Code** through [hooks](#claude-codes-hooks)
  that call `steer_take`, and **OpenCode** through a [plugin](#opencodes-notes-plugin)
  that polls the notes route and hands each note to the run's session.

  A take is pinned to the run's **job** and to a run that is launching or
  working with its ending undecided: a relaunch's job, a run parked on a
  question, a collected run and a run whose tools are closed — its seat moved,
  or a person stopped it — take nothing, and their notes wait for whatever reads
  the turn next. The bridge itself decides nothing about notes; it routes the
  call, like every bridged call, to the node holding the run's seat, whose
  inbox for the run is rebuilt from the record — so a run whose node restarted
  is served its notes by whichever node reopens its session.
- **The review's first round.** A note the run never took is read by the
  segment that reviews the run, before its first provider call.
- **After its question is answered.** A run parked on a question takes nothing
  while it waits; `steer_turn` answers `reads: "after_answer"`, and the launch
  that carries the answer carries the notes.

**A take whose answer was lost is settled.** A take's write can land while its
answer does not — a `steer_take` the store answered after its five seconds, or
a review's take whose answer a broken connection lost — and the record then
says a note was read by a step that was never handed it. So every take carries
an identity of its own, and one whose call answered an error is **settled** by
it, in one of two ways:

- **A hook's take is marked.** The hook has been answered nothing already, so
  its take is settled off its path: the reads it recorded are marked
  **unanswered**, the job named never having had them. They stay read by the
  record's word — no later take hands them over, and the review reads them,
  carried as every read note is — but no capture of the job's session counts
  them held, and every later launch tells them again in its brief, as notes it
  may already show: the fresh brief among every note the turn has read, and a
  continued session from the first marked read on. The launch's write records
  each one it tells again read by the job it opens, told again, at the marked
  take's own place among the turn's reads, so a capture of that job counts it
  held, and keeps the take's read beside the telling. The settle asks the
  coordination store until it answers,
  however long that takes, and says so at thirty seconds
  ([`steer_take_unsettled`](#failure-modes)); only the node stopping first ends
  it, once it has asked every take it holds once more — a take a segment the
  stop ends hands off among them — in a window of five seconds from the start
  of the stop's teardown, beside the leases the stop gives back and never on
  their allowance, since a settle that runs out leaves a take unsettled rather
  than a lease lapsing.
- **A review's take is answered.** Its round is still waiting on it, so its
  take is settled on the review's own path, in answer mode, and what its write
  recorded is handed over there and then, never marked. Where the store answers
  that settle no more than it answered the take, the take is kept by the
  segment reviewing the run and settled again at its next round, or at the
  brief of a relaunch it makes, handing over what it recorded to whichever the
  store answers first — a brief's launch then records the note read by the job
  it opens, in the take's place, so the note is that job's as every other note
  its brief carries is, and keeps the take's own place among the turn's reads,
  where the brief tells it too: a note a later round read, a correction of it,
  is never told ahead of it; meanwhile, for thirty seconds, it stands for a note
  waiting, so a round asking for tools ends and the next one opens with the
  note. Only a take the store has still not answered when the segment ends is
  marked, as the segment ends: in the segment's own path first, so the run's
  ending, which reads the record next where the segment ended the turn, finds
  the mark and reports the note `missed`, and off its path where the store
  answers that attempt no more.

Either way, a settle that finds the take recorded nothing writes the record all
the same, so that take's write, should it still be on its way to the store,
can never land after it; and the notes wait for the next step. A launch reads
the marks off the record as its brief is written, so one landed after the
segment that launches resumed is counted all the same.

What became of each note is published as `agent_turn_steered`: `delivered` by
the node whose step read it, with the kind of step (`boundary`: `brief`,
`tool_batch`, `finish`, `step`, or `round` for the review) and, for a read by
the run, its job (`launch_id`). Every note no step reported reading is reported
by the node that finishes the run's ending, as the record knows it, and by
nothing else, in this order:

- **`delivered`, told again** (`retold: true`), a note marked unanswered that a
  later launch's brief told again, where that launch's job started: at that
  brief (`boundary: brief`, its `launch_id`). The take that recorded it never
  handed it over; the brief did.
- **`unconfirmed`**, a note the run's bridge served to its job's harness that
  never confirmed it, and that no step read after: at that job (`boundary:
  step`, its `launch_id`). Its session may hold it, and nothing says it does.
- **`missed`**, a note a take whose answer was lost recorded read, marked
  unanswered, that no later launch whose job started was told: at the step that
  took it. The review after it, if one ran, read it carried. A note a later
  launch told again and whose job never started (the launch failed after
  writing the record) is `missed` too, at that take: the brief handed nothing
  over, and the record keeps the take's read beside the telling.
- **`expired`**, a note no job ran with: still waiting, or read first into the
  brief of a launch that failed after writing the record, whose job never
  started, at no step.

So a note served and then left to a lost take, or to the brief of a job that
never started, is `unconfirmed`, since a session may hold it. Each is published
before the record is deleted, under an id derived from the ending, so a retried
ending records it once. A park is never an ending. Whether a job started is on
that job's own record, written when its command is attached and kept when a park
or an expired pause lets its box go, so a note a started job's brief carried,
recorded `delivered`, is never then recorded `expired`.

A proxy between the boxes and `CREWLET_MCP_BRIDGE_URL` holds a poll open for
up to those ten seconds, so it needs a request timeout of at least fifteen —
see [Deployment](../guides/deployment.md).

### Claude Code's hooks

Every Claude Code run its launch wires to its turn's notes — every seat's
executor run; not Ask Crewlet's deep work, whose session has no inbox — is
started, on a release that reads them, with a settings file of its own,
`claude-settings-notes.json` in the box's work directory, written afresh at
`0600` and named with `--settings` in place of the [settings every run
has](#a-claude-code-runs-session), which Claude Code ranks above the
repository's own settings and below a managed policy. From 2.1.310 the run is
handed its pinned copy instead, `claude-settings-notes-pinned.json`, which
holds the same and [the compaction point's pin](#a-claude-code-runs-session) in
an `env` block. The file holds every run's setting, two hooks and the setting
that keeps them on, nothing else:

```json
{
  "cleanupPeriodDays": 36500,
  "disableAllHooks": false,
  "hooks": {
    "PostToolBatch": [{"hooks": [{"type": "mcp_tool", "server": "crewlet", "tool": "steer_take",
      "input": {"event": "${hook_event_name}", "agent": "${agent_id}"}, "timeout": 10}]}],
    "Stop": [{"hooks": [{"type": "mcp_tool", "server": "crewlet", "tool": "steer_take",
      "input": {"event": "${hook_event_name}", "agent": "${agent_id}"}, "timeout": 10}]}]
  }
}
```

- **An `mcp_tool` hook**, a call of `steer_take` on the run's own `crewlet`
  server: it rides the bridge connection the run already holds, under its
  token, so there is no new credential, no URL to allow and nothing to run in
  the box; its input is the two values the harness fills in, where an `http` hook
  would post every batch's tool calls; and every way it can fail is
  non-blocking, where a `command` hook that exits 2 ends the loop. The server
  and tool names are the ones the bridge serves, filled in by the engine, so
  the file and the bridge cannot disagree.
- **What the model sees**, measured on 2.1.263 and 2.1.310 against a stub model
  API: a note handed back after a batch is read after that batch's tool
  results and stays for the rest of the run; one handed back at `Stop`
  continues the turn, and the run's result is the answer given after it.
  **Where it sits depends on the model**: on 2.1.310, after a batch, a system
  message after the tool results for `claude-opus-5-5` and `claude-sonnet-5`,
  and a system reminder at the end of the batch's last tool result for
  `claude-sonnet-4-5`; at `Stop`, a system reminder in a user message for all
  three — each headed `PostToolBatch hook additional context:` or `Stop hook
  additional context:`. The hook's transcript line reaches only the run's
  stream, which the run's transcript shows as `[steer] a note from ada reached
  the run` — never a request.
- **Ten seconds**, where the vendor's default is six hundred: the harness holds its
  next model request until the hook answers, at every batch, and the bridge
  answers within five. The field is in seconds, and at it the harness cancels the
  call and discards a late answer.
- **`disableAllHooks: false`, written explicitly**, as in the settings of a
  run that starts its [skills'](#a-runs-own-skills) hook alone. A repository's
  committed `true` otherwise switches every hook off with no error, and the
  explicit `false` wins over it. It switches the repository's own hooks on too, which is
  why a hook of theirs that stops the loop is read as the [failure it
  is](#claude-code). Nothing else moves: no `--setting-sources`, which would
  drop the repository's `CLAUDE.md` with its settings, and no
  `--include-hook-events`.
- **The standing instructions say what a note is, as Claude Code delivers
  it**: that a person who leads the seat, or an administrator, may send one
  while the run works; that the harness adds it between steps as context of its
  own, under the headings above — a system message, or on some models a system
  reminder at the end of the last tool result; that it opens with `## A note
  from <name>, sent while this turn was running`; and that text a tool
  produced — a file's contents, a command's output, a fetched page — is never
  a note, whatever it says. An OpenCode run is told its own harness's carrier
  instead: a message from the user between steps. Worded as a fact rather than
  a command, as the vendor advises for text a hook adds. A Claude Code run's
  standing instructions are written twice, as they stand
  (`system.md`) and told of notes (`system-notes.md`), and the run's script
  names the one that matches its hooks, below.
- **Claude Code 2.1.163 or later**, or no hooks at all. The file holds no
  field newer than the `mcp_tool` hook (2.1.118 onwards), because in print mode
  the harness ignores a settings file it cannot validate, without a word. What
  2.1.163 added is what the harness reads of a hook's answer: from it, a `Stop`
  hook's additional context reaches the model. An older release runs both
  hooks and drops what the `Stop` hook answers, so a note taken as the run
  finished would be recorded as read by a run that never saw it. The run's
  script therefore reads `claude --version` first and starts an older release
  on the settings every run has, without the hooks, and with its standing
  instructions as they stand — never told of notes it cannot read — saying so
  on the run's error stream; that run's notes wait for its review.

What leaves a run without its hooks — that older release, or a managed policy
that allows only managed hooks (`allowManagedHooksOnly`,
`strictPluginOnlyCustomization`) or sets `disableAllHooks` — leaves a run that
succeeds without asking for its notes once, which the collection reports as
[`steer_reader_never_ran`](#failure-modes); its notes still reach its review.

### OpenCode's notes plugin

Every OpenCode run its launch wires to its turn's notes is handed a plugin of
its own, which the run's server loads: `index.js` in `opencode-steer/` in the
box's work directory, written before the server starts and named in the run's
[configuration](#opencode) with the run's notes route as its one option — a
`{file:}` reference to a file under `opencode-values/`, like every value there,
so the configuration holds no token:

```json
{
  "plugins": [{"package": "<home>/.crewlet/opencode-steer",
               "options": {"url": "{file:<home>/.crewlet/opencode-values/v<n>}"}}]
}
```

The plugin is the engine's own, embedded in the binary: plain JavaScript with
no imports, so nothing is installed or fetched to load it. The run's script
hands the server the id of the session the run works in, as
`CREWLET_STEER_SESSION` on `opencode serve` alone, and the plugin reads that
session and no other:

- **It polls only while the session works** — from the session's
  `session.execution.started` (or any of its steps starting) to its
  `succeeded`, `failed` or `interrupted`, again for an execution OpenCode
  starts itself — and nothing before, so the brief is always the first thing
  the session reads. Its setup only registers and returns: OpenCode holds
  every prompt until each plugin's setup has resolved.
- **Each note is admitted as a steer**, under the id the route gives it
  (`ctx.session.prompt` with `delivery: "steer"`): OpenCode hands every steer
  waiting at its next step boundary to the model as a user message after that
  step's tool results — or after a final step that called no tool, whose
  boundary reads the inbox again — so a note sent while the run writes its
  answer is still read in the same run. The id is derived from the job and the
  note, so a note admitted twice is admitted once. The cursor moves past a note
  only once it is admitted.
- **With `resume: false`**: a note admitted once the session has settled
  starts nothing — an execution `opencode run` no longer watched would answer
  nobody — and the plugin admits none then anyway. Such a note waits on the
  run's record for its review. One whose admission landed just as the session
  settled, after OpenCode last read its inbox, is left pending in the session
  too, and the session's inbox outlives the server: a run carried on in the
  same session with a person's answer would be handed it ahead of its next
  prompt, beside the continuation brief's copy. So once `opencode run` has
  exited, and before the server stops, the run's script lists the session's
  inbox and **cancels every steer the plugin admitted and OpenCode never
  delivered** (`session.inbox.cancel`, an id of the plugin's own shape and
  nothing else), and the record alone carries the note — read once, by the
  review or the next launch's brief.
- **It confirms only what OpenCode delivered**: on the session's
  `session.inbox.delivered` for a note it admitted, it posts the note's id to
  the route's `read`, which records it read by the run's job (`boundary:
  step`). A note admitted and never delivered is never confirmed, and its
  review reads it. One delivered whose confirmation never landed — its harness
  cut off first, or the store not answering — waits on the record all the same,
  **served** (above): its review reads it, and a launch that continues the run's
  session tells it as a note the session may already show, never as one it had
  not read; a fresh run is told it as it is told every note. Where nothing reads
  it before the turn ends, the ending reports it `unconfirmed` at the job it was
  served to, never `expired`: its session may hold it. A confirmation whose
  write landed while its answer was lost is reported `delivered` all the same:
  the route settles its take on its own path and reports what the take
  recorded, and a confirmation the plugin makes again that finds the note
  already read at its job's step reports it as the same record.
- **It asks, and never insists**: a poll is held up to the route's ten seconds
  and given twenty, a confirmation ten; a failed request — a stopping node's
  `503` among them — is made again after a pause of a second, doubling to
  thirty and reset by the next success; a poll that admitted nothing is
  followed by the next no sooner than a second after it began, so no answer
  can drive it into a loop of requests; and a refused token (`401`) or a run
  with no notes route (`404`) ends the plugin for the rest of the run, its
  notes waiting for the review.
- **It writes nothing**: OpenCode reloads a configured plugin whose directory
  changes, and a note never touches the box's disk.

Measured against a stub model API: on OpenCode 2.0.32 and 2.1.4, a note
admitted while a tool call ran was read by the next request, after the tool's
result, and one admitted during the final model call was read in the same
execution, after the answer it superseded; on 2.1.4, one admitted after the
execution settled, with `resume: false`, started nothing, stayed pending in the
session across the server's restart and was handed to the next run in that
session ahead of its prompt — and, cancelled, was handed to nobody. The run's
[result](#opencode) is its **last** message's text, so a run steered past its
answer reports the answer it gave after the note.

A managed policy that denies the plugin (`integration.use`), or a plugin
OpenCode fails to load — which its own log names — leaves a run that succeeds
without asking for its notes once, which the collection says as
[`steer_reader_never_ran`](#failure-modes); its notes still reach its review.

---

## Mid-run clarification (`ask_and_wait`)

A run may hit something only a person can answer — an ambiguous spec, a design decision above its remit. A headless harness cannot pause to ask interactively, so every executor run's [standing instructions](#what-a-harness-is-told) carry `## When you need a person's answer`, with two ways to ask:

- **If it can go on without the answer**, it asks with its own tools — `comment_on_work_item` with an `ask` naming whoever owes the answer, or a message where it was asked — keeps working, and finishes its turn as usual with `submit_work`, saying what is waiting on whom. The answer wakes a new turn of the seat's, like any other message; this run does not wait for it.
- **If it cannot go on without it**, it **parks**: it commits and pushes its work-in-progress branch, calls **`ask_and_wait`** on its [tool bridge](#the-tool-bridge--a-seats-own-tools-from-inside-a-box) (`internal/agent/builtin/runask.go`) with the question, and ends its turn without calling `submit_work`. The audience (`to`) is `requester`, `team`, `manager`, or a named teammate: the instruction carries the seat's unit roster and its manager, by handle, so the run can name a real person and the park can resolve the name exactly. When the person answers, the run **continues where it stopped**, in its own harness session, with the answer as its next prompt: in the box it left where the relaunch reuses that box — the box was kept, the member the launch picks runs the same harness in the same cell, and the box is this node's to drive (any `e2b` box, or a local one on this node's own host) — and otherwise in a new box from the branch, its session restored there from the capture the turn kept ([A relaunch continues the turn's session](#a-relaunch-continues-the-turns-session)). Where the session cannot be continued, a fresh run starts in a new box from the branch, told the question and the answer ([Answering a parked run](#answering-a-parked-run)).

The rest of this section is the second path.

**A run asks in the shape a decision is asked** ([ADR-0068](../../adr/0068-a-run-asks-a-person-in-the-shape-a-decision-is-asked.md)). `ask_and_wait` takes:

| Argument | What it is |
|---|---|
| `question` | What the run needs answered, in one sentence of at most 300 bytes: the title the person reads first. Required. |
| `context` | What the person needs to answer well, in Markdown (what the run found, what is at stake, links to what it cites), at most 16 KiB. |
| `options`, `recommended`, `rationale`, `role`, `evidence` | The choice every question offers: 2 to 4 options (`id`, `label`, `detail`), the one the run recommends and why, the role the person answers in, and what it looked at. The options, the recommendation, the rationale and the role are **required**: the person answers with one press, and in words through **Something else**, so a question the run would ask in words still offers the answers it expects ([ADR-0100](../../adr/0100-a-question-an-agent-puts-to-a-person-offers-options-and-recommends-one.md)). The same shape, caps and checks as a [work item's decision](../guides/worklet.md#asking-for-a-decision): a cited task is resolved to its id and a cited page must exist. |
| `to` | Who should answer, as above. Required. |
| `branch` | The branch the run pushed its work to, exactly as pushed. |

**The ask is validated whole before anything is written**, and every fault comes back to the run at once, while it can still fix it ([ADR-0059](../../adr/0059-a-tools-contract-is-its-validators-and-every-fault-comes-back-at-once.md)): a question past its cap, a question with no options or no recommendation, a branch that is not one by Gitlet's own grammar (`gitref.ValidBranch`: a name git accepts for a branch, valid UTF-8 (the run's record is JSON, where any other byte is replaced and the name would arrive as another branch's), and at most 244 bytes, `gitref.MaxBranchBytes`), an option id that is not a slug. A run's ask carries no `inform`: its answer resumes the run itself, so there is no answered turn of the seat's to hold to a promise. A valid ask is **recorded on the run's row** by the job that made the call (`question`, `question_context`, `decision`, `audience`, `branch`), redacted, and only while that job is running: a second question in one job is refused (one answer resumes a run, so it asks one question at a time), and a call from a job that has ended or been replaced records nothing. The tool then tells the run to end its turn, and from then on the job's `submit_work` is refused (`not_running`) with the same instruction: the job is parked on the answer, so a submission it makes is not delivered work, and a harness told it was would end believing so. The bridge nudges the run's waiter, so the run is collected and parked at once rather than at the waiter's next pass. **The branch** is what a run re-seeded on a fresh machine is told to check out ([below](#answering-a-parked-run)), so it is recorded as its own fact at the one moment anything knows it, and is never inferred from the refs the run delivered, which name a pull request's URL as often as a branch. A run ended at its turn's wall-clock cap is never parked on a question it asked on its way out: it is collected and resumed like any ending. When a run completes with a recorded question:

1. The engine (never the sandbox) **announces** the question as `executor_asked` on the event stream, attributed to the seat whose run asked and carrying the question, its context and its decision, the audience as the agent wrote it, the run's conversation and its work item, the job that asked (`launch_id`) and, for a question about a held tool call, that call (`held_call`, never its arguments), and resolves that audience into the people it is put to ([below](#who-a-question-is-put-to)), keeping identity attribution and capability guards on the engine. **That is how it reaches them**: the run becomes a row in each of those people's own decisions on the dashboard (Home and the Inbox's *Needs you*, and *Asked of me* on My work, all served by [`GET /worklet/decisions`](../reference/api-endpoints.md#queries)), a banner on the run's page and its rail in Live › Executors, a row of Live › Now running's *Waiting on a person*, and an entry on the board an operator reads (`GET /executors?audience=<handle>`, "what is waiting on me"). **Nothing is posted to a chat surface**: the engine sends no message into the run's conversation or to anybody's direct messages, so a person who works only in chat learns of the question from somebody who has seen it there. It is answered from that row, [by turn](#answering-a-parked-run), or by a reply on the run's conversation (step 3).
2. The run is parked (`awaiting_clarification`) with both conversation values; the turn's parked state is kept on the row untouched; the box is left **paused** so the checkout and the harness's session survive. Unlike a running job, **the agent goes free** — a human reply can take days, so its inbox flows and it can do other work. The question is announced first and recorded second, and the agent is freed only once the record lands: a question recorded but never announced would wait for an answer nobody was asked for, and a seat freed ahead of the record reads as idle with nothing open, which is the state in which a person's reply is run as an unrelated turn.

   **A park that could not be written is not a park.** The run reaches this step holding the claim a completion took, so a step that fails leaves the claim to give back: handed back, the completion poll fires again, the paused box is collected a second time and the park is retried. If the claim cannot be handed back either, nothing retries at all — a row left in the claim is read by no poll, re-claimed by no redelivery, matched by no answer and expired by no reaper — so the run is ended like any other lost turn (record deleted, box reclaimed, `executor_failed` with reason `claim_unreverted`) rather than left to strand a billed box until the seat changes hands. It is ended **only while it is still the claim**, though — its status, its job and its lease, which the store checks as it decides the ending: a hand-back that reported a failure may still have landed, and a run it did reach is back where the claim found it and owed its retry, so it is left to that retry rather than killed under it — and an ending that could not reach the store either, kept and finished later, that finds the run handed back after all resumes it with its reply instead of settling into nothing. The ending is decided first and the box reclaimed second for the same reason, since the decision is what establishes the run was still the claim's to end.
3. The **first reply posted in that conversation after the question was asked, by somebody it was put to and written for whom the run works for,** is the answer. The row records the instant the question was put (`asked_at`, taken before the question is announced, so nobody can have read it earlier), and a delivery answers only if it was posted at or after that instant — its envelope's own timestamp, which for a chat message is the vendor's post time. Without that anchor the rule was purely positional ("the next thing to arrive here"), and two things went wrong with it: a message written while the job was still running was taken as the answer the moment the run parked, and a reply that answered an *earlier* question, held back and redelivered, was taken as the answer to whatever the run asked next. The conversation is matched on the [conversation identity](event-system.md) the row was written with, which lets the answer arrive wherever the person actually replies. It has to be the identity rather than the inbox partition: a run launched from a *top-level direct message* parks under the bare DM channel, and the engine's own chat prompt tells the seat to reply **as a thread** — so the person's answer arrives partitioned on that thread, and comparing partitions meant the clarification was never delivered and the box waited out its pause TTL. A direct message is one conversation however it is threaded, which is exactly what the identity says. A row with no identity — a run an A2A wake or a task assignment launched — is answered by no chat reply at all, and neither is a row of a schedule's conversation, which every fire of the schedule carries and nothing a person writes does: a delivery on it is never offered to a parked run, so the schedule's next fire is never taken as the answer to the question its last fire asked. Every question is parked with its anchor — a park without one is refused — and a delivery that cannot show it was posted after the question answers nothing: it goes its ordinary way.

   **Only words answer it.** A delivery is a reply only when it carries words its writer addressed to the conversation: a chat message, a comment on a work item, a page or a change, or a review, judged from the surface the event's source reads its body from (its provenance), never from its text. A status or field change on the run's own work item, a page save, an alarm firing again, a monitor's re-alert or a work pickup on the same conversation is not a reply, whoever made it: it runs as the ordinary event it is, and the run keeps waiting for its answer.

   **Only somebody the question was put to answers it there.** One turn works for one writer ([ADR-0109](../../adr/0109-a-turn-somebody-woke-is-held-to-the-apps-they-use.md)), and the answer is what the run carries on with, so a reply in the conversation answers the question only when its writer is a seat of the question's audience ([below](#who-a-question-is-put-to)) or the run's requester, and whoever holds that seat may act when the reply arrives (read then, never at the park). The writer is the seat the engine's party registry resolves the reply to, never its words: a vendor still delivers a suspended person's chat, and the registry withholds their identities, so it names nobody. Anybody else's reply (a stranger, a person or a seat outside the audience, a credential) runs as the ordinary message it is, its own turn worked for its own writer, and the run waits on its question. And a reply answers only a run working for whom it was written for: an agent's reply written in a turn for somebody else is that somebody's work, so it runs as its own turn held to their apps, and the run waits. A reply written for nobody else (every vendor's, every person's, an agent's own work) qualifies. A credential is matched by its login, never its apps. A colleague on a node that cannot place the run's credential works for a stranger and can post no first-party reply, so such a run waits for the dashboard or a vendor reply. A question whose park named neither an audience nor a requester is answered from the dashboard alone ([ADR-0077](../../adr/0077-a-tool-call-that-needs-approval-parks-its-run-in-the-shape-a-runs-ask-does.md)).

   **The answer is recorded before anything is done with it.** The first qualifying reply is written onto the run by a compare-and-set (the run moves to **`answered`**, carrying the reply, `answer`), and only then is the delivery acknowledged. From that moment the answer is durable and nothing about it depends on the inbox again: a second reply racing it, on this node or across a seat handoff, loses the compare-and-set and finds the question answered. The coordinator then claims the answered run (at-most-once) and **resumes the same turn, which carries the run on**: the round the run asked in is not over, so the answer is never filed as the run's report: the executor is relaunched **continuing the harness's own session** (Claude Code's `--resume`, OpenCode's session) with the answer as its next prompt, so the run that asked goes on knowing everything it had read and tried — in the paused box where the relaunch reuses that box, as [Reuse across launches](#how-a-coding-task-runs) has it (the box was kept, the member the launch picks runs the same harness in the same cell, and the box is this node's to drive), and otherwise (the box reclaimed while the question waited, a fallback onto a member in another cell, or a local box left on the host of a node the seat has since moved off) in a new box, its session restored there from the capture the turn kept when the run parked, and told that the box is new and the branch to check out ([A relaunch continues the turn's session](#a-relaunch-continues-the-turns-session)). Where the session cannot be continued — the turn kept no capture of it, a fallback onto a member of another harness, a box whose harness cannot take it up — the run carries on **fresh**, in a new box, its brief telling it the question, the answer and the branch to check out. Either way it is the same round: the calls the run made before it asked stay on its record, the carried-on run's calls are numbered after them, and its collection judges the round whole, reading every call in the order the run made it. **Its submission is one made after the answer.** The job that carries the run on records where the answer cut the round, the place of the last call the row held when it opened (`answered_through`), and the collection replays only a `submit_work` placed after it: one the asking job made before its question was made without the answer. The cut is that recorded place rather than the round's last `ask_and_wait`, because the row's call log is bounded: past 200 calls it drops from its middle, its ordinary calls first and a submission or an ask only once nothing else is left there, so a long enough run can still lose the ask from it. A job carried on after a loss keeps the cut, and a new round starts without one. No fresh turn and no re-investigation.

   **The answer is sealed on the run, like every other instruction channel.** Its words and who gave them are written onto the row only inside a seal under the fleet keyring (Tier A `secrets.keys`, in a key domain of its own), bound to the run, the question it answers — the job that asked — and the deliveries it was made of ([ADR-0031](../../adr/0031-every-state-log-record-is-signed.md)). Whichever node resumes the run opens the seal first, and the turn is handed only what the seal carried. An answer whose seal does not open as an answer to that question is never handed to the run and never attributed to anybody: it is logged at error (`sandbox_answer_unverified`) and let go of — nothing handed back, nothing announced — and the run waits on its question again, where the next reply answers it. One sealed under a key the node does not hold waits instead, recorded and owed with the seat's mail behind it, logged at warning with the keys the node does hold (`sandbox_answer_key_unknown`), until a node that holds the key takes the seat or this one restarts with it ([Rotating the keyring](secret-store.md#rotating-the-keyring-with-nothing-in-flight-lost)).

   **Recording and resuming were split because the inbox's retry order is the wrong order for an answer.** The answer used to be held by its delivery until a resume succeeded, and a resume that failed handed the *message* back to the broker with a NAK. On JetStream a NAK'd message comes back after its redelivery backoff, **behind** the conversation's newer mail ([event system](event-system.md#delivery-semantics)): so the person's next message reached the still-waiting run first and was taken as its answer, and the real answer came round afterwards to answer whatever the run asked next — or ran as a turn with no context. The in-memory twin replayed a NAK at the head, so every engine test certified the order production never gave. A resume that fails now is the **coordinator's** to retry, on its own schedule, and the person's message is never handed back for it.

   **The seat's later mail waits behind the answer.** While a seat owes any of its runs an answer's resume, the coordinator holds the seat's inbox (the `sandbox` hold a busy seat is held under too) and lifts the hold once the resume has *returned* and, where the resume ended the run, once the run's record is gone, its turn's end on the executor log — recorded off the resume's frame once every capture of the turn has settled ([How a turn ends on the executor log](turn-engine.md#how-a-turn-ends-on-the-executor-log)) — so the person's follow-up is worked as an ordinary turn, with the run's own replies already in the thread it reads, rather than racing the resume or overtaking its end. A follow-up sent while the executor log does not answer waits until it does, which its own alarms name. A hold rather than a park: the mail waits on the broker with its order and its delivery count intact. A later reply is an ordinary message, not a note to the resumed run: a [steer note](turn-engine.md#steering-a-running-turn) is sent with `steer_turn`, and one sent to a parked run waits on the run's record and is read in the brief of the launch that carries the answer ([notes to a running run](#notes-to-a-running-run)).

   **Every delivery on a conversation somebody can reply into, to a seat with a question open, is offered to that match first**, and that follows from step 2 rather than adding to it: a parked run frees its seat, so the answer arrives on the ordinary path looking like any other message — nothing about a chat reply says which parked executor it answers. So the dispatcher offers each such delivery to the parked run *before* consuming it, and only what no parked run claims becomes a turn. A delivery on a conversation nobody can reply into — it carries none, only the per-event fallback, or a schedule's — is never offered: the dispatcher answers it *not mine* without asking the coordination store, so a schedule's next fire runs as a turn of its own beside the fire parked on the question. It is offered after the completion ledger has had its say, so a redelivered trigger that already produced a turn is never taken as an answer as well. A **held** seat offers nothing — its inbox is held, so a reply to a second run of the same seat, waiting while the first is still going, waits with the seat's mail and is offered when the first stops holding the seat. A delivery that **becomes** an answer is recorded in that same ledger, so a copy of it — a redelivery whose acknowledgement was lost — is dropped rather than run as a turn, on whichever node it reaches; while the run still holds the answer, a copy is recognised on the run itself and spent as the answer it is.

   **The offer answers what to DO with the delivery, in four words, and an error is never one of them.** *Consumed* — the answer is recorded on the run (whatever its resume then does), or the delivery is a copy of one already recorded — so it is acked and no turn runs. *Deferred* — a run is waiting and the answer could **not be recorded**, because the coordination store failed or could not say whether the write landed, or the identity directory could not say whether the writer's seat is held by somebody who may act now — so the delivery is **handed back by deferral** and offered again: it keeps its place at the head of the seat's inbox (a NAK would put it behind the person's next message), and the seat's inbox stops until the seat host's next renew. *Not mine* — nothing was awaiting this conversation, the delivery was written before the question, its writer is nobody the question was put to or holds a seat whose person may not act now, or it is on a conversation nobody can reply into (a schedule's, above) — so it is worked as the ordinary message it looks like. *Refused* — the delivery is a reply too large to be the run's answer, and its refusal is now the question's outcome (below) — so it is **handed back by deferral, unspent,** and no turn runs: the run's record carries none of the reply, so the delivery is its only copy, kept at the head of the seat's inbox until the refusal leaves the run. Which of these a refused reply is, is read off the run **as the resume's first attempt left it**: an attempt that let the refusal go — a resume that broke before its turn, a seal that did not open — leaves the delivery *not mine* at once, rather than deferred for a refusal no run holds. A delivery whose answer was recorded is *consumed* even when the run's first resume ends it for good — no conversation to re-enter, a claim that could not be given back, a resume that broke before its turn: the ending hands the reply back through the run's row as below, with the seat's inbox held until the copy is out, so it still reaches the seat once and in order. The inline attempt used to hand the delivery it held on as the ordinary message instead, which needed the store to delete a run still holding the reply on that caller's word — and an ending that could not land then left the reply both on the row and handed on. The ambiguous cases resolve towards the run, because an answer that arrives twice is recoverable and an answer that is spent is not.

   **A lookup that cannot be made is decided by what this node already knows.** It is the ambiguous case in its purest form — the store could not say *which* of the seat's runs is awaiting a reply — so it is not answered by the store at all, but by the seat's own awaiting count, which the node holds in memory (seeded from the store when it claimed the seat, moved since by its own transitions) and can therefore read when the store is exactly what is failing. No awaiting run on the seat: *not mine*, because nothing is owed the delivery and an unreadable store must not swallow an ordinary message — on a company with no parked runs that is every message a seat receives. An awaiting run: *deferred*, because something here **is** waiting for somebody's reply and only which row could not be read. Its hand-backs are bounded by the same count as any other, under a key naming the seat; past it the message is let go to the ordinary route and `sandbox_answer_lookup_exhausted` says so.

   **Both series are bounded, and the bounds are the old ones.** *The resume of a recorded answer* is retried by the coordinator a second after it fails, doubling to a 30-second ceiling (the queue's own failure backoff, deliberately), for at most **10 attempts** and at most the run's own **`pause_ttl_seconds`**, measured from the first failure: about two and a half minutes, long enough for the failures that actually reach it to clear (a seat lease moving to a node that can resume the run, 45 s; a config apply that brings the missing runner; a store blip). A retry the seat's own conditions refuse charges nothing, and it **waits for what refused it** rather than re-checking on a timer: a person's pause is waited out until the pause watch hears them resume the seat, a company with no model until the apply that brings one, a spent budget window until the instant it turns over (or an apply that moves the ceiling), a relaunch every member of which is at a usage limit it already reported until the first is tried again, a node whose posture refuses new work until the reconcile tick that finds it admitting again, and a seat whose ownership cannot be proven until it can: the moment the seat's acquisition is established (which is how an answer the seat inherits from its last holder resumes as soon as the new holder can run it, rather than a heartbeat later), or the renew that proves it again after a store blip, with one heartbeat on the clock behind both for a renew that was merely late. A retry the **node** refuses waits the same way and is charged nothing either: every resume asks the node's admission before it claims the run (see *Completion → resume*), and a draining node refuses it until the drain hands the seat back, which lets the answer go to the seat's next holder, recorded on the run as it was. The inline attempt asks too, under the delivery that carried the answer, whose own admission answers for it. An answer's admission asks its models' limit as well, of the call it makes first, the relaunch: the members of the executor chain a launch can take. It asks the seat's budget too, a window full or too short for a launch. A completion's admission asks neither (only whether its seat is parked, below), because its job is over and its tokens are spent: it is collected first, charged in the window it ran in and its box paused, and its review and relaunch meet the budget themselves (below), while a limit its review's chain (`llm_review`, else `llm`) already reported refuses its resume once it is collected, before its turn runs, and parks the seat on that limit. Refused before its claim, a completion stayed uncollected until the condition cleared: on a full window it was collected only once that window had turned over, its tokens charged into the next one; on a limit its reviewer's entry already knew (every seat on a plan another seat's run had just spent) it was left uncharged for as long as the entry sat out, the seat never parked; and the waiter kept its box awake meanwhile. A completion the admission refuses is acknowledged and kept until the condition clears, and the waiter announces it again meanwhile, so it spends none of its deliveries. A resume that meets its models' limit before its turn writes anything (a limit its review's chain already knew once its job was collected, its reviewer's chain at the call, or a relaunch whose every runner member sits out) is reverted and waits the same way, rather than spending one of its attempts, and a completion's is also held by the node that met the limit, until the instant the limit names: the gate admitted it and would admit it again, so that node refuses the waiter's announcements of the job itself meanwhile, before its gate and its claim, rather than collect the box and run the review into the same limit at every poll. **The resume parks its seat on the limit too**, as a dispatch's turn that met it would ([the provider park](llm-backends.md#falling-back-between-entries)): `seat_provider_parked`, the seat reading `stopped` with the reason `provider`, and the chain's probe, whose answer releases the park and re-fires the resume. While the park stands the admission refuses every resume of the seat, so a completion whose own instant passes while the probe finds the limit still there is refused before its claim, where its reviewer's chain, ready again, admitted it into the same window, and its relaunch launched a box into it, once an hour. An answer the park refuses waits for its release, and for the instant of the probe's next call only while that is still to come: from the probe's instant until its call comes back (a whole run of the entry's harness) the instant is past and the park still stands, so the refusal names none, and a retry that re-checked at that instant re-checked at once, thousands of times a second, until the call came back. A refusal that names an instant already past is waited out as one that names none, whatever refused it. **The hold is that node's alone**, like the limit it waits on: it is kept in the node's memory and never written to the run's record, so a restart forgets it and so does the node's letting go of the seat, and the next announcement asks whoever holds the seat then, whose own models may know of no limit at all. The waiter goes on announcing the job meanwhile and leaves its paused box asleep, since the job was read back already (see [Durability, completion, and observability](#durability-completion-and-observability)). One its seat's budget refuses once it runs (a completion's review on a full window, or a relaunch on a window full or too short for a launch) waits that window out, whether or not it wrote: a completion held on its record, collected in its paused box, which the waiter leaves asleep until the window turns over or a revision moves the ceiling, and an answer reverted to wait uncharged in its own series. A signal that lands while a retry is being refused is not slept through: the wait that follows it re-checks at once. Each of those is a discrete event the engine already observes, and it re-checks exactly the answers waiting on it, at once; nothing runs while the condition holds. A five-minute backstop re-checks a wait whose signal never came, so a missed signal costs a late resume, well inside the 30-minute default `pause_ttl_seconds`, and never a stranded answer. Past the bound the answer is **let go**: the run goes back to waiting on its question, the reply is handed to the seat's inbox as the ordinary message it is (under a new id derived from the original, because the original is spent in the completion ledger), and that reply is **declined** for this question (`declined_answers`) so it never answers it again, and `sandbox_answer_requeue_exhausted` says so. **Letting go is exactly once**, across a crash at any step: one write clears the answer, declines the reply and its copy, and records the copy on the run as owed to the seat (`hand_back`); the copy is published off that record and cleared from it afterwards. A node that stops before the write leaves the answer recorded and nothing published, and the seat's next holder drives it again; one that stops after the write leaves the copy owed, and whoever reads the run next (this node's retry, the seat's next holder before it opens the mailbox, or the run's own ending) publishes it; one that stops after the publish leaves the copy published *and* owed, and the repeat is the same message under the same id, which the inbox's same-id dedupe and the completion ledger collapse. It used to publish first and write second, so a crash between the two delivered the reply twice: as an ordinary message and as the answer a later resume used. The run is **not** ended, because every failure that gets there is a statement about *this node* (no resumer, a parked turn state of an `execstate` version this build does not read, a seat not in this node's company), and the seat's next holder, or this node after a restart, takes over an `answered` run from its record and drives the resume itself. **A run that ends still owing a copy is not deleted until the copy is out**: the store refuses to delete a row that owes the seat anything, so the ending publishes the copy, clears it, and only then deletes the row: a copy published off the deleted record, as it used to be, was lost to a crash between the delete and the publish. A publish that fails keeps the row, holds the seat's inbox behind the copy, and the ending is retried with the hand-back. **What a run owes is bounded by one decline**: a second answer is let go of only once the first one's copies are cleared, so the row never carries more than one delivery batch of copies (20 events by default, the size of the answer they were made from); past it nothing is lost: the second answer stays recorded and owed, its seat stays held, and the retry publishes the copies already owed before it decides again. And **each answer gets its own attempts**: one recorded while an earlier answer's copy is still owed starts a fresh count, rather than being let go of on the strength of the earlier answer's spent one.

   **A reply that leaves its run without a turn taking it goes back to the seat, once — whoever ends the run.** The run can end under its own resume, on the inline attempt or a retry — it has no parked turn state to resume, its claim cannot be given back, or the resume broke before its turn began — or under a seat that left the company, or a reap, and the delivery that carried the reply was spent when the answer was recorded, so nothing else would ever bring the reply to the seat. The ending is [decided on the run's record](#how-a-coding-task-runs) first, and **the decision settles whether the reply goes back**: a turn cannot take the answer once the ending is decided, so one no turn took by then goes back to the seat, and one a turn took has been used and goes with the run. A reply that arrives once the ending is decided is matched to no run, since the run takes no answer from then on: it is the ordinary message it is, at once. Matched on the run's status, which an ending leaves as it was until the record is deleted, it lost the record to the ending on every read and came back deferred until the ending finished. The ending then **lets the answer go** in one write that takes it off the run and records its copy as owed (`hand_back`), and hands the copy back before it deletes the row — **the store will not delete a run still holding a reply the ending owes the seat**, so an ending can never take the reply with the record. An ending is decided only under its own terms: a hand-back that landed and reported a failure, or an old holder's hand-back landing under a reap, puts a run back to `answered` with the reply on it, and a claim's own ending is licensed for the claim alone, so it decides nothing about that run, which is left to its retry, reply and all. The crash guarantees are a decline's: stopped before the let-go's write, the reply is still on the row and whoever reads it next lets it go; after it, the next reader publishes the copy; after the publish, the repeat is the same message; after the clear, nothing is published again. An ending that cannot be finished — the let-go not recorded, the copy not published, the announcement refused, or **the delete itself not taken** — keeps the row and its terms: the seat's inbox is held behind it, and this node finishes it, once the store takes it, exactly as decided — the box included, where the ending reclaims that only after its decision. A delete the store did not take used to be left for the seat's next recovery pass, which comes only when the seat moves, and the record it left was a claim, which holds the seat: the seat took no mail at all until it changed hands. And a kept ending used to be finished without the reclaim it was decided with, so the only record of a paused box was deleted and the box billed, named by nothing, until its provider's TTL. A reply used to be published straight off the retry *after* the ending had deleted the row, and later straight off a refused record, so a crash between the delete and the publish, or a broker refusing that publish too, lost it.

   **A claim whose node stopped before its turn took the answer is revived, and the run resumed with it.** The coordinator claims an answered run for its resume, and between that claim and the resumed turn lie steps that can stop the process — a crash, a deploy, a seat that moves. The row reads `resumed` from the claim until it is settled either way, so the seat's next holder, finding the claim as it takes the seat, could not tell a reply nobody had acted on from one a turn had used, and read every one of them as spent: the person's answer was lost without a trace. The resume now records, in one compare-and-set at the last moment before the turn runs, that **the turn took the answer** (`answer.taken_at`); a resume that cannot make that write does not run, and a release that gives the claim back for a retry clears it. A claim whose answer no turn took never used it — and never touched the box, because the take is the first thing a resumed turn does — so the seat's next holder **gives the claim back to the answer** (`sandbox_answer_revived`): in one compare-and-set the run is `answered` again, fenced to the holder's lease, with the lost claim counted on the answer (`answer.lost_claims`, `answer.first_lost_at`), and the holder drives its resume exactly as it drives any answer its last holder recorded and never resumed with — the seat's mail held behind it, and the box still paused or, past `pause_ttl_seconds`, reclaimed and the run re-seeded from its branch, as any answered run's is. It used to be reaped, the run ended and the reply handed to the seat as an ordinary message, so the answer reached the seat but never the run it answered, which was lost.
