A revival the store does not confirm — a write that failed, or a claim that moved under it and could not be read again — is retried by the holder on the hand-back's spacing with the seat's mail held behind it (`sandbox_answer_revive_owed`), never read as a reason to end the run; finding it landed after all, or the claim given back, the holder resumes the run it finds. A claim a node made itself and lost track of — the claim's write reported a failure and landed — is held by no resume either, but it is **not a lost claim**: no node stopped. The answer's next attempt on that node finds it and gives it back to the answer under the lease it was taken under (`sandbox_answer_claim_given_back`), **uncounted**, and goes on with the answer's own attempts — resuming, or declining the answer once those are spent, never ending the run. That holds on the answer's last attempt too, and when its window lapses under a claim: the attempt that spends the budget gives the claim back first and then hands the reply back once, rather than declining a run that is not answered any more — which the store refused, leaving the run in a claim nothing drove, the person's answer on it and the seat held behind it. The run used to sit in that claim, the seat busy behind it, until the seat happened to move; and once that was fixed, a few of those claims in a row were counted as dead ones and ended the run as an `abandoned_tail` on a node that had never stopped. **Revival is bounded, by two counts kept on the row**, because a node that stops resets every count it kept: after **3** revivals of one answer (`sandbox.MaxAnswerRevivals`), or once the run's own `pause_ttl_seconds` has passed since the first claim of it died, the next claim that dies is reaped instead. Three claims dying in a row is not bad luck but a resume that takes its node down before its turn begins, and reviving it again would hand it to the next holder to fall over the same way, the seat's mail held behind it each time. The store will not delete that run with the reply on it, so the reap lets the answer go as above — the copy handed back, the run ended, and its loss announced once as `abandoned_tail`, saying why it was not resumed again and that the reply went back to the seat (`sandbox_answer_not_revived`). A claim whose turn took the answer has used it, and a copy or a second resume now would answer the person twice: it is spent, the run reaped, and the announcement says nothing went back. **The take, the revival and the ending are exclusive in the store's own compare-and-set**: a revival and an ending both refuse an answer a turn took, a revived run has no claim left to take it under, and a decided ending takes no take — so whichever lands first wins, whatever any lease says, and a node still on its way to its turn when the seat's next holder revives or ends the run cannot also answer the person with it. (Only the claim's own ending lets go of an answer its turn took: a turn that gave its claim back as a retry has said nothing it did reached anybody, and once the hand-back cannot land, no retry will hand the reply to a turn again.) The reap **fences the row first**, moving the run to its own, newer lease, so a node that lost the seat but is still on its way to that turn has its take refused and the person is answered once, through the seat's next holder, rather than also by a turn the seat's lease will stop. That fence holds because **every claim carries the claimant's own lease**: the claim stamps it on the row and every write the claim makes after it — the take, the hand-back, the ending — carries it back off the row; a launch stamps the lease that launched it the same way, and the seat's new holder stamps its own on every run it recovers, parked ones included. And **a take, a claim and the record of an answer are refused under no lease at all** on a run any lease owns, which other writes let through so that a recovery holding no lease yet can still write: a run launched under no lease, or one nothing re-stamped, sat at the zero epoch, and a stalled claimant that held no lease took the answer on a row its successor had fenced — the person was answered by its turn and by the copy. **A node that knows it lost the seat writes nothing on it.** Its seat host no longer holds the lease, and every write the coordinator would make on the seat's behalf — recording an answer, claiming one or a completion, giving a claim back, launching a run — is refused before it reaches the store (`sandbox_seat_not_held`): a reply or an answer by turn goes back to reach the seat's holder, a completion is handed back to be fired again for the holder, and a retry this node was making is dropped for the holder's recovery pass to drive. It used to write under the zero fence, which constrains nothing, so a node that had noticed it lost the seat recorded and claimed a person's answer past its successor's fence, took it and ran the turn on a seat it no longer held; one that has not noticed yet writes under its old lease, which the successor's fence outranks — an answer it records is refused rather than left on a run its successor recovered as waiting, which nothing would then drive. The reap reads the row again after fencing it, so a claim its old holder gave back first is taken over as the run it is, owed its retry, rather than reaped. Where the reap can neither fence nor read the row, it is revived or reaped as listed: the revival is a compare-and-set that stamps the holder's lease itself and refuses an answer a turn took, and past the bounds the store's refusal to delete a reply no turn took is what hands the reply back. A resume that broke before its turn began — a panic re-entering the conversation — is settled without a retry like every abandoned resume, but nothing ran, so its reply is handed back the same way rather than read as spent, and the run is announced lost once, as `resume_broken`: no turn ran to publish a completion of its own, and the run used to end with a guard breach and nothing else, so the turn parked on it read as parked for good.

   **A reply too large to be the answer is the question's refusal** ([ADR-0114](../../adr/0114-a-reply-too-large-for-its-run-is-its-questions-refusal.md)). The record carries an answer up to **512 KiB** (below), which a reply reaches at about 3.4 times its words in plain text and up to about 20 in markup: a pasted log or configuration of a hundred KiB is taken whole, but five 32 KiB Chatlet messages delivered together, or one very long Slack message or GitLab note, are not. Such a reply is not taken, and its **refusal is recorded as the question's outcome** in the answer's place, by the same compare-and-set: the run reads `answered`, holding the reply's delivery ids, when it was posted and who sent it, sealed, and beside them the refusal — how many messages the reply was, what it came to as the record would hold it (rounded up) and what an answer may — and none of its words — nor of where it was posted, beyond its address (the transport, the conversation and its thread, as ids, up to 4 KiB), which the resumed turn raises its working indicator in. From that write on, **while the refusal is the run's**, the question no longer waits, so nothing the person writes afterwards (an "any luck?") is taken for its answer. The refusal is then resumed exactly as an answer is, with every guarantee above: owed with the seat's inbox held behind it, retried, taken once, revived, or let go of. **A refusal let go of** — declined after its attempts, or one whose seal does not open here — puts the question back to waiting under the anchor it was asked at, as an answer let go of does, and the next qualifying reply answers it, a short message written since included; the let-go is recorded as `executor_answered` with the outcome `declined` (below), since the refusal's own record said the run would be told. The resumed run is told, in the engine's words and where an answer would reach it — its own session's next prompt, or a fresh run's brief — that the person replied, that the reply was too large to take (the message count and both figures), so it was given none of it; that if it still needs it, it asks again with `ask_and_wait`, saying in the question why, for a much shorter form or another way to get it — a file in its checkout, committed to its branch, a path it can read, or a link; and that otherwise it carries on and says in its final reply that the reply never reached it. A question it asks again takes its own `asked_at`, so whatever the person wrote before it is an ordinary message to the seat. The reply itself stays where it was: in the conversation, and in its delivery, which the seat's inbox keeps (*refused*, above) until a turn keeps the refusal and it is spent, or the refusal is let go of — after its attempts, by an ending no turn reached, or a reap — and it comes round as the ordinary message it is: **back to the seat the way it came**, as an answer let go of goes, with nothing handed back from the row. A run that ends before any turn told it of the refusal says so: its `executor_failed` names a reply too large for it to take, which it was never told of, going back to the seat. A reply to a run that was answered another way, moved on to another question or ended meanwhile is matched to nothing, whatever it weighs, and is the ordinary message every reply after an answer is. **The turn's reviewer is told of the refusal**: the reply is in none of the round's calls, where the run's question asked again reads as the same question sent twice, so the relaunch that carries the round on keeps each refusal's message count and figures for the round's review (never its words), and the review prompt shows them beside the log (`## Replies the agent did not get`), saying that a question asked again after one is the follow-up the engine told the run to make, which the duplicate rule does not hold against it.

   **A reply's original delivery is spent the moment the reply leaves the run.** When a turn takes the answer — on the inline attempt and on a retry alike, at the same commit that records the take — and before the write that lets an answer go, by a decline, an ending or a reap, the delivery that carried it is recorded as worked in the [completion ledger](seat-ownership.md#the-completion-ledger); a reap that finds an answer a turn took records it again, for a node that stopped between the take and its spend. A node that recorded the answer — or took it inline, holding the delivery unacknowledged through the whole resumed turn — and stopped before acknowledging the delivery leaves it to come round again, and by then the run may have used the reply (its turn may already have answered the person), handed a copy back, relaunched, or be gone, so nothing on the run would recognise it; the ledger drops it instead of running it as a second message — or, for an answer by turn, instead of asking the run about it again and announcing it `gone` or `not_awaiting` after it resumed the run. That holds for an answer by turn because its route reads the ledger before it reaches the run, and because its delivery is recorded whatever its type: the ledger otherwise keeps only the types that run a turn, which an answer by turn never does, so its spend used to record nothing at all. It used to be spent only once a retried resume's turn had *returned*, so a node that stopped mid-turn left the original to run as a second turn on whichever node took the seat. A reply spent and still on its row is not lost: the row, never the delivery, is what resumes it or hands it back, and a copy handed back goes under an id of its own. **A refused reply is the exception**, because its row carries no copy: its delivery is spent only once a turn that took the refusal *keeps* it — returns having run, breaks after it began, or dies holding it, as the reap of its claim finds — and never at the take itself, which a turn that broke before reaching anybody gives back, nor at a let-go, which is exactly when the delivery has to come round as the ordinary message it is. The delivery in hand while the refusal's first resume runs is acknowledged on that attempt's own account once its turn keeps the refusal, as an answer's is at its record, and never on the completion ledger's, which fails open: read there, an unreadable ledger handed the seat, as a new message, a reply its run had just been told never reached it. Where another attempt's turn kept it, the run's row says so: the relaunch that told the run keeps the refused question for the round's review, and only that turn writes it. A copy that comes round once the refusal has left the row (after a retry's turn kept it, or to the seat's next holder) is dropped by the completion ledger, one event at a time, as every redelivered event is, and that read fails open as every completion read does: a miss runs it as the ordinary message it is.

   *A delivery whose answer could not be recorded* is handed back by deferral, and that series has three clauses. The first is the one the *message* carries: every hand-back spends one of the message's deliveries, so the offer stops while **5 deliveries are still left** and those are kept for the ordinary route. That reserve is read on **each message's own** count, never on the partition's: a delivery is a whole partition, its messages sit at different counts as a matter of course, and gated on the partition's number a person's clarification reply on its *first* delivery would be refused the answer route whenever an older message on the same conversation is near its own budget — and refused for good. One message with the deliveries to spare is enough to keep the offer open; `sandbox_answer_offered_over_a_spent_sibling` says when the hand-back may spend the last delivery of a co-partitioned one, and `sandbox_answer_headroom_reserved` when the route lets go. The second is **10 attempts on one message** inside one process, and the third the run's own `pause_ttl_seconds` (`sandbox_answer_record_exhausted`). A second reply gets its own count, and one run keeps at most four of them at a time.

   **Two questions open on one direct message** are told apart by the thread each was asked in: the identity admits both — that is what makes a DM one conversation — and the run whose own partition matches the reply's wins, with the most recently parked question answering a top-level reply that matches neither.

### Who a question is put to

The audience a harness names is its own words, so the park resolves it — once, against the org chart, in the same write as the question — into the seats the question waits on (`audience_handles` on the run's record and on [`GET /executors`](../reference/api-endpoints.md#get-executors)):

| Audience | Put to |
|---|---|
| `requester` | The seat whose message, notice or ask woke the turn that launched the run. The launch records it on the run (`requester`), because the park runs when the job finishes — days later, perhaps on another node — and sees no trigger. |
| `manager` | The seat's manager. |
| `team` | The lead of the seat's unit and the **people** in it; the unit's other agents are left out, since the seat could ask them itself. |
| a name | The one colleague it names exactly — a handle, an id on a chat surface, or a role name, case aside. A partial or approximate match is not a person: a guessed colleague is somebody else's question in their inbox. |

When the audience names nobody the chart has — no requester was recorded, the seat has no manager, the name matches nobody — the question is put to the seat's **lead chain** instead (its managers, nearest first, or, for a seat nobody manages, the leads of the units that hold it) and `audience_fallback` says so. A question is never parked waiting on nobody while somebody is answerable for the seat, and a person can tell a question put to them from one that reached them because nobody else could be named. `?audience=<handle>` on the board is "what is waiting on me".

### Answering a parked run

A reply on the question's conversation (step 3 above) is one way to answer. The other is to **name the run by its turn**, with the operator tool [`answer_executor`](../reference/api-endpoints.md#answering-a-parked-executor) — from your own assistant over `/operator/mcp`, or from the dashboard. It is the only way for a run started by a **schedule, a task assignment or a colleague's ask**: a task assignment's and a colleague's ask's turns have no conversation, so the run's record names none, and a schedule's names its schedule's, which no person writes into — so nothing a person says in chat can reach any of them. Until this existed such a run's question waited out its pause TTL however promptly somebody knew the answer.

In the dashboard it is the **Answer** on a parked run's banner — on the run's page and its rail in Live › Executors, on Live › Now running's "Waiting on a person", in the Inbox and on Home. The banner names who the question is put to (the audience resolved when the run parked), how long the box is held, and whether a reply on the turn's conversation would reach it too.

**Who may answer is the run's requester — the person whose message, notice or ask woke the turn that launched it — or whoever leads the run's seat, or an administrator (`admin`)**, and never a seat: the person who asked for the work answers what it asks them, and nobody who merely leads that person does (see [the authority table](identity-and-access.md#the-sixteen-rules)).

`answer_executor{turn_id, launch_id, choice, answer}` checks the run is waiting (`not_running` if it is not), that it waits on the question the person read (`launch_id`, the job that asked it; `stale_version` once the run has moved on to another) and, where it names a `choice`, that the choice is one of the options the run's question offers (refused, naming them, otherwise, and on an open question); an answer is a choice, words, or both. It then puts an `executor_answer_given` event on **the seat's control topic**, answering `pending`. The control topic, not the seat's inbox, because the answer is addressed to the run, like a completion: the inbox is held while the seat's executor holds the seat for any turn and is worked oldest first after that, so an answer that rode it waited behind every launch and every older message, and on a busy seat it never arrived. The node holding the seat takes it off the control topic and hands it to the coordinator, which **records it on the run** at once (the run reads `answered` from that moment) exactly as it records a chat reply, the answer, the credential and the person it names, sealed, and the event it arrived in, and carries the run on with it as above, the answer attributed to the person who gave it. From the record on, an answer by turn is a recorded answer like any other, except that its first attempt is queued rather than made inline: the resume starts a turn, so it waits until the seat's executor holds it for no other turn, no other turn of the seat is running on the node (a seat runs one turn at a time) and no person has the seat paused, and is then the seat's next turn, with the seat's mail held behind it. Its retries are the coordinator's, a resumed turn **takes** it at the last moment before it runs, and a claim whose node stops before its turn took it is revived by the seat's next holder, which resumes the run with it (see [Mid-run clarification](#mid-run-clarification-ask_and_wait)). It used to be resumed with directly, held only by the claim the resume took: a node that stopped between that claim and the turn left a claim its successor reaped as an abandoned tail, and the answer's delivery, coming round to the new holder, found the run gone. It is **never a turn**: whatever it finds, the delivery is spent there, and only an answer the run is still owed but could not be recorded against (the store could not be read, or the record could not be confirmed) is handed back to the broker for its spaced retry, a redelivery that finds the record landed after all is spent as the answer it already is. Once it has become something, recorded, declined, `gone`, `not_awaiting`, it is recorded as worked in the [completion ledger](seat-ownership.md#the-completion-ledger), and so is an answer its run spends (taken by a resumed turn, let go of, reaped); the route reads the ledger first, so a redelivery after either is acknowledged without reaching the run and without a second announcement (`sandbox_answer_by_turn_already_spent`). That hand-back is a NAK, and on this route it is safe in a way it is not for a chat reply: the answer names its run, so coming back behind the seat's newer mail lets no later message take its place, and it cannot be taken as the answer to anything else. An answer already recorded against the question wins: the run is `answered`, no longer waiting, and the answer by turn reports `not_awaiting`. **An answer by turn the run could not be resumed with is let go of** like a chat reply, after the same attempts: the run waits on its question again, and the copy handed back to the seat's control topic (it has no ordinary form to become) is spent there and announced `declined`, once, so the person knows to answer again; it is never recorded against that question again. One let go of because its run ended is announced `gone` the same way. An answer given while the seat is [busy executing another turn](#how-a-coding-task-runs) is recorded at once and its resume waits until that turn's executor settles or parks; one given while the seat is paused is recorded and resumed when the pause lifts; one given while the holder restarts is still on the control topic afterwards. **It answers the question it was given against, and no other.** The resume's wait can outlast the question, and so can a NAK's backoff or a retried copy: somebody else's answer resumes the run meanwhile, its turn relaunches (a reviewer's send-back), and the new job parks on a question of its own, and an answer that named only its run claimed whatever the run waited on by then, resuming it a second time with the first question's answer presented as the second's. So the event names the question too, the job the run held when it asked (`launch_id`), off the row `answer_executor` checked, and the run is resumed with it only while it still waits on that question; once it has moved on, the answer is spent as `not_awaiting`. Every new question comes with a new job, so the job identifies it. The question is **required**: an event naming none is spent without resuming the run, with the reason logged (`sandbox_answer_names_no_question`), it cannot be told from an answer to a question the run has moved past, and `answer_executor` never delivers one. Two answers to one question, a chat reply and an answer by turn, or two people, or a retried `answer_executor`, contend for the one record, and the run is resumed once, however far apart they arrive.

**A node that has let go of the seat hands the answer on once.** An answer or completion that reaches a node whose seat host no longer holds the seat is deferred: it goes back to the head of the control topic for the seat's holder, at the cost of one delivery, and the node stops consuming that topic until its release detaches it. It used to be handed back on the broker's backoff, one delivery per attempt, which walked a person's answer to the dead-letter subject while a stale node kept refusing it. A refusal on the run store's word alone (a newer lease stamped the row) while the host still holds the seat is still handed back on the backoff, because quiescing a seat the node does hold would leave it hearing no answer or completion at all; the heartbeat settles that disagreement within a beat. The fencing epoch the store compares is monotonic across restarts on every topology ([Coordination](coordination.md#the-leases-follow-coordinationtype-the-shared-state-does-not)), so a seat's own holder is never refused as older than its runs.

**Both routes leave a record.** `executor_answered` names the run, the question it answered (`launch_id`, `question`, `question_context`, `decision`, `asked_at` and the audience), the option chosen (`choice`) and the person's own words (`answer`), which is the one account of the question that outlives the run: a person's [answered decisions](../guides/worklet.md#asking-for-a-decision) read it back from the event log. It also names the route (`chat` or `operator`), who answered (the chat sender, or `answered_by` and `answered_by_kind` as `iam.ActorFor` names the person, the seat they hold, which every person does, with the credential they came through in `operator_id`) and what the answer became: `resumed`, `not_awaiting` (another answer had it first, a chat reply too large for the run closed the question first, or the run is running a job), `gone` (the run had ended), `declined` (an answer by turn every attempt failed to resume the run with, let go of; the run waits on its question again — or, on the chat route with `answer_bytes`, a refused reply's refusal let go of before any turn told the run, the reply going back to the seat as the message it was and the question waiting again, naming nobody where the refusal's seal did not open), `unoffered` (an answer by turn whose choice the question it names does not offer, spent without being recorded; the question still waits) or `too_large` (an answer past what its run may take as one, with what it came to and what an answer may, `answer_bytes` and `answer_max_bytes`, never its words: a chat reply too large for the run's record to carry as its answer, weighed as that record would hold it, whose refusal is the question's outcome, the run to be resumed told so — one record per run, question and delivery, published under the id and the instant, the reply's own, those derive, and never a `resumed` beside it; or an answer by turn that says more than 16 KiB, spent, the question still waiting for a shorter one). An answer by turn is measured as `answer_executor` measures it, trimmed and before any secret in it is redacted, so an answer the tool took is never refused after it, and one that did not come through the tool is held to the same 16 KiB. The chat route used to leave nothing at all. A chat message that matched no parked run was never an answer and records nothing; an answer recorded on its run whose resume is still being retried has not become anything yet and records nothing until it has. A chat reply let go of becomes the ordinary message it is, and records nothing either — but for a refused reply, whose `too_large` record said the run would be told, and whose let-go is recorded as `declined`.

The durable state is always **git plus the persisted row** — the WIP branch is pushed *before* the agent asks, so the paused box is a fast-resume optimization, never the store. Clarifications chain within the same round of the same turn — an answer carries the run on rather than spending an iteration — bounded by the budget: every relaunch is held to its [launch floor](#what-a-continued-launch-costs) — `turn_engine.executor_min_budget_tokens`, or more for one that continues the turn's session — and each run to its `max_turns`. The wait for the person has no deadline: `pause_ttl_seconds` bounds only how long the paused box is held, and past it the pause reaper below releases the box and the answer's relaunch starts from the branch in a fresh box, continuing the turn's session there where it can.

**The pause reaper.** A human reply can take days, and E2B holds a paused box — billing for the snapshot — until something kills it. Nothing else would, so the completion poll enforces `pause_ttl_seconds` on the same tick it uses for running jobs. The box's age is durable (`paused_at` on the run record, stamped when the box is paused), so the deadline survives an engine restart — and a seat handoff — instead of resetting with the process. Past the TTL, a parked run gets:

1. **the box killed by id** with `sandbox.Provider.Kill`, never `Connect`, which auto-resumes a paused sandbox and would boot the VM back up purely to shut it down;
2. **the box released on the row** (`sandbox_id` cleared, `paused_at` cleared) — which is also what makes the answer's relaunch provision a fresh box (a re-seed of its working tree) rather than reattach to a dead id;
3. **the run flipped to `reseed`** — still claimable, still matched by conversation, because the run is not over. The answer can arrive days later and still carries the run on: the same round is relaunched in a new box — continuing the asking run's kept session there, told the answer, that its box is new and the pushed branch to check out, or, where the turn kept none it can continue, as a fresh run whose brief tells it the question, the answer and the pushed branch to check out ([A relaunch continues the turn's session](#a-relaunch-continues-the-turns-session)). A run given [repositories on Gitlet](#repositories-on-gitlet) is relaunched with the same grants, each repository on the branch it worked on — the branch it pushed to, so a fresh clone holds its work — with a repository it was given to read kept to reading, its cone kept and its `open_change` kept: the parked run opened none, so the run that finishes the work does.

**A pause that was never recorded is still a pause.** Stamping `paused_at` is a *second* store write, made once the box is already paused, and it is warn-only: the job is over by then, and refusing a collected result over a timestamp would throw the whole run away. So a run can park holding a snapshot its row does not date — and reading that as "no snapshot" skipped exactly the box that needed reaping, on a remote provider, for ever. A parked row naming a box therefore *is* a held box: with no stamp, the deadline runs from the row's own last write, which on a parked run is the park itself (a park that does not land is not a park — see step 2 above), and which can only be later than the pause it failed to record, never earlier. The same reading draws the dashboard's `paused_at`, so the screen and the reclaim cannot disagree about which boxes are being paid for. A pause held for any *other* reason takes no such fallback: every one of those lasts a single dispatch and is settled by the tail that made it, so there a missing stamp means the box is live.

**An answer still waiting on its resume is still a clarification wait.** A recorded answer (`answered`) is resumed by whichever node holds the seat, when the seat's own conditions allow — and a seat that no node holds, or one that stays paused, can leave that resume waiting as long as the question waited. Its box is held for the whole of that wait, so the reaper treats it exactly as it treats a parked run: it kills the box and clears it from the row (with the same last-write fallback when no pause instant was recorded). It does not change the status. The answer stays recorded and is still owed its resume, which re-seeds from the pushed branch just as a `reseed` run's would. If the answer is later let go, the question reopens as `reseed` rather than `awaiting_clarification`, because the box it was parked in no longer exists. A resume that has already claimed the run moved it out of `answered`, so the reaper's flip loses, exactly as it does against an answer's claim.

`pause_ttl_seconds: 0` takes the same path with no snapshot at all: the box is torn down the moment the run blocks and the run parks straight into `reseed`.

The reaper is scoped to **clarification waits** on purpose. The lifecycle pauses a box in one other place — between collecting a completion and the turn's settle or relaunch — but that pause lasts a single dispatch and is settled by the tail that made it, so expiring it would only break a relaunch's reuse. The one paused box no tail will ever settle is a run whose engine died mid-resume; that one is reaped by the coordinator's recovery pass when the seat is next acquired, the single moment the row is unambiguously abandoned.

### A call held for approval

A run parks the same way on a call to an MCP tool at Needs approval
([Asking a person before a tool runs](../guides/tools-and-mcp.md#asking-a-person-before-a-tool-runs),
[ADR-0077](../../adr/0077-a-tool-call-that-needs-approval-parks-its-run-in-the-shape-a-runs-ask-does.md)).
The [bridge](#the-tool-bridge--a-seats-own-tools-from-inside-a-box) does not
make the call: the seat's surface records it on the run's row as the job's
question (the tool, its server, its definition's digest, the work item, and the
arguments sealed under the fleet keyring), answers the harness that the call was
not made and to commit, push and end its turn, and records the call as the one
that ended the job, so the waiter collects the run at once. The collection parks
the run on the question exactly as on `ask_and_wait`, its seat freed, and the job
takes no submission after it.
Where the run's turn was woken by a person's answer to a question its seat
asked, the held call names that decision: the question and the option chosen
ride the held call (`acting`) and its question's context ("It carries out a
decision on ENG-9: jane answered ... with ..."), and the approvals list says
it, so whoever approves the call reads a step of an answer already given. The
approval card on the server's page in Settings › MCP & tools draws the same
sentence above the call. The
answers that woke the turn ride the run's row, so a later job of the same turn
names them too. A turn woken by anything else, or by an agent's answer, names
no decision.

Only `POST /mcp-approvals/{id}` answers it, never a chat reply on the run's
conversation and never `answer_executor`. The resume the answer drives makes the
call itself, through the seat's own tools on the node holding the seat, after
marking it on the row; it never makes it twice, and hands the result (or the
refusal, for `deny`) to the job that carries the work on, as its next prompt in
its own session or in a fresh run's brief. A held call names no branch, so a
fresh run is told to find the work-in-progress branch the run pushed before it
ended its turn.

---

## Durability, completion, and observability

**Pending runs survive restarts, and seat handoffs.** Every detached run is a record in the fleet's [coordination store](coordination.md#what-the-fleet-shares), not in the node's own database, and the difference is the whole reason it is durable at all: a run outlives its turn, its process and sometimes its *node*, and the node that owns the seat next is the one whose recovery pass has to find it. Per-node, that pass listed nothing, so the parked turn was unreachable and a billed box was neither resumed nor reaped. On acquiring a seat (boot included), and for every seat a node already holds when an apply brings its sandbox up, the coordinator's recovery pass (`Coordinator.RecoverSeat`) takes ownership of the seat's running jobs and marks the seat busy again; the waiter then drives those jobs to completion. Recovery also takes over a run left in `resumed`: that state can only mean the previous engine died between claiming the run's tail — a completion, or a person's answer — and settling it, so nothing will ever pick it up again (the at-most-once claim already flipped) and its paused box would stay billed with nothing to reclaim it. A completion's claim is reaped. A claim taken for a person's recorded answer that no turn took yet is **revived** instead — given back to the answer, and the run resumed with it — and reaped, its answer handed back to the seat, only past the revival's bounds; one whose resumed turn had already taken the answer is reaped as spent (see [Mid-run clarification](#mid-run-clarification-ask_and_wait)). The store revives and lets go of only an answer no turn took, and will not delete a run still holding one, so neither can happen beside the take. The reap fences the row to the new holder's lease first, so the node that stopped cannot take the answer after all, and reads it again, so a claim that node gave back first is taken over as the run it is rather than reaped. Every run the seat holds is fenced the same way, a parked one included, so a node that lost the seat cannot claim the answer that arrives for it either. A run left in `launching` is reaped for the same reason one step earlier: that engine died between starting the job and writing the parked turn state a resume reads, so there is nothing to resume and never will be. Both are only safe to reap *here*: taking the seat's lease is what proves no live process is still driving the row, which for a `launching` row is the whole argument, since one on a seat this node already owns is a launch happening right now. A restart never orphans a job: an E2B box runs server-side, independent of the engine, and any process can reconnect by `sandbox_id`; a `direct` or `container` box keeps running on its host while the engine process alone restarts, and the node that launched it picks it up again when it is back (below). A restart that takes more than the engine process with it — its container, its systemd unit's control group, its host — takes a `direct` box's job too, and that job is not collected as though it had finished: its round is carried on (*A job lost with its host*, below).

### The run's record, and what it costs to read

A run's **record** holds what is decided about it: its status and launch, its owner and fencing epoch, its box, its parked turn, its question and answer, its charge, its grants on [Gitlet](gitlet.md#coding-boxes) and the floor its box reads at, what its turn holds from Gitlet's expiry. What a job can make large is kept BESIDE it, each part under a key of its own, which the run's end removes with the record and the [marker sweep](coordination.md#removal-markers-are-swept) takes where a node stopped between the two: the secrets the job's box was handed, sealed, for a [kept session's scrub](#each-launchs-session-kept) (up to 64 KiB before sealing), and **the bridged tool log**: every call the job made through the [tool bridge](#the-tool-bridge--a-seats-own-tools-from-inside-a-box), its arguments and its output, up to 200 kept with the middle dropped past that, each call's place in the round numbered by the session before the call runs, after the last call the log held when the job opened. The collection reads the log to rebuild the phase the job ran, a node that recovers the run reads it to open the run's bridge again, and Live › Executors reads it to show it — to a reader shown the run's body, and to nobody else.

The log carries the one condition its appends are taken on, the job whose calls it takes, so an append is one compare-and-set of the log and never touches the record (the record is written after it only when the call moved what the job's bridged calls cost). A launch opens the log to its job once the record names the job, in one write that also decides what the job keeps: a new round starts the log afresh, a job that carries the round on keeps its calls, and one carried on by a person's answer cuts the round after the last of them. What it keeps is decided by the round the record names, written by the launch's own write to the record: a launch whose opening of the log failed, or whose node stopped before it, leaves the log on an earlier job of the same round, and the relaunch that carries the round on still keeps its calls and takes the cut the answer owed them. A launch whose own record is removed before it opens the log (its job was stopped in that moment) is refused rather than recorded again. The run's end removes the record and then its log; the store writes a log only beside a record, so a call landing as the run ends leaves nothing behind, and a log a node left by stopping between the two removals is taken by the [marker sweep](coordination.md#removal-markers-are-swept).

Every flip of the run is a compare-and-set of that record **whole**, and every listing reads every record whole — the completion poll and each seat's busy check and recovery, the pause reaper, the live panel's reconcile on every node, Live › Executors — and so does every git request a coding box makes, which reads its run's record before anything else, and every ref answer past the box's floor, which writes it. None of them moves the tool log, nor the sealed set of secrets the job's box was handed. Measured on three members with every bucket at three replicas, from a member not leading the runs' bucket, for a job parked with a 32 KiB frozen context and a full log, its launch numbered on the record and no capture stamped there yet: the record is 37 957 bytes once its box's attach has said which brief the job was handed (`"brief":"fresh"`, 16 bytes; a job whose launch continues no session records no `session_start`), and a box's read of it takes 0.38 ms and a raise of its floor 0.90 ms at the median (0.61 and 2.40 at the 99th percentile, measured on the benchmark's record of 37 941 bytes, which names no box), while the 478 374-byte log is moved only by its own readers and by an append, 10.6 ms (15.6), or 10.8 ms (15.3) for a call that moved the job's spend and so writes the record's total too. The sealed set of secrets beside it is about 0.7 KiB for a launch handed a model key, two MCP servers' tokens and its own telemetry and Gitlet tokens (computed from its encoding), and is read only by a capture and the next launch's opening. With the log on the record it was 516 173 bytes, 5.8 ms and 11.1 ms (7.9 and 26.0), for every one of those requests ([Gitlet → Coding boxes](gitlet.md#coding-boxes)).

### What a run's record and its log keep

A run's record and its tool log are each **one value** in the coordination store, read and written whole by every write that touches it, and one value is one broker message: at most 8 MiB (`queue.MaxPayloadBytes`), its headers included. So each is held to a byte budget, **8,384,512 bytes** (`sandbox.RowBytes`: the payload bound less 4 KiB for the broker's headers), and every field it carries has a share of it, counted in the bytes the store's JSON encoder writes, escapes included (a `<` is six bytes, a control byte six, a quote two):

| Value | Share | Bytes | Held by |
|---|---|---|---|
| The tool log | Its calls: `calls`, `elided`, `elided_calls`, `absorbed_calls` | 3,473,408 (3.3125 MiB) | each call's own share, two hundred calls, and the identities of the calls the log counts without listing them |
| The tool log | Its calls in flight: `in_flight` | 1,048,576 (1 MiB) | 256 entries, each its call's identity and the id its harness gave it, at most 4 KiB |
| The tool log | Everything else: the tool names of the turn's earlier rounds, the log's own fields, and room for what a later version adds | 3,862,528 (3.68 MiB) | each field's own bound |
| The record | The parked turn's state (`execute_state`) | 2,097,152 (2 MiB) | the park, which condenses it to fit and refuses it past that |
| The record | Everything else, and 256 KiB for fields a later version adds | 6,287,360 (6 MiB less 4 KiB) | each field's own bound, held by the write that puts it on the record |

The sealed set of secrets beside them is the third value, a fraction of the same budget: at most 64 KiB before sealing.

**A call's share.** Each call keeps up to **16 KiB**: its arguments up to **8 KiB** as the log holds them, its answer up to **4 KiB**, its name up to 256 bytes, the id its harness gave it up to 64 bytes, and room for what a later version adds to a call. Past its share, a call's arguments **keep their shape**: their largest values are left out one by one, so the short ones (the ids, paths and names that say what the call was about) stay, each value left out replaced by a marker such as `(1.0 MiB not kept on the run's record, digest 1a2b3c4d)` naming its size and four bytes of its digest, so a commit keeps its repository, branch and file paths beside the file contents it leaves out. Its answer keeps its opening, cut on a character boundary, ending in `…` and `(60 KiB of 64 KiB not kept on the run's record)`. And the call states the size of what it sent and answered (`args_bytes`, `output_bytes`) wherever the log keeps less, which is what every reader decides by: the [executors board](../reference/api-endpoints.md#get-executors) draws such a call **partly kept**, and the reviewer and the prior-work ledger read a left-out value as the marker it is, never a fragment as the whole.

**The run's submission is kept whole.** The collection replays the run's newest successful `submit_work` from the log as the round's outcome, so that one call keeps its arguments up to **64 KiB**; the moment a newer one lands, the one before it is held to the ordinary 8 KiB. A submission past **32 KiB** (as the log would hold it) is refused while the run makes it, naming its size, so the run shortens it rather than being collected as a round that said nothing; and a submission its log still did not keep whole is never replayed: the round is rescued as one with no submission, never reported with a marker as its summary.

**A long run's middle is kept as identities.** Past two hundred calls the middle is dropped, and each dropped call is tallied by its tool and the digest of its arguments, with how many calls had each and how many failed (`elided_calls`, up to 64 KiB). A resumed segment counts them as the calls they were, and every delivery check reads a reply among them as delivered; the reviewer, the board, the execute phase's record (`calls_elided`, `elided_calls`), the next round's prior-work ledger and a session a relaunch continues after its save — how many of the dropped calls came after the save, beside the tally ([What a continued session is told](#a-relaunch-continues-the-turns-session)) — are told what was dropped, by tool. Past its own share the tally gives up an identity's digest (counted as calls to its tool asking for something new), and past that its tool (counted against every later call): an over-count, which makes a repeat after the resume a fresh write, and never an under-count, which would answer a new write with an earlier one's operation. The tallies are the round's, as the log is: a new round's log starts without them, and calls a version without them dropped, with no identity, are read as calls whose tool is not known.

**Every call the run made is counted when it resumes.** A segment that resumes the run — its collection, its continuation after a person's answer, its reopening on a node that took the seat over — starts its count of the run's calls from its log: the calls it lists, by the identity each was admitted under, never by the arguments it kept; the ones it counts without listing, by their tallies; and the calls a bridged `delegate`'s workers made, which the run counted between its own and the log lists nowhere, tallied apart (`absorbed_calls`, up to 64 KiB) and read by nothing but that count, since a worker's call is no reply of the turn's. A call held for a person's approval, and the call they approved, keep neither their arguments nor a digest of them in the clear — the arguments ride the record sealed, and a digest beside the tool's name would confirm a guess at them — so each is counted as a call of its tool whose arguments are not known: an over-count, of a tool no write's operation is derived from. And a call still running elsewhere is counted too, by its entry on the log (below), so a later call that asks for something else never derives its operation id.

**A call is on the log before it runs.** The bridge writes each call's entry on the run's log ahead of it — its tool, its place among the run's calls, when the bridge took it, the digest it was admitted under (none for a tool that may ask a person first, so a held call's digest is never in the clear), the node process running it and the instant past which nobody waits for it (`in_flight`), beside the id its harness gave it — and runs the call only once the log has taken it. **Only then is the call counted in the run's call log** (the count every operation id a write derives carries, so a write that asks for something else is a new operation): a call refused at its entry is in no call log, as it is in no record, so a segment that resumes the run counts exactly what this one counted, and calls made side by side are still counted in the order they arrived, each waiting for the entries of the calls that arrived before it. The call's one update, as it finishes, takes the entry out and lists the call in its place, in the same write, so the log never holds two records of one call; the update finds its entry by the call's place, the instant the bridge took it and its tool, never by the id its harness gave it. A segment that resumes the run, on any node, counts a call still in flight by its entry's identity — as a call of its tool whose arguments are not known, where the entry carries no digest — and numbers its next call after it. **An entry the store does not take within five seconds**, four attempts included, **is a call not run**: its box is told `This call was not run: it could not be recorded. Call it again if it is still needed.`, failed, so its harness makes it again, and the engine's log names it (`bridge_call_not_recorded`); a log holding 256 calls in flight refuses the next one the same way, and a log that takes no more calls of the job tells the call its run has ended. A call whose entry was written and that is then not run — its job's session ended while the entry was written, its seat's fence closed meanwhile — has the entry withdrawn in the same step; one the call log counted that its tool then could not run at all is recorded as a call that was not run, failed, by the identity the log counted. **An update the store does not take** after four attempts over 1.75 seconds, which outlast a stream leader's re-election, leaves the entry, the call's identity on the record, and is asked for again once the call's answer is out — so its box waits for none of it — every one to five seconds, until it lands or the call's own bound passes (`mcp_bridge_call_update_owed`): a submission or a work item whose update met an outage lands as soon as the store takes it, and a collection waiting for the call ends on it. Past the call's bound its entry stays: what it answered, cost and committed is lost (`mcp_bridge_call_update_unrecorded`), every later reader of the run reading it as a call whose outcome is unknown. One the store takes after a relaunch that carried the round on is listed as a late call of the round ([A relaunch continues the turn's session](#a-relaunch-continues-the-turns-session)), and it is counted as any call is. **What it costs**: one coordination-store write before every bridged call runs, 11.89 ms at the median and 17.04 ms at the 99th percentile over a thousand writes (the update after it 13.62 ms and 18.96 ms, where an append before was 12.14 ms and 17.13 ms), on the engine's own broker of three members from a member that does not lead the runs' bucket, with the log at its full count of calls ([Gitlet](gitlet.md)); the update after it is the write the call made before. A call made beside others waits, to be counted, for the entries of the calls that arrived before it, each written at the same time as its own. And a coordination store in an outage refuses the calls it used to let run off the record, and is asked for each refused update about once every seven seconds until it is back or the call's bound passes.

**A parked state past its share is condensed, then refused.** The state grows with the turn's closed rounds — up to two hundred calls each, and as many rounds as `max_iterations` allows — so past 2 MiB the park gives up, in order, the closed rounds' successful calls' answers (nothing reads them again), then their arguments and errors, tier by tier, then their produced text past its opening, then the intent each round set out with past its opening, then the digests in their tallies of dropped calls, each tool's calls folded into one count; then the closed rounds' calls themselves, each folded into its round's tally — by identity first, so a resumed count is still exact, then by tool — which the prior-work ledger then says by tool rather than call by call, the names of the round's read-only tools, which only a listed call is drawn with, going with them; and last their reviewer's prose past its opening. Each tier cuts a payload as its round was parked, never what the tier before kept of it, so the marker a cut ends with says how much of the whole is not kept, and the park's log line (`sandbox_suspension_condensed`) counts each round and each call it cut once. Every call keeps its tool and its outcome however far it is condensed, and its identity until the last fold of its tool. A state still past its share is refused, and its park fails as `sandbox_suspension_too_large` rather than as a record the broker refuses, saying which of two things is past the share: the turn's own context — what it was asked, its trigger's words, its turn-start blocks, and how its conversation's earlier turns ended (up to 24,000 bytes as the state holds them) — which no condensing touches, or its closed rounds, condensed as far as they go, of which a turn with a high `max_iterations` can carry more than the share holds. A run's task description and label, and a colleague's question its turn answers, are a trigger's own words too, and the record keeps each to 16 KiB, as its opening with the cut said; the turn's own record keeps the whole.

**What a person, a tool or a box puts on the record is held too.** A person's reply recorded as a parked run's answer — sealed, and with every message it was made of — keeps to **512 KiB**, and so do the copies a let-go answer owes back to the seat; a reply past that is not recorded as the answer at all: its **refusal** is, as the question's outcome, with its figures and none of its words, and the run is resumed told the reply was too large to reach it, so it asks again for less or carries on ([above](#mid-run-clarification-ask_and_wait), `sandbox_answer_refused`). The bound is not lowered for it: a log or a configuration pasted whole, the answer a coding run's question typically gets, is taken whole. The refusal is recorded as `executor_answered` with the outcome `too_large`, naming what the reply weighed and what an answer may and none of its words. An answer by turn is held far under it, to a page, by `answer_executor`. A call held for approval carries its arguments sealed, up to **256 KiB** of them as the record holds them — about 190 KiB in plain text: a call past it is refused when it is made, naming its size, so the run splits or shortens it. An executor's ask to open a change keeps its title to **256 bytes**, Gitlet's own bound on a change's title, on each repository the record grants it. A person's note keeps to **16 KiB** sealed, and a question is put to at most **256** seats — a `team` question to the unit's lead and then its members in the chart's order — so in a unit larger than that, a member past the 256th who holds no other reach into the asking seat is not shown the question on the board or asked it in their list, though who may answer it is unchanged. What a box says of its session — the release of the harness that wrote it, read off a file the run's own agent can write — is held to the bounds the executor log holds it to: a release longer than 64 bytes is read as none, and a capture's stamp past any of its bounds is refused before it reaches the record.
**The completion poll is THE completion signal.** The box's waiter — the fleet's duty for an `e2b` box, the launching node's own for a `direct` or `container` one (below) — attaches to each running box every 15 s (`DefaultPollInterval`) and asks the runner whether the job finished; a run's `submit_work` nudges an immediate poll, and the 15 s tick is the fallback. Nothing from inside the box replaces it: only a poll can see a job that died before its last step, and the tick has to run anyway because it **doubles as the box keepalive** (the TTL refresh that keeps a running box from being reclaimed on its clock).

**Two waiters: the fleet's, and each node's own.** A box in the `e2b` cell can be reached from any node, so one node polls all of them: the **fleet's waiter**, a [singleton duty](seat-ownership.md#singleton-duties) (`sandbox-waiter`). A `direct` or `container` box is a process tree or a container on the host of the node that launched it — the run's record names that node, `launch_node` — and no other node can reach it: polled from another node, it reads as gone. So **every node runs a waiter of its own** for the boxes on its own host, whoever holds the duty and whatever roles it has: it polls those runs, keeps their boxes alive and reaps their pauses, and no other waiter touches them.

**A run that submits is polled at once.** When a run's `submit_work` is recorded on its row over the [tool bridge](#the-tool-bridge--a-seats-own-tools-from-inside-a-box), the node serving the run's session tells the waiter that drives it — on whichever node that is — to poll it now, and again 1, 3 and 7 s later while its harness makes its closing model call and exits; the schedule doubles its gaps and stays inside one poll interval, past which the ordinary poll is as soon. The run's completion is found within seconds of its exit instead of up to 15 s after it, and the 15 s poll stays the fallback for a run that ends any other way — one that died, was cut off at its turn's cap, or never submitted. The nudge is a hint on an ephemeral broadcast, never an instruction: the waiter it reaches reads the run's row and does what its own poll would, so a nudge that is lost costs one interval, never the run.

**No box waits on another.** Each tick of the fleet's waiter claims the waiter duty — a node's own waiter claims none, since nothing else reaches its boxes — lists the runs and hands every box that needs work — a poll, or the reclaim of a pause past its TTL — to a task of its own, at most **16 at once** across every tick in flight (`sandbox.MaxConcurrentPolls`, half the shared transport's warm connections to one host, so the launches and collections beside the poll keep the other half). The tick does not wait for its tasks: the next one comes on schedule, renewing the duty and polling every box whose last poll has finished, and passes over a box whose poll is still running, so one box is never polled by two polls at once. A box's completion is announced the moment its own poll finds it. Free slots go first to the boxes whose last poll ended longest ago, so a box that held one goes to the back of the line after its turn. Each poll — reaching the box, its keepalive and asking its runner — is bounded by the **duty claim that authorised it**: the claim's TTL less one interval, **30 s** at the default cadence, or **60 s** (the control plane's whole-request budget) where that comes first. A peer can take the duty only once it lapses, so a poll never overlaps a peer's on the same box; a node told the duty is a peer's stops its polls at once. A box that took the whole bound is not answering, and it costs that box's poll (counted against reaching it, where it was reaching it) and a slot, never anybody's tick: with N boxes held at the bound, a healthy box waits at most N/16 bounds, rounded up, for a slot — against a fifteen-minute TTL. The tick used to poll its boxes one after another, so a box whose in-box agent held a command open for its ten-minute silence bound held every box behind it, their completions unannounced and their keepalives unsent toward a fifteen-minute TTL.

Two signals cover every way a job can end:

1. **The done-marker** — the wrapper shell wrote the exit code after the agent process returned (clean finish or crash; the marker is non-empty by construction so it can't be confused with "not yet").
2. **Process liveness**: the box is asked whether the detached wrapper is still running. **A read that fails never skips this probe**: the probe still runs, a dead wrapper is still a finished job, and the read's error is reported beside a "not done". A remote or container box answers with `kill -0` on the wrapper's pid inside its own process namespace; a `direct` box answers from the job's recorded identity (the pid and its start time, see [Local sandboxes](#local-sandboxes)), because on the engine host a pid the kernel has handed to another process would otherwise read as the job and hold a dead run open. A dead wrapper with no marker means the whole process group was killed (SIGKILL / OOM); the run completes and `collect` surfaces the partial output and exit code instead of stalling forever — unless nothing saw it die: a job on the engine host whose last witness, the engine process that last saw it running, is gone too was **lost** with that process rather than ended, and is not collected as a result (below).

Both harnesses this build drives exit when their work is done, so nothing is read off the end of a stream to decide it. OpenCode 1.x did not — it finished and held its process open — and was once polled for a terminal event at its stream's end; OpenCode 2 exits when its session does, and its run's script exits after it.

**A reader never wakes a box.** The poll and the live view reach a box with `Provider.Attach`, which never resumes it: on E2B it reads the box (`GET /sandboxes/{id}`) and refuses a paused one, rather than `POST /connect`, which wakes a paused box and resets its timer; a paused container is refused too, since its liveness probe is an exec it cannot take; a `direct` box is read as it is, stopped or not, since its files and its probe are the host's. Only the collection and a follow-up run reconnect with `Connect`, which resumes. This closes a race: the poll's list of running runs is a moment old, and a collection claims a run, reads its box and pauses it — a poll reaching the box after that pause used to wake it, nothing renewed it again, and on E2B it was killed at its timer, taking the parked run's snapshot with it. A paused box sends the poll to the run's record instead. A record still running the same job in the same box whose job a collection already read back — the collection paused the box and its resume then handed the claim back, held by the node that met a model's limit or to be tried again — has the job announced again and its box left asleep, neither woken nor kept alive: the job is over, and the collection that next claims it wakes the box to read it. A poll that woke it kept a running box alive at every tick for as long as the hold lasted, up to the hour a vendor's window is sat out. Only a box under a job no collection has read back is woken, kept alive and polled, since only the poll can say whether that job finished, and no finished job is left that way for a condition of its seat's: a completion is refused for its seat's budget or a limit its models know only after it is collected.

**A local box is the node's that launched it** ([ADR-0065](../../adr/0065-a-local-box-is-driven-by-the-node-that-launched-it.md)). The seat a run belongs to can move while its job works — a rebalance, a drain — and the run's tool calls follow the seat over the bridge, but a `direct` or `container` box stays where it is, so every touch of that box is made on its host. The seat's new holder collects the run by asking the host to read its box back, over a channel the fleet signs (`sandbox.box_read`), and fits the result itself; the host makes the read only where the run's own record licenses it, and a host that does not answer — restarting — has the claim handed back uncounted (`sandbox_collect_host_silent`) and announces the completion again from its own poll once it is back. A person's stop of such a run is decided and its tool-bridge session closed at once on the seat's holder, and that node's own retry asks the host to end the job and read what it had spent, off the frame the pause reached it on — the host ends the job, as the only node that can, and only where the run's record says it is stopped: a host that does not answer is asked again rather than having the run's spend written off (`sandbox_stop_read_waits`), until it answers or has been silent for `default_timeout_seconds` since the stop — the bound after which the fleet takes a silent launch node for gone — and only then is the run ended with nothing read (`sandbox_stop_unread`). The collection of a run claimed past its turn's wall-clock cap is read on the host the same way, and the host ends a job still working past that cap — the cap as its own clock reads the row's deadline, or as the run's record says an earlier collection of the job found it — before it takes the session. A relaunch on the new holder starts in a fresh box of its own, from the pushed branch, rather than continuing in one it cannot reach. A box no run names any more — its run ended, stopped or relaunched elsewhere — is reclaimed by its host: each box records the run it was made for, every pass of the host's own waiter kills the box whose run no longer names it (`sandbox_host_box_reclaimed`), and the node that left the box there tells the host to look at once. A box whose run ended while its host was stopped is reclaimed the same way on the host's first pass. The run's [live output](#watching-a-run-live) is read on the host too, and a `from_sandbox` publish on another node is refused rather than reading a box it cannot reach.

**A node that never comes back.** If the node a local box is on leaves the fleet and does not return — its presence lease lapsed and stayed lapsed for `default_timeout_seconds` (15 min), the same bound an E2B box gets without its engine's keepalive — the fleet's waiter records on each of that node's running jobs that it was lost with the node (`sandbox_launch_node_gone`) and announces their completion, and the seat's holder carries each job's round on in a box of its own, as for any lost job (below). A run of that node parked on a question has its box released and waits for its answer without it, resuming from its pushed branch. A node that is merely restarting — a drain gives its presence up at its first step — is back inside that bound, and its own waiter picks its runs up again; the bound is measured from when the fleet's waiter first found the node away, within one stint of its holding the duty: a waiter that loses the duty, or cannot confirm its claim, forgets every absence it was timing, so an absence nobody watched whole is never counted, and a move of the duty can only make a verdict later. Raise `default_timeout_seconds` for a deployment whose nodes are routinely away longer than that.

**A vanished sandbox can't orphan a run.** If reconnecting fails on several consecutive ticks (~1 min at defaults), the waiter gives the box up and fires completion anyway. A box its backend says is **gone** — a local box root that did not outlive its node (a pod recreated with its scratch volume), a VM E2B reclaimed while the engine was down past the keepalive grace — took its job with it: the waiter records it lost (`box_gone`), and its round is carried on (below). A box that is only **unreachable** — a network partition — may still hold a working job, so the coordinator's collection asks again for its own minute of retries, which a partition that heals in that minute survives (see [Failure modes](#failure-modes)), and only then frees the agent, ends the run and announces it as `executor_failed` with reason `collect_unreachable`, rather than polling a dead box forever.

**A job lost with its host is carried on, not collected.** A job can go without ending: a `direct` box's job is a process tree of the engine host, detached into a session of its own so that it outlives the engine process — and it does, when only that process dies — but a container restart takes every process in the container, a systemd unit stopped or restarted kills every process in its control group (the default `KillMode`), and a host reboot takes a `container` box's job as well. Each kills the job mid-step, before its wrapper writes the marker that says how it ended, which is exactly what a job that crashed on its own leaves; read that way, the turn resumed on a truncated run as though the run had failed, or — once its box was gone too — the run ended the turn, which the park had already acknowledged, so nothing would ever start it again. So each local box records its job's **witness**: the engine process that last saw the job running — the one that started it, and then whichever process of the engine first finds it still running after a restart — by its pid and the start time the kernel gives it, which together do not repeat. A job that is not running ended in its witness's sight while that process is still there, and is collected as before, a crash included; a job whose witness is gone too went with it, or after it, before any engine could see it end, and is **lost** (`sandbox_run_lost`, cause `engine_restarted`). A box with no witness record is read as ended. A `container` box's runtime is asked whether the container is up before the job is (a listing it answers, absence included), so a daemon that is briefly away is no answer at all rather than a job that ended.

The waiter that drives the box records the loss on the run's row before it announces the completion — `engine_restarted`, `box_gone`, or `launch_node_gone` for a node that left (above) — and the seat's holder, claiming it, never collects it as a result. Where the box is still there it reads what the job did up to then off it, charges what it spent once and publishes it as the job's own record, noted *cut off* rather than failed; a box that does not answer is asked again like any collection, and one that has not answered for the give-up window is left behind, its spend uncharged (`sandbox_lost_run_unread`). Then the turn resumes and **carries the round on** (`sandbox_run_lost_carried_on`): the same round relaunched, its calls so far kept and counted — in the lost job's box where the relaunch runs on that box's host with the same harness, its working tree as the job left it, and in a fresh box from whatever the job pushed otherwise. It **continues the lost job's own session** where it can ([A relaunch continues the turn's session](#a-relaunch-continues-the-turns-session)) — the capture taken when the loss was found, where the engine's restart left its box behind, never the box's copy as it stands — told that it was cut off and why, every call it made through the seat's tools since the newest save point its launch held, some of which its session may already hold, and that its session may end mid-call; otherwise — a box that went with its host left no capture, and an earlier job's holds none of what this one was told — and always after a second loss in the round, it starts a fresh harness session told that a run before it was cut off, why, where to look for its work, and that what it did through the seat's tools did happen. Two exceptions: a job whose round's calls already hold its `submit_work` finished its work and was lost on its way out, so the round is collected; and a job that had asked a person before it went is parked on its question, as one that finished asking is. **A round is carried on after at most two lost jobs** (`sandbox.MaxRoundLosses`): one loss is routine — a deploy, a drained node, a patched host — and two in one round is a long run that saw a deploy and its rollback, but a third is a run taking its own host down (exhausting the memory its container is held to kills every process in the container on a cgroup-v2 Kubernetes node), and each further relaunch would take the node's other seats down with it. The next loss ends the round's executor phase, and with it the turn, saying how often the round was lost.

**Per-run OTel, without handing the sandbox a secret.** The harness's telemetry exports to an **engine-fronted OTLP receiver**, enabled for a box off its node's host by setting `CREWLET_SANDBOX_OTEL_RECEIVER_URL` to the engine API base the sandbox can reach — the public listener's address when Tier A sets `api.public.port`, since an `ingress` node serves `/otlp/` there and nowhere else. A value that is not an absolute `http://` or `https://` URL a box can use — a host a URL parser accepts (a name, or an address with no zone: an unfilled `<host>` placeholder or a mistyped IPv4 address such as `10.0.0.300` is refused), a port from 1 to 65535 if it names one, and no user information, query or fragment — refuses the node's boot, naming the variable and never repeating the value, rather than handing every box an endpoint its exporter cannot use and losing the run's telemetry without a word. Plain `http://` is accepted, and what that costs is in [Security model](#security-model). A `direct` box needs no address: on a node that forwards — `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` set, which the engine's own spans read too — and binds `api.port`, it exports to that node's own listener on loopback, by the tool bridge's rule (above), whether or not the variable is set. A node that forwards nothing hands its own boxes no endpoint of its own, since it would drop what they sent, and a box with no address that reaches it gets no telemetry endpoint at all. Each run gets a **per-run, trace-scoped, expiring token embedded in the endpoint path** (`POST /otlp/{token}/v1/{signal}`), so `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS` — the backend's ingest credential — never enters the box; the receiver validates the token and forwards upstream, adding the real auth outside the sandbox. That variable is set **empty** in the box rather than left unset, because an exporter reads it from its ambient environment otherwise — and while a box inherits nothing of the engine's own environment, its image or E2B template may export a value of its own, and so may a `-e` in `local.run_args`. The empty value is what makes the endpoint's token the only credential the run's exporter presents.

The token is **signed and self-describing** rather than a key into a store, because minting and verifying can happen on different nodes: the node running the seat mints when it launches a run, and whichever node the box exports to verifies. Every node derives its signing keys from the Tier A keyring, so a fleet agrees without sharing state. There is no per-process fallback: every node holds a keyring (a node without one does not start), and a keyring that cannot sign for the fleet is refused by name when the receiver is built, because a key of one process's own would validate at the node that minted and nowhere else. Expiry rides in the token, so nothing is reaped and a restart does not invalidate a live run's endpoint. The token also **names the key that signed it**, so a keyring rotation does not invalidate a run already in flight: the key stays on the ring until the operator drops it, and a token naming a key a node no longer holds is refused rather than retried against the active one. The runbook is in [Secret store](secret-store.md).

The receiver **accepts and drops** when no upstream is configured: the engine's own per-turn span still carries the trace, and a deployment with no backend should not have every export fail. A failing backend is never reported to the box either — an exporter that gets a 5xx retries, and retries against a backend that is down turn one outage into two. The run env also carries non-secret resource attributes (`crewlet.turn_id`, `crewlet.agent_handle`) and a `TRACEPARENT`, so Claude Code's spans/metrics nest under the turn (its `CLAUDE_CODE_*` telemetry toggles are injected only for that runner). OpenCode emits no OTLP today — its observability is the published transcript plus the engine-side lifecycle events.

**A run keeps the work item its turn is on.** The launch writes the turn's `work_item` onto the run's record and onto `executor_launched`, and a question the run parks on carries it on `executor_asked`. The record is where the resumed turn reads its item from — the event that resumes it, a box's completion or a person's chat answer, names none — so the second half of the turn is charged to the same work as the first, under the basis `resume` (see [Which work a turn is on](turn-engine.md#which-work-a-turn-is-on)). What the turn had written before it parked travels on its parked state (`execstate`, `Written`) for the same reason. **A record keeps every key it cannot read**: each status flip, claim and release rewrites the whole record, and a node running an older build writes back the keys a newer one added rather than dropping them, so a rolling upgrade cannot strip the item, or anything added after it, off a parked run.

**Dashboard.** The live-state projection keeps the set of runs in flight, and it builds that set from two sources, because neither is enough alone.

- **The lifecycle events keep the set current.** `executor_launched` adds a run. `executor_asked` moves the run to `awaiting_clarification`. `executor_finished`, `executor_failed` or `executor_stopped` removes it. A failure used to be read by nothing, so a lost run stayed on the panel as running; a stop is read the same way, so a run a person stopped leaves the panel the moment its stop is announced rather than at the next reconcile.
- **The durable run record decides which runs exist.** The record is read once when the node starts, before the node serves (before its listener is handed the API surface), and then every 30 seconds (`livestate.ReconcileInterval`). What the record holds replaces what the events built. A process that starts while runs are in flight never saw their starts, and a completion that never arrived would otherwise leave a finished run on the panel with nothing to remove it. Two cases keep the event's version. A run whose event arrived after the read began is newer than the read. A run whose end the events already reported is not put back, because the record lags the end by the collection the end starts. A completion names the job it finished, and a failure is compared with the record's last write.

This replaced a twelve-hour age-out, which was wrong in both directions. A finished run whose completion was lost stayed on the panel as running for twelve hours. A run parked on a question for longer than twelve hours disappeared from the panel, and a run waiting on a person is the one the panel most needs to show.

Each entry is `{turn_id, role, agent_handle, agent_id, harness, provider_key, model, sandbox_id, task, status, started_at, question, audience, body_withheld, work_item, owner, paused_at, launch_id, held_call}` — `provider_key` and `model` the `providers.llm` entry the launch picked and its model. `launch_id` is the job the run is on, which an answer to its question names, and `held_call` (`{id, server, tool, raw, digest, held_at}`) the tool call its question is about where it asks to make one, answered through [`POST /mcp-approvals/{id}`](../reference/api-endpoints.md#post-mcp-approvalsid) rather than `answer_executor`, so a surface reading the live set offers the answer the engine takes without reading the board first. The entry is the company's configuration, sent to an admin alone, as it is on the [`executors`](../reference/api-endpoints.md#get-executors) board; every other reader, a seat's lead included, is sent the model the run runs under.

- `body_withheld` is set for a reader who may not read the launching seat's body, which only an administrator and the seat's lead may: `task` is then empty, and so are `question` and `held_call` unless the question is put to that reader ([A status reader never receives a transcript](../reference/api-endpoints.md#a-status-reader-never-receives-a-transcript)). A reader without Worklet is sent no `work_item`.
